Wall-crossing structures on surfaces
Sergey Mozgovoy

Abstract
. Families of Bridgeland stability conditions induce families of stability data, wall-crossing structures and scattering diagrams on the motivic Hall algebra. These structures can be transferred to the quantum torus if the stability conditions of the family have global dimension at most 2. We show that geometric stability conditions on surfaces with nef anticanonical bundle have global dimension 2 and we study the resulting family of stability data. We formulate a conjecture relating this family to the family of stability data associated with a quiver with potential and we verify this conjecture for the projective plane.



1.
Introduction

Bridgeland stability conditions on triangulated categories were introduced in
[
13
]
as a mathematical incarnation of the notion of
\Pi
-stability in string theory
[
23
,
3
]
.
One can define the notion of a continuous family of stability conditions and consider the universal object in the category of all such families.
This universal object is given by the set of all stability conditions equipped with a natural topology.
It has a structure of a complex manifold
[
13
]
.

On the other hand, one can also define the notion of a stability condition on an abelian category
[
32
,
13
]
.
Under appropriate assumptions, one can define moduli spaces and moduli stacks of semistable objects of the category
[
32
,
33
,
34
,
36
,
35
,
37
]
and consider their invariants, known as Donaldson-Thomas (DT) invariants or BPS invariants.
The behavior of these invariants is controlled by a formula, called the wall-crossing formula
[
33
,
37
]
, reflecting the fact that DT invariants change their values when stability condition crosses certain codimension one subspaces of the space of all stability conditions.

The above two approaches were combined in
[
41
]
, where one constructed DT invariants associated to stability conditions on appropriate triangulated 3CY categories.
Moreover, in
[
41
]
one conceptualized the structure formed by the collection of all DT invariants associated to a given stability condition by introducing the framework of stability data on graded Lie algebras
(stability data
(Z,a)
consists of a central charge
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
, where
\Gamma
is the grading lattice, and a collection
a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
of elements of the Lie algebra).
In the case of unrefined DT invariants, the corresponding Lie algebra is the Lie algebra of a Poisson torus,
and in the case of motivic DT invariants, the corresponding Lie algebra is the Lie algebra of a quantum torus.
One can think about stability data on a graded Lie algebra
as an analogue of the notion of a stability condition on a triangulated category.
As in the case of stability conditions, one can introduce the notion of a continuous family of stability data on a graded Lie algebra
[
41
]

(also called a variation of BPS structures
[
17
,
18
]
).
Importantly, the wall-crossing formula is intrinsically embedded into the notion of continuity of a family of stability data
(the Kontsevich-Soibelman wall-crossing formula).
Given a continuous family of stability conditions on an appropriate triangulated 3CY category, one can define a continuous family of stability data on the corresponding quantum torus
[
41
]
.

There exist alternative ways to encode a continuous family
of stability data on a graded Lie algebra.
One of them is a wall-crossing structure
[
43
]
which captures information of stability data along a fixed ray in
\mathbb{C}
(usually
\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
or
-\mathbb{R}_{>0}
).
This new structure may carry less information than the original family of stability data unless the central charge rotates in the family.
In the case of a wall-crossing structure on a vector space, it can be effectively and intuitively represented by a scattering diagram
[
43
,
40
,
29
]
.

Continuous family of stability conditions
Continuous family of stability data
Wall-crossing structure
Scattering diagram

While the above hierarchy seems to be rather natural, in some situations (for example, in the case of quivers with potential) there is no need to go through all of the above steps and one can go directly from a continuous family of stability conditions (parametrized by a vector space) to a scattering diagram
[
28
,
16
,
52
]
.
The applications of this approach to the theory of cluster algebras are ubiquitous.
The theory of generalized scattering diagrams, associated to wall-crossing structures on manifolds, has yet to be developed (cf.
[
12
]
).

In this paper we will give a detailed introduction to stability conditions, stability data and relationship between them.
We will develop some tools to treat both of the above notions in a unified manner.
For example, it was observed in
[
6
,
4
]
that the support property with respect to a quadratic form can be extended from a stability condition to its neighborhood.
The same property for stability data was made one of the axioms in the original definition of a continuous family of stability data
[
41
, §2.3]
.
We will show that this axiom follows automatically from a weaker support property.
In doing so, we introduce the notion of a continuous family of stable supports and prove that the support property for such family with respect to a fixed quadratic form is an open condition.
The corresponding statements for stability conditions and stability data then follow immediately.

It was mentioned above that one can associate stability data with a stability condition on a triangulated 3CY category
[
41
]
.
On the other hand, one can associate stability data with a stability condition on an abelian category of homological dimension one
[
33
]
.
The reason for these restrictions on a category is that the precursor of the wall-crossing formula, the Harder-Narasimhan identity in the Hall algebra of the corresponding triangulated or abelian category, can not be transferred to the quantum torus in general.
In this paper we will show that this still can be done if a stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
has global dimension
\leq 2
.
The global dimension is a real invariant of a slicing
\mathcal{P}

[
31
,
58
]
which measures the distance between interacting slices §
3.8
.
We will show that stability data associated with such a stability condition satisfies the wall-crossing formula
(Corollary
5.7
).
We will also show that a continuous family of such stability conditions induces a continuous family of stability data (Theorem
5.9
).
Morally, these results are similar to
[
36
, §6.4]
, where one proves a wall-crossing formula for Gieseker-semistable sheaves on a surface having a nef anticanonical bundle.

Our next goal is to find meaningful examples in which the above results can be applied.
Given a smooth projective surface
X
, we say that a stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})

on
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
is special geometric if the sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{x}
, for
x\in X
, are
\sigma
-stable of the same phase and if the discriminant (
157
) is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
.
For any pair of real divisors
\beta,\omega
with ample
\omega
, one can define some special geometric stability condition
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}

[
2
,
14
]
.
We will prove in Theorem
6.10
that every special geometric stability condition is equal to some
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
up to the action of
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
on the space of stability conditions
(cf.
[
14
]
).
The space of special geometric stability conditions is an open subset of the space of all stability conditions.

We will prove that if
X
is a surface having a nef anticanonical divisor, then the global dimensional of any special geometric stability condition is equal to
2
(Theorem
7.7
).
In the case of
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
this result was proved in
[
25
]
based on the method developed in
[
46
]
.
This method, for a stability condition
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
, relies on the fact that
K_{X}
is proportional to
\omega
and it doesn’t seem to generalize to other surfaces.
In our approach we develop a method to control the behavior of mini/max phases of objects under deformations of stability conditions (Theorem
3.12
) and we show that this method can be applied in our situation by proving a new Bogomolov-type inequality for
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects (Theorem
6.16
).
In Theorem
7.2
we prove that given two semistable objects
E,\,F
with
\phi(E)<\phi(F)
and a nef line bundle
L
, we have

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0.

This statement is trivial for Gieseker-semistable sheaves, but it becomes more involved in the case of
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects as the object
F\otimes L
is not necessarily
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable.

The above results imply that for any smooth projective surface
X
with a nef anticanonical bundle we have a continuous family of stability data parametrized by the space
\operatorname{Stab}^{+}(\mathcal{D})
of special geometric stability conditions.
Stability conditions contained in the same
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
-orbit produce equivalent stability data, hence it is enough to consider the family of stability data parametrized by pairs of divisors
(\beta,\omega)
with ample
\omega
.
In the case of
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
, one defined in
[
12
]
the corresponding scattering diagram by using the interpretation of DT invariants through intersection cohomology (see §
7.3
).
This approach relies on the equation
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
for semistable objects
E
, hence one needs to restrict the possible phases (the equation is not true, for example, for
E\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}_{x}
).
As we mentioned earlier, the wall-crossing structure (and the corresponding scattering diagram) associated to a continuous family of stability data does not capture in general the whole information of the family as it only preserves stability data along one ray, for every point of the family.
On the other hand, this scattering diagram captures a significant part of the family of stability data and it is fully described in
[
12
]
.

Another topic that we address in this paper is the relationship of the above stability data for surfaces and stability data for quivers with potentials.
There exist many examples (see e.g.
[
8
]
)
of smooth projective surfaces
X
with a nef anticanonical bundle such that
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
admits a tilting objects
T^{\prime}
that can be lifted to a tilting object
T
on the canonical bundle
Y\mathchar 61\relax\omega_{X}
considered as a non-compact 3CY variety (see §
8.1
).
In these examples, the algebra
\operatorname{End}(T)^{\mathrm{op}}
can be interpreted as the Jacobian algebra
J_{W}
of some quiver with potential
(Q,W)
,
and the algebra
\operatorname{End}(T^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}
can be interpreted as the partial Jacobian algebra
J_{W,I}
for a certain cut
I\subset Q_{1}
(see §
8.4
).
By the tilting theory we have

(2)

D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}),\qquad D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W}),

where
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
denotes the derived category of sheaves with compact support.
The first equivalence implies that stability conditions and stability data that we constructed earlier, can as well be associated with the algebra
J_{W,I}
.

For a del Pezzo surface, under appropriate assumptions on stability conditions on
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
and the phases of semistable objects, one can expect that these semistable objects are push-forwards of semistable objects in
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
Then one can expect that some components of stability data for
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
coincide with components of stability data for
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
This is not what we are going to do.

Given a quiver with potential
(Q,W)
and a stability function
Z\colon\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
, one can define the corresponding stability data
A_{Z}^{Q,W}
in the quantum torus with coefficients in the (localized) Grothendieck ring of varieties with exponentials §
8.4
.
One could invoke instead monodromic mixed Hodge structures
[
42
]
, but this would be unnecessary.
In the presence of a cut, the coefficients are contained in the usual (localized) Grothendieck ring of varieties.
Morally, stability data
A_{Z}^{Q,W}
should correspond to some stability condition on the derived 3CY category of DG modules over the Ginzburg DG algebra associated to
(Q,W)
.
The above stability data enjoys the wall-crossing formula (Theorem
8.5
) and the family of such stability data (parametrized by stability functions
Z\colon\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
) is continuous.

Let us assume now that
(Q,W)
and a cut
I\subset Q_{1}
arise from the tilting theory on a surface
X
with a nef anticanonical bundle, as we discussed earlier.
Then we have a family of stability data for
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
(parametrized by special geometric stability conditions) and a family of stability data for
(Q,W)
(parametrized by stability functions
Z\colon\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
).
The quantum tori, where these families live, turn out to be the same.
We conjecture that the above families are compatible, meaning that there exists a special geometric stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
such that its heart is equal to
\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
(so that we have a stability function
Z\colon\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
) and such that stability data
A_{\sigma}
corresponding to
\sigma
is equal to
A_{Z}^{Q,W}
.
This conjecture looks somewhat surprising as it gives a strong compatibility of DT invariants on a surface and on its canonical bundle.
We verify this conjecture for
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
in §
8.6
.
In other examples from
[
8
]
we checked that, as long as there exists a special geometric stability condition with the heart equal to
\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
, the conjecture is true (see Remark
8.7
).
This is a manageable problem which will be addressed elsewhere.
The above compatibility means that it is enough to know stability data
A_{Z}^{Q,W}
for some
Z
in order to determine stability data
A_{\sigma}
for all special geometric stability conditions
\sigma
.
A complete (conjectural) description of stability data
A_{Z}^{Q,W}
exists for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and other surfaces

[
8
,
55
]
.
It is fully verified for small dimension vectors.

Plan of the paper

In §
2.1
and §
2.2
we introduce strict cones and strict semigroups and study their properties.
This will be needed later in the definition of stability data and wall-crossing formulas.
In §
2.3
we introduce families of strict semigroups and study the behavior of their lower sets under deformations.
In §
2.4
we study the relationship between strict cones and quadratic forms.
In §
2.5
we introduce the notion of a continuous family of stable supports and prove a result about the behavior of such families with respect to quadratic forms.
This result has an important applications in the case of stability conditions (Theorem
3.10
) and in the case of stability data (Theorem
4.10
).

In §
3
we give a detailed introduction to Bridgeland stability conditions.
In particular, in Theorem
3.10
we give a proof of the fact that the support property with respect to a fixed quadratic form is an open condition
and in §
3.7
we introduce a method to control the behavior of mini/max phases under deformations.

In §
4
we give a detailed introduction to stability data.
In particular, in §
4.5
we introduce continuous families of stability data with a weakened support axiom and we prove in Theorem
4.10
that our definition is equivalent to the original definition of
[
41
, §2.3]
.
In §
4.6
we introduce wall-crossing structures and discuss their relation to continuous families of stability data.

In §
5
we discuss various wall-crossing formulas.
In §
5.1
we introduce Grothendieck rings of algebraic varieties and stacks
and in §
5.2
we introduce motivic Hall algebras of exact categories.
In §
5.3
we prove the usual wall-crossing formula in the motivic Hall algebra for an appropriate stability condition on a triangulated category.
In §
5.4
we prove the wall-crossing formula in the quantum torus under the assumption that our stability condition has global dimension
\leq 2
.
In §
5.5
and §
5.6
we construct continuous families of stability data associated with continuous families of stability conditions having global dimension
\leq 2
.

In §
6
we study geometric stability conditions on surfaces.
In particular, in §
6.4
we show that every
special geometric stability condition is equal to some
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
up to the action of
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
on the space of stability conditions.
In §
6.6
we discuss the relationship of the large volume limit with Gieseker and twisted stability.
In §
6.7
we prove a new Bogomolov-type inequality.
In §
6.8
we discuss alternative ways to parametrize geometric stability conditions.

In §
7
we study stability data associated to special geometric stability conditions.
In §
7.1
we prove various vanishing results for semistable objects with respect to geometric stability conditions and we prove that special geometric stability conditions on surface with a nef anticanonical bundle have global dimension
2
.
In §
7.2
we construct a continuous family of geometric stability data.
In §
7.3
we discuss a relation of geometric stability data to intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of semistable objects.

In §
8
we discuss a relation of geometric stability data to stability data associated to quivers with potentials.
In §
8.1
we briefly discuss basic tilting theory for smooth projective algebraic varieties and their canonical bundles.
In §
8.3
we illustrate this approach on the example of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
In §
8.4
we introduce quivers with potentials, their Jacobian algebras, partial Jacobian algebras, and their motivic invariants (stability data).
In §
8.5
and §
8.6
we prove that the family of geometric stability data on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and the family of stability data associated to the corresponding quiver with potential are compatible.

2.
Cones and semigroups

Given an abelian group
M
and a commutative ring
R
, we define
M_{R}\mathchar 61\relax M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
.
Given a module
M
over a commutative ring
R
, we define
M^{\vee}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)
.

2.1.
Cones

Let
E
be a real vector space of dimension
n>0
.
We equip it with a norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
.
For any subset
S\subset E
, let
\operatorname{conv}(S)
denote its

convex hull
.
By Carathéodory’s theorem, every element of
\operatorname{conv}(S)
can be written as a convex combination of
n+1
points in
S
.
This implies that if
S\subset E
is compact, then
\operatorname{conv}(S)
is also compact.

A subset
C\subset E
is called a
cone
if
\mathbb{R}_{>0}C\subset C
and it is called a
convex cone
if also
C+C\subset C
.
We will say that a cone
C
is
blunt
if
0\notin C
.
We define a
ray
to be a blunt cone of dimension
1
.
For any subset
S\subset E
, we define the

conical hull

\operatorname{cone}(S)
of
S

(or the convex cone generated by
S
)
to be the minimal convex cone that contains
S
.
Explicitly,

(3)

\operatorname{cone}(S)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{conv}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}S)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\sum_{i\mathchar 61\relax 1}^{k}a_{i}x_{i}\ \right|a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,x_{i}\in S,\,k\geq 1\right\}.

Note that traditionally one also includes
0
in
\operatorname{cone}(S)
.
We will say that a convex cone
C\subset E
is
strict
if its closure
\bar{C}
does not contain a line
or, equivalently, if
\bar{C}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
is convex.

Lemma 2.1
.

A convex cone
C\subset E
is strict if and only if there exists
u\in E^{\vee}
such that

(4)

C\subset C_{u}\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|u(x)\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right\}.

Proof.

It is clear that
C_{u}
is a strict closed cone.
Conversely, let
C
be a strict cone.
We can assume that it is closed.
Then the set
C_{1}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x\in C\ \right|\left\lVert x\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax 1\right\}\subset C\backslash\left\{0\right\}
is compact,
hence the convex hull
\operatorname{conv}(C_{1})\subset C\backslash\left\{0\right\}
is also compact.
By the Hahn-Banach separation theorem, there exists
u\in E^{\vee}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
u(x)>\varepsilon
for all
x\in\operatorname{conv}(C_{1})
.
This implies that
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}u(x)\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert
for all
x\in C
.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
Z\colon E\to F
be a linear map between two finite-dimensional normed vector spaces.
For any strict cone
V\subset F
and
\varepsilon>0
,
consider the convex cone

(5)

C(V,Z,\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cone}\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|Z(x)\in V,\,\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right\}.

Then
C(V,Z,\varepsilon)
is strict and there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
(independent of
Z
) such that

(6)

\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert\qquad\forall x\in C(V,Z,\varepsilon).

Proof.

There exists
0\neq u\in F^{\vee}
such that

V\subset C_{u}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.y\in F\ \right|u(y)\geq\left\lVert y\right\rVert\right\}
.
If
Z(x)\in V
and
\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert
,
then
uZ(x)\geq\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert
.
This implies that
C(V,Z,\varepsilon)
is contained in the strict cone
C^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|uZ(x)\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right\}
.
Let
\left\lVert u\right\rVert>0
be the operator norm of the bounded operator
u\colon F\to\mathbb{R}
.
For any
x\in C^{\prime}
, we have

\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\left\lvert uZ(x)\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert

and we choose
\varepsilon^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\varepsilon\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{-1}
.
∎

Note that for a ray
\ell\subset F
, we have

(7)

C(\ell,Z,\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|Z(x)\in\ell,\,\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right\}

as the right hand side is automatically convex.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
C\subset E
be a strict cone.
Then there exists
c>0
such that

\sum\left\lVert x_{i}\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\sum x_{i}\right\rVert\qquad\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in C.

Proof.

Let
u\in E^{\vee}
be such that
C\subset C_{u}
.
For any
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in C
, we have

\sum\left\lVert x_{i}\right\rVert\leq\sum u(x_{i})\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert\sum x_{i}\right\rVert,

where
\left\lVert u\right\rVert
is the operator norm of
u\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Example 2.4
.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{C}
be a strict cone of angle
\theta<\tfrac{\pi}{2}
.
Then

\sum\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\cos(\theta)}\left\lvert\sum x_{i}\right\rvert\qquad\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in V.

Note also that
\left\lvert x_{j}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\sum_{i}x_{i}\right\rvert
for any
1\leq j\leq k
.

2.2.
Strict semigroups

Let
S
be a commutative semigroup, written additively.
We define an
ideal
of
S
to be a subset
I\subset S
such that
I+S\subset I
.
For any subset
X\subset S
, we define the semigroup generated by
X
to be

(8)

\operatorname{sgr}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.a_{1}+\dots+a_{k}\ \right|a_{i}\in X,\,k\geq 1\right\}\subset S.

If
S
has
cancellation property
, meaning that
a+b\mathchar 61\relax a+c
implies
b\mathchar 61\relax c
,
then we can embed
S
into an abelian group generated by
S
(the Grothendieck group of
S
).
In what follows we will always assume that
S
has cancellation property and satisfies
S\cap(-S)\subset\left\{0\right\}
.
In this case we define the partial order on
S

(9)

a\leq b\iff b-a\in S\cup\left\{0\right\}.

A subset
I\subset S
is an ideal if and only if
it is an
upper set
of the poset
S
, meaning that
a\leq b
and
a\in I
imply
b\in I
.
Similarly, a subset
I\subset S
is the complement of an ideal if and only if it is a
lower set
of the poset
S
, meaning that
a\leq b
and
b\in I
imply
a\in I
.

Let
\Gamma
be a free abelian group of finite rank and
S\subset\Gamma
be a semigroup.
We will say that
S
is
strict
if it generates a strict convex cone in
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
S
has automatically the cancellation property and satisfies
S\cap(-S)\subset\left\{0\right\}
.
Recall that a map between topological spaces is called
proper
if the preimage of every compact set is compact.
In particular, a map
u\colon S\to\mathbb{R}
(where
S
has discrete topology) is proper if and only if the preimage of every bounded set in
\mathbb{R}
is finite.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
S\subset\Gamma
be a strict semigroup without zero. Then

(1)

There exists an additive proper map
u\colon S\to\mathbb{R}

such that
u(S)\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(2)

For any
a\in S
, the lower set
\left\downarrow a\right.\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.b\in S\ \right|b\leq a\right\}
is finite.

(3)

For any
a\in S
, the set

\left\{\left.(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in S^{k}\ \right|\sum a_{i}\mathchar 61\relax a,\,k\geq 1\right\}

is finite.

Proof.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be a norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
.
By Lemma
2.1
, there exists a linear map
u\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}

such that
u(a)\geq\left\lVert a\right\rVert
for all
a\in S
.
This map satisfies the required properties.
The other statements of the lemma follow immediately.
∎

Note that if
u\colon S\to\mathbb{R}
is an additive proper map such that
u(S)\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then the subset

(10)

I_{N}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.a\in S\ \right|u(a)\leq N\right\},\qquad N\geq 1,

is a finite lower set.
Moreover, every finite lower subset of
S
is contained in
I_{N}
for some
N\geq 1
.

2.3.
Families of strict semigroups

Let as before
\Gamma
be a free abelian group of finite rank and let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be a norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
.
For any strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
,
Z\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
and
\varepsilon>0
, we define the semigroup

(11)

S(V,Z,\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{sgr}\left\{\left.a\in\Gamma\ \right|Z(a)\in V,\,\left\lvert Z(a)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert\right\}\subset\Gamma

which is strict by Lemma
2.2
.
Note that for a ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
, we have

(12)

S(\ell,Z,\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.a\in\Gamma\ \right|Z(a)\in\ell,\,\left\lvert Z(a)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert\right\}.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
M
be a topological space,
Z\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
be a continuous map and
x\in M
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and
V\subset\mathbb{C}
be a strict cone such that

Z_{x}(\Gamma)\cap\partial V\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0\right\},\qquad\left\lvert Z_{x}(a)\right\rvert\neq\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert\quad\forall 0\neq a\in\Gamma.

Then, for any finite set
D\subset\Gamma
, there exists an open set
x\in U\subset M
such that

D\cap S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax D\cap S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\qquad\forall y\in U.

Proof.

It is enough to assume that
D\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\gamma\right\}
.
Let us define

S^{\circ}(V,Z,\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.a\in\Gamma\ \right|Z(a)\in V,\,\left\lvert Z(a)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert\right\}

so that
S(V,Z,\varepsilon)
is the semigroup generated by
S^{\circ}(V,Z,\varepsilon)
.
It follows from our assumptions that for any finite set
D^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
, there exists an open set
x\in U\subset M
such that

D^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ}(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax D^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ}(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\qquad\forall y\in U.

If
\gamma\in S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
, then
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}a_{i}
with
a_{i}\in S^{\circ}(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
.
By the previous remark, there exists an open set
x\in U\subset M

such that
\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i}\subset S^{\circ}(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
for all
y\in U
.
This implies that
\gamma\in S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
for all
y\in U
,
proving one of the required inclusions.

Let us prove the other inclusion.
There exists an open set
x\in U\subset M
such that

\left\lVert Z_{x}-Z_{y}\right\rVert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon
for all
y\in U
.
If
\left\lvert Z_{y}(a)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert
, then

\left\lvert Z_{x}(a)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert
.
Therefore

S^{\circ}(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\subset S^{\circ}(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon).

By Lemma
2.3
, there exists
c>0
such that
\sum\left\lVert a_{i}\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\sum a_{i}\right\rVert
for all
a_{i}\in S(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon)
.
Let us consider the finite set
D^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.a\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lVert a\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}
.
Then we can shrink
U
so that

D^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ}(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax D^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ}(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
for all
y\in U
.

If
\gamma\in S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
for some
y\in U
, then
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\sum a_{i}
with
a_{i}\in S^{\circ}(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon)
, hence
\left\lVert a_{i}\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\sum a_{i}\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax c\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
and
a_{i}\in D^{\prime}
.
This implies that
a_{i}\in S^{\circ}(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
, hence
\gamma\in S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
M
be a topological space,
Z\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
be a continuous map and
x\in M
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and
V\subset\mathbb{C}
be a strict cone such that

Z_{x}(\Gamma)\cap\partial V\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0\right\},\qquad\left\lvert Z_{x}(a)\right\rvert\neq\varepsilon\left\lVert a\right\rVert\quad\forall 0\neq a\in\Gamma.

Then, for any finite lower set
I\subset S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
,
there exists an open set
x\in U\subset M
such that

I\subset S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
is a lower set for all
y\in U
.

Proof.

By the previous result, we can assume that
I\subset S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
for all
y\in U
.
Using the same argument as before, we can assume that

S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon)\qquad\forall y\in U

and
c>0
is such that
\sum\left\lVert a_{i}\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\sum a_{i}\right\rVert
for all
a_{i}\in S(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon)
.
Let us consider the finite set

D\mathchar 61\relax\bigcup_{\gamma\in I}\left\{\left.a\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lVert a\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}.

By the previous result, we can assume that

D\cap S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax D\cap S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
for all
y\in U
.

We need to show that for all
y\in U
the subset
I\subset S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
is a lower set, meaning that if
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax a_{1}+a_{2}\in I
and
a_{i}\in S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
,
then
a_{i}\in I
.
We have
a_{i}\in S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z_{x},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon)
, hence
\left\lVert a_{i}\right\rVert\leq c\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
and

a_{i}\in D\cap S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax D\cap S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
.
As
I\subset S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
is a lower set, we conclude that
a_{i}\in I
.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

We don’t know if a similar statement is true if one uses semigroups
C(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)\cap\Gamma
instead of
S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
.

2.4.
Cones and quadratic forms

Lemma 2.9
.

[cf.
[
41
,
6
]
]
Let
Z\colon E\to F
be a linear map between finite-dimensional real vector spaces,
Q\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form, negative semi-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
, and
\ell\subset F
be a ray.
Then the cone

(13)

C(\ell,Z,Q)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|Z(x)\in\ell,\,Q(x)\geq 0\right\}

is convex and
Q(x+y)\geq Q(x)+Q(y)
for all
x,y\in C(\ell,Z,Q)
.
This cone is strict if
Q
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
Q(x+y)\geq Q(x)+Q(y)

for all
x,y\in C(\ell,Z,Q)
.
There exists
\lambda>0
such that
y-\lambda x\in\operatorname{Ker}Z
.
Let
q
be the symmetric bilinear form corresponding to
Q
.
Then

2\lambda q(x,y)\mathchar 61\relax\lambda^{2}Q(x)+Q(y)-Q(y-\lambda x)\geq 0
.
Therefore
q(x,y)\geq 0
, hence
Q(x+y)\geq Q(x)+Q(y)
as required.
The last statement can be proved directly or by applying Lemma
2.11
.
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

Let
Z\colon E\to F
be a linear map between finite-dimensional real vector spaces,
Q\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form, negative semi-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
, and
\ell\subset F
be a ray.
Then the cone

(14)

C^{+}(\ell,Z,Q)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|Z(x)\in\ell,\,Q(x)>0\right\}

is convex.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
Z\colon E\to F
be a linear map between finite dimensional real vector spaces,
Q\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form, negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
, and
V\subset F
be a strict cone.
Then the convex cone

(15)

C(V,Z,Q)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cone}\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|Z(x)\in V,\,Q(x)\geq 0\right\}

is strict.

Proof.

Let
K\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Ker}Z
and
K^{\perp}\subset E
be its orthogonal complement with respect to
Q
.
Then
E\mathchar 61\relax K\oplus K^{\perp}
and
Z\colon K^{\perp}\hookrightarrow F
is injective.
Let
F
be equipped with an inner product and the induced norm.
There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varepsilon Q(y)<\left\lVert Z(y)\right\rVert^{2}
for all
0\neq y\in K^{\perp}
.
We define the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
on
E
by

\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}\mathchar 61\relax\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert^{2}-\varepsilon Q(x).

It is indeed a norm:
for any
x\in K
and
y\in K^{\perp}
we have

\left\lVert x+y\right\rVert^{2}\mathchar 61\relax\left\lVert Z(y)\right\rVert^{2}-\varepsilon Q(x)-\varepsilon Q(y)\geq-\varepsilon Q(x)\geq 0

and the equality is only possible for
x\mathchar 61\relax y\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
For
x\in E
, we have
Q(x)\geq 0
if and only if
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert
.
As
V
is strict, there exists
u\in F^{\vee}
such that
V\subset C_{u}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.y\in F\ \right|u(y)\geq\left\lVert y\right\rVert\right\}
.
If
Z(x)\in V
and
Q(x)\geq 0
, then

uZ(x)\geq\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert
.
Therefore
C(V,Z,Q)
is contained in the strict cone

\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|uZ(x)\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right\}
.
∎

Given a linear map
Z\colon E\to F
, we will say that a subset
S\subset E
satisfies the
support property
(with respect to
Z
) if there exist norms on
E
and
F
such that

(16)

S\subset\left\{\left.x\in E\ \right|\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right\}.

Remark 2.12
.

The support property is equivalent to the condition that
the following function on
\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)
defines a norm (cf. Lemma
3.4
)

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{Z,S}\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\sup_{x\in S\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\frac{\left\lVert u(x)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert},\qquad u\in\operatorname{Hom}(E,F).

Lemma 2.13
.

A subset
S\subset E
satisfies the support property if and only if there exists a quadratic form
Q
on
E
such that

(1)

Q
is negative definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
.

(2)

For any
x\in S
, we have
Q(x)\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let us assume that
S
satisfies the support property.
We can assume that the norms on
E
and
F
are induced by inner products.
Then we define the quadratic form

Q(x)\mathchar 61\relax\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}

which satisfies the required properties.

Conversely, let us assume that
Q
satisfies the required properties.
Let
K\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Ker}Z
and
K^{\perp}\subset E
be its orthogonal complement with respect to
Q
.
Then
E\mathchar 61\relax K\oplus K^{\perp}
and

Z\colon K^{\perp}\hookrightarrow F
is injective.
Let
F
be equipped with an inner product and the induced norm.
There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varepsilon Q(y)<\left\lVert Z(y)\right\rVert^{2}
for all
0\neq y\in K^{\perp}
.
We define the norm on
E
by
(cf. Lemma
2.11
)

\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}\mathchar 61\relax\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert^{2}-\varepsilon Q(x).

For
x\in S
, we have
Q(x)\geq 0
, hence

\left\lVert Z(x)\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert
.
∎

2.5.
Families of stable supports

Let
\Gamma
be a free abelian group of finite rank and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be a norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We define a
stable support
to be a triple
(Z,\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{s})
,
where
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
is a linear map
and
\mathfrak{r}\subset\mathfrak{s}\subset\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}
are subsets
satisfying for some
\varepsilon>0
(cf. (
16
))

(17)

\mathfrak{s}\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}

and such that for every ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
, we have

(18)

\mathfrak{s}\cap Z^{-1}(\ell)\subset\operatorname{sgr}(\mathfrak{r}\cap Z^{-1}(\ell)).

Intuitively, one can think about
\mathfrak{r}
as the set of classes of stable objects and about
\mathfrak{s}

as the set of classes of semistable objects.

Let
M
be a topological space.
We define a
continuous family of stable supports
on
M

to be a collection of stable supports
(Z_{x},\mathfrak{r}_{x},\mathfrak{s}_{x})_{x\in M}
such that
Z\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
is continuous and

(1)

For any
x\in M
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
and an open set
x\in U\subset M
such that

\mathfrak{s}_{y}\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}\qquad\forall y\in U.

(2)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the set
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\gamma\in\mathfrak{r}_{x}\right\}
is open.

(3)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the set
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{x}\right\}
is closed.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
M
be path-connected and
(Z,\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{s})
be a continuous family of stable supports on
M
.
Let
Q\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form that is negative semi-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}
for all
x\in M

and such that there exists
x\in M
satisfying
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{x}
.
Then we have
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}
and
y\in M
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.9
it is enough to show that
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{r}_{y}
and
y\in M
.
We can assume that
M\mathchar 61\relax[0,1]
and that
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
.
As
M
is compact, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\mathfrak{s}_{t}\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}

for all
t\in M
.
Let
0<\theta<\pi/4
.
By dividing
[0,1]
into a finite union of intervals,
we can assume that

\left\lVert Z_{t}-Z_{1}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sin(\theta)

for all
t\in[0,1]
.
For any
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{t}
, we have

\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)-Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)\right\rvert
, hence

\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert.

On the other hand, if
\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}
satisfies

\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
, then

\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)-Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\sin(\theta)\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\leq\sin(\theta)\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert,

hence the angle between
Z_{t}(\gamma)
and
Z_{1}(\gamma)
is
\leq\theta
.
If
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}
with
Z_{t}(\gamma_{i})
contained in the same ray and
\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma_{i})\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma_{i}\right\rVert
,
then the angle between
Z_{t}(\gamma_{i})
and
Z_{1}(\gamma_{i})
is
\leq\theta
,
hence the angle between
Z_{1}(\gamma_{1})
and
Z_{1}(\gamma_{2})
is
\leq 2\theta<\pi/2
.
This implies that

\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma_{i}\right\rVert\leq\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma_{i})\right\rvert<\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert,\qquad i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2.

For a fixed
\gamma\in\mathfrak{r}_{0}
, we can assume by induction that the result is true for all elements of the finite set

D\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma_{1}\in\bigcup_{t}\mathfrak{s}_{t}\ \right|\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma_{1}\right\rVert\leq\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma_{1})\right\rvert<\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\right\}.

Let us choose maximal
t>0
such that
\gamma\in\mathfrak{r}_{t^{\prime}}
for all
t^{\prime}\in[0,t)
.
Then
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{t}
.
If
t\mathchar 61\relax 1
, then we are done.
Otherwise, we have
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{t}\backslash\mathfrak{r}_{t}
, hence we can decompose
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i\mathchar 61\relax 1}^{n}\gamma_{i}
, where
\gamma_{i}\in\mathfrak{r}_{t}\cap Z^{-1}(\ell)
,
\ell\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}Z_{t}(\gamma)
and
n\geq 2
.
By the above discussion, we have
\gamma_{i}\in D
, hence
Q(\gamma_{i})\geq 0
by inductive assumption.
By Lemma
2.9
we conclude that
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
.
∎

Corollary 2.15
.

Let
M
be locally path-connected and
(Z,\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{s})
be a continuous family of stable supports on
M
.
Let
Q\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form and
x\in M
be such that
Q
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}

and
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{x}
.
Then we have
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}
and
y
in some neighborhood of
x
.

Proof.

There exists an open subset
x\in U\subset M
such that
Q_{x}
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{y}
for all
y\in U
.
We can assume that
U
is path-connected.
Now we apply the previous result.
∎

3.
Stability conditions

Given a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
and a family of subcategories (or objects)
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{D}
, we denote by
\left\langle\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathchar 58\relax i\in I\right\rangle
the minimal full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
that contains all
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and is closed under extensions.
For two subcategories
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{D}
, we will write
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})\mathchar 61\relax 0
if
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)\mathchar 61\relax 0
for all
X\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
Y\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Given two objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{D}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we will sometimes denote
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y[n])
by
\operatorname{Hom}^{n}(X,Y)
.
For any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{D}
, we will write
\operatorname{cone}(f)
for the third object (unique up to an isomorphism) of the corresponding distinguished triangle.
Sometimes we will use the word triangle for a distinguished triangle.

3.1.
t-structures

A
t-structure
on a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
is a pair
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
of full subcategories in
\mathcal{D}
such that, using

\mathcal{D}^{\leq n}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}[-n]
and

\mathcal{D}^{\geq n}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}[-n]
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1})\mathchar 61\relax 0
.

(2)

\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\leq 1}
and

\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}\supset\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1}
.

(3)

For any
X\in\mathcal{D}
, there exists a distinguished triangle
X^{\prime}\to X\to X^{\prime\prime}\to
with
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}
and
X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1}
.

We have
[
9
]

(19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1}\mathchar 61\relax(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0})^{\perp}\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.Y\in\mathcal{D}\ \right|\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},Y)\mathchar 61\relax 0\right\},

(20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\mathchar 61\relax{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1})\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.X\in\mathcal{D}\ \right|\operatorname{Hom}(X,\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1})\mathchar 61\relax 0\right\},

hence a t-structure is uniquely determined by the subcategory
\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}
.
The category
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}
is abelian
and is called the
heart
of the t-structure
[
9
]
.
A t-structure is called
bounded
if every object in
\mathcal{D}
is contained in
\mathcal{D}^{\leq n}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\geq-n}
for some
n\geq 0
.
In this case the t-structure is uniquely determined by its heart, namely,

(21)

\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\mathcal{A}[n]\mathchar 58\relax n\geq 0\right\rangle,\qquad\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\mathcal{A}[n]\mathchar 58\relax n\leq 0\right\rangle.

3.2.
Slicings

We define a slicing
\mathcal{P}
of a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
to be
a collection
(\mathcal{P}_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathbb{R}}
of full subcategories in
\mathcal{D}
such that

(1)

\mathcal{P}_{\phi+1}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi}[1]
for all
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi},\mathcal{P}_{\phi^{\prime}})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for all
\phi>\phi^{\prime}
.

(3)

For any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
, there exists a sequence of maps

0\mathchar 61\relax E_{0}\to E_{1}\to\dots\to E_{n}\mathchar 61\relax E

such that
\operatorname{cone}(E_{k-1}\to E_{k})\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi_{k}}
for some
\phi_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\phi_{1}>\dots>\phi_{n}
.

We will call the above sequence the Harder-Narasimhan (HN) filtration of
E
and we will address axiom (3) as the HN property.
The HN filtration of
E
is uniquely determined up to isomorphism.
We define
\phi^{+}_{\mathcal{P}}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{1}
and
\phi^{-}_{\mathcal{P}}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{n}
.
For any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, let

(22)

\mathcal{P}_{I}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{\phi}\mathchar 58\relax\phi\in I\right\rangle\subset\mathcal{D}.

Note that traditionally one denotes
\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
by
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{P}_{I}
by
\mathcal{P}(I)
.
The categories

(23)

\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{>0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{(0,+\infty)},\qquad\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\leq 1}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{(-\infty,1]}

form a bounded t-structure with the heart
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{(0,1]}
, called the
heart
of
\mathcal{P}

[
13
]
.
The categories
\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
are also abelian
[
13
, Lemma 5.2]
.
For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we define a new slicing
\mathcal{P}[a]

(24)

\mathcal{P}[a]_{\phi}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi+a},\qquad\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

Its heart is equal to
\mathcal{P}_{(a,a+1]}
.
Note that
\mathcal{P}[n]_{\phi}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi+n}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi}[n]
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Example 3.1
.

Given a full subcategory
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{D}
,
let us define
\mathcal{P}_{n}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}[n-1]
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
n\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Z}
.
Then
\mathcal{A}
is the heart of a bounded
t
-structure if and only if
\mathcal{P}\mathchar 61\relax(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{R}}
is a slicing (cf.
[
13
, Lemma 3.2]
).
The heart of
\mathcal{P}
is equal to
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}
.

3.3.
Bridgeland stability conditions

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category,

\Gamma
be a free abelian group of finite rank and
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{D})\to\Gamma
be a linear map.
We define a
pre-stability condition
on the triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
(with respect to
\operatorname{cl}
) to be a pair
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
, where

(1)

Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
is a linear map, called a
central charge
.

(2)

\mathcal{P}
is a slicing such that for any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
, we have
Z(E)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax Z(\operatorname{cl}E)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}
.

Nonzero objects of
\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
are called
\sigma
-semistable
objects of phase
\phi
.
Simple objects of
\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
are called
\sigma
-stable
objects of phase
\phi
.
The heart of
\mathcal{P}
is also called the heart of the stability condition
\sigma
.
For any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
, we will denote
\phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\pm}(E)
by
\phi^{\pm}_{\sigma}(E)
.
Assuming that
\phi_{\sigma}^{+}(E)-\phi_{\sigma}^{-}(E)<1
, we have
Z(E)\neq 0
and we define the phase
\phi_{\sigma}(E)
to be the unique value such that

(25)

\phi_{\sigma}(E)\in[\phi_{\sigma}^{-}(E),\phi_{\sigma}^{+}(E)],\qquad Z(E)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi_{\sigma}(E)}.

We define the support of
\sigma
to be

(26)

\operatorname{supp}(\sigma)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\operatorname{cl}E\ \right|E\text{ is $\sigma$-semistable}\right\}\subset\Gamma.

Let us equip
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\mathchar 61\relax\Gamma\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
with a norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
.
A pre-stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
on
\mathcal{D}
is called a
stability condition
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
we have (the support property)

(27)

\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\operatorname{cl}E\right\rVert

for all
\sigma
-semistable objects
E
.
It is enough to verify this property for
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
with
\phi\in(0,1]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

A pre-stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
satisfies the support property if and only if there exists a quadratic form
Q
on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(1)

Q
is negative definite on
\operatorname{Ker}(Z\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{C})
.

(2)

For any
\sigma
-semistable object
E
we have
Q(\operatorname{cl}E)\geq 0
.

Proof.

See Lemma
2.13
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Note that if
\operatorname{rk}\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax n
, then a quadratic form
Q
as above has negative index
\geq n-2
.
If
Q
has signature
(2,n-2)
, then
\dim(\operatorname{Ker}Z)\mathchar 61\relax n-2
and
Z\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{C}
is automatically surjective.

Lemma 3.4
.

A pre-stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
satisfies the support property if and only if the following map
defines a norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})

(28)

\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\sigma}\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}\to[0,+\infty],\qquad u\mapsto\sup\left\{\left.\frac{\left\lvert u(E)\right\rvert}{\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert}\ \right|E\text{ is $\sigma$-semistable}\right\}

Proof.

The above map satisfies all properties of a norm, except that it may be not finite.
Let
S\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\operatorname{cl}E\ \right|0\neq E\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi},\,\phi\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\subset\Gamma
.
If
\sigma
satisfies the support property, then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\left\lvert Z(x)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert x\right\rVert
for all
x\in S
.
A linear map
u\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{C}
is bounded, meaning that there exists
C>0
such that
\left\lvert u(x)\right\rvert\leq C\left\lVert x\right\rVert
for all
x\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
.
This implies that
\left\lvert u(x)\right\rvert\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert Z(x)\right\rvert
for all
x\in S
, hence
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}
.

Conversely, we can assume that the norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is given by
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}\left\lvert u_{i}(x)\right\rvert

for some basis
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
of
\Gamma^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}
. For any
x\in S
, we have

\left\lVert x\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}\left\lvert u_{i}(x)\right\rvert\leq\sum_{i}\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\sigma}\cdot\left\lvert Z(x)\right\rvert\leq\max_{i}\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\sigma}\cdot\left\lvert Z(x)\right\rvert.

This proves the support property.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

In our applications the group
\Gamma
will be the numerical Grothendieck group of
\mathcal{D}
.
More precisely, let us assume that
\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Hom}^{n}(E,F)
is finite-dimensional for all
E,F\in\mathcal{D}
and let
\chi(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}\dim\operatorname{Hom}^{n}(E,F)
be the
Euler form
of
\mathcal{D}
defined on the Grothendieck group
K(\mathcal{D})
.
Assuming that
\mathcal{D}
has a Serre functor, meaning an automorphism
\mathcal{S}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}

such that there are natural isomorphisms
\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(F,\mathcal{S}E)^{\vee}
for all
E,\,F\in\mathcal{D}
,
we conclude that the right and the left kernels of
\chi
are equal and we define the
numerical Grothendieck group

\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
to be the quotient of
K(\mathcal{D})
by this kernel.
We define the linear map
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{D})\to\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
to be the projection.

3.4.
Stability functions on abelian categories

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{D}
be the heart of a bounded t-structure so that
K(\mathcal{A})\simeq K(\mathcal{D})
.
We define a
stability function
on
\mathcal{A}

(with respect to
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{A})\to\Gamma
) to be a linear map
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
such that for any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
, the number
Z(E)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax Z(\operatorname{cl}E)
is contained in the (semi-closed) upper-half plane

(29)

\mathbb{H}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.re^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}\ \right|r>0,\,0<\phi\leq 1\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}.

Let us consider the argument map

(30)

\operatorname{Arg}\colon\mathbb{C}^{*}\to(-\pi,\pi],\qquad re^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}\mapsto\pi\phi,\qquad r>0,\,\phi\in(-1,1],

and define the
phase
of
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
to be

(31)

\phi(E)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z(E)\in(0,1].

An object
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
is called
Z
-
semistable
if for any subobject
0\neq E^{\prime}\subset E
we have
\phi(E^{\prime})\leq\phi(E)
.
Equivalently, this means that

(32)

\operatorname{Im}(Z(E)\cdot\bar{Z}(E^{\prime}))\geq 0.

A stability function
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{A}
is said to satisfy the
HN property
if, for any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
, there exists a (unique) filtration, called the
HN filtration
of
E
,

0\mathchar 61\relax E_{0}\subset E_{1}\subset\dots\subset E_{n}\mathchar 61\relax E

such that
E_{k}/E_{k-1}
are
Z
-semistable and have strictly decreasing phases.

It is proved in
[
13
, §5]
that to give a pre-stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
on the triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to giving a pair
(Z,\mathcal{A})
, where

\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{D}
is the heart of a bounded t-structure
and
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
is a stability function on
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the HN property.
The category
\mathcal{A}
is defined to be the heart
\mathcal{P}_{(0,1]}
of the slicing
\mathcal{P}
.
Conversely, one defines
\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
for
\phi\in(0,1]
to be the category of all semistable objects of
\mathcal{A}
having phase
\phi
(plus the zero object).
Then one defines
\mathcal{P}_{\phi+n}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi}[n]
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In what follows we will sometimes write
(Z,\mathcal{A})
for the pre-stability condition
\sigma
.

3.5.
Families of stability conditions

Let
\operatorname{Slice}(\mathcal{D})
be the set of all slicings of
\mathcal{D}
and
\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
be the set of all stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
(satisfying the support property).
Given two slicings
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
, we define the distance between them to be

(33)

d(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\mathchar 61\relax\sup_{0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}}\left\{\left\lvert\phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{+}(E)-\phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}(E)\right\rvert,\left\lvert\phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{-}(E)-\phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}(E)\right\rvert\right\}\in[0,+\infty].

Equivalently
[
13
, Lemma 6.1]
,

(34)

d(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\mathchar 61\relax\inf\left\{\left.\varepsilon\geq 0\ \right|\mathcal{Q}_{\phi}\subset\mathcal{P}_{[\phi-\varepsilon,\phi+\varepsilon]}\ \forall\phi\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

This distance function induces a topology on
\operatorname{Slice}(\mathcal{D})
.
We equip
\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
with the induced topology using the inclusion

(35)

\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})\times\operatorname{Slice}(\mathcal{D}).

It is proved in
[
13
]
that the forgetful map
\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
is a local homeomorphism
and one can equip
\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
with a structure of a complex manifold.

We define a
continuous family of stability conditions
to be a continuous map
\sigma\colon M\to\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
, where
M
is a topological space.
Equivalently, a family of stability conditions
(\sigma_{x}\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{x},\mathcal{P}_{x}))_{x\in M}
is continuous if

(1)

The map
Z\colon M\to\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
,
x\mapsto Z_{x}
, is continuous.

(2)

For any
x\in M
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a neighborhood
x\in U\subset M
such that
d(\mathcal{P}_{x},\mathcal{P}_{y})<\varepsilon
for all
y\in U
.

We can similarly define a continuous family of pre-stability conditions.
In what follows, we will denote
\phi^{\pm}_{\sigma_{x}}
by
\phi^{\pm}_{x}
.
The support property is an open and closed condition
by the following result.

Lemma 3.6

(
[
13
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

Let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
and
\sigma^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
be pre-stability conditions such that for some
0<\eta<\tfrac{1}{4}
we have

\left\lVert Z-Z^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\sigma}<\sin(\pi\eta),\qquad d(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})<\eta.

Then there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

c_{1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma^{\prime}}\leq c_{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}\qquad\forall u\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C}).

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of pre-stability conditions.
If
x\in M
is such that
\sigma_{x}
satisfies the support property, then there exists a neighborhood
x\in U\subset M
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

(36)

\left\lvert Z_{y}(E)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\operatorname{cl}E\right\rVert

for all
y\in U
and
\sigma_{y}
-semistable objects
E
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{x}
satisfy the support property.
It is enough to show that for some neighborhood
x\in U\subset M
and
C>0
, we have
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma_{y}}\leq C\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}
for all
u\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
and
y\in U
.
Indeed, we can assume that the norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is given by
\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}\left\lvert u_{i}(\gamma)\right\rvert
, for a fixed basis
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
of
\Gamma^{\vee}
.
Then, for any
y\in U
,
\sigma_{y}
-semistable object
E
and
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cl}E
, we have

\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}\left\lvert u_{i}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq\sum_{i}\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\sigma_{y}}\cdot\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq C\sum_{i}\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\sigma}\cdot\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert

which is equivalent to the statement of the lemma.

Let
0<\eta<\tfrac{1}{2}
.
For any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
satisfying
\phi_{\sigma}^{+}(E)-\phi_{\sigma}^{-}(E)\leq\eta
and any
u\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
, we have

(37)

\left\lvert u(\operatorname{cl}E)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}}{\cos(\pi\eta)}\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert.

Indeed, let
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
be
\sigma
-semistable HN factors of
E
.
Then (see Example
2.4
)

\left\lvert u(\operatorname{cl}E)\right\rvert\leq\sum_{i}\left\lvert u(\operatorname{cl}F_{i})\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}\cdot\sum_{i}\left\lvert Z(F_{i})\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert}{\cos(\pi\eta)}.

There exists a neighborhood
x\in U\subset M
such that for all
y\in U
we have

\left\lVert Z_{y}-Z\right\rVert_{\sigma}<\tfrac{1}{2}\cos(\pi\eta),\qquad d(\mathcal{P}_{y},\mathcal{P})<\eta/2.

For any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{P}_{y,\phi}
, we have
E\in\mathcal{P}_{(\phi-\eta/2,\phi+\eta/2)}
, hence

\phi_{\sigma}^{+}(E)-\phi_{\sigma}^{-}(E)<\eta
and (
37
)

\left\lvert Z_{y}(E)-Z(E)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lVert Z_{y}-Z\right\rVert_{\sigma}}{\cos(\pi\eta)}\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert.

This implies that

\left\lvert Z_{y}(E)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert

and for any
u\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
we have (
37
)

\left\lvert u(\operatorname{cl}E)\right\rvert<\frac{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}}{\cos(\pi\eta)}\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}}{\cos(\pi\eta)}2\left\lvert Z_{y}(E)\right\rvert.

Therefore
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma_{y}}\leq\frac{2}{\cos(\pi\eta)}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\sigma}
as required.
∎

The following result is well-known.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability conditions
and
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
. Then

(1)

The set
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|E\text{ is }\sigma_{x}\text{-stable}\right\}
is open in
M
.

(2)

The set
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|E\text{ is }\sigma_{x}\text{-semistable}\right\}
is closed in
M
.

We will prove its variant.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability conditions and let

(38)

\mathfrak{r}_{x}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\operatorname{cl}E\ \right|E\text{ is }\sigma_{x}\text{-stable}\right\},\qquad\mathfrak{s}_{x}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\operatorname{cl}E\ \right|E\text{ is }\sigma_{x}\text{-semistable}\right\}

for
x\in M
.
Then, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

(1)

The set
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\gamma\in\mathfrak{r}_{x}\right\}
is open in
M
.

(2)

The set
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{x}\right\}
is closed in
M
.

Proof.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
and an open set
x\in U\subset M

such that

\mathfrak{s}_{y}\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}

for all
y\in U
.
We can assume that
\left\lVert Z_{y}-Z_{x}\right\rVert\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}
for all
y\in U
.
Then
\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)-Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert
, hence
\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
for all
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}
.

Let
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cl}E
for some
\sigma_{x}
-stable object
E\in\mathcal{P}_{x,\phi}
.
For any
0<\eta<1/2
, we can assume that
d(\mathcal{P}_{x},\mathcal{P}_{y})<\eta/2
for all
y\in U
.
Then
\phi_{y}^{\pm}(E)\in(\phi-\eta/2,\phi+\eta/2)
.
This implies that for every
\sigma_{y}
-HN-factor
F_{i}
of
E
,
we have
\phi_{x}^{\pm}(F_{i})\in I\mathchar 61\relax(\phi-\eta,\phi+\eta)
,
hence
F_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{x,I}
.
All nonzero objects in
\mathcal{P}_{x,I}
have classes in the semigroup
S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
, where
V\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi I}
is a strict cone.
There are finitely many such classes that are
\leq\gamma
.
By decreasing
\eta
(and shrinking
U
) we can assume that all such classes are contained in
S(\ell,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
, where
\ell\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}
.
This implies that
\phi_{x}^{\pm}(F_{i})\mathchar 61\relax\phi
.
As
E
is
\sigma_{x}
-stable, the above HN-filtration is trivial and
E
is
\sigma_{y}
-semistable.
Let
E\in\mathcal{P}_{y,\phi^{\prime}}
and let
0\neq F\subset E
be its subobject in
\mathcal{P}_{y,\phi^{\prime}}
.
Then
\phi_{x}^{\pm}(F)\in(\phi^{\prime}-\eta/2,\phi^{\prime}+\eta/2)\subset I
, hence
F\in\mathcal{P}_{x,I}
.
As before, we obtain
\phi_{x}^{\pm}(F)\mathchar 61\relax\phi
.
As
E
is
\sigma_{x}
-stable, we conclude that
F\mathchar 61\relax E
, hence
E
is
\sigma_{y}
-stable.

To show that
\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{x}\right\}
is closed, we need to prove that if
x\in M
is such that for every open
x\in U\subset M
, there exists
y\in U
with
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}
, then
\gamma\in\mathfrak{s}_{x}
.
Let us assume that
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cl}E
for some
0\neq E\in\mathcal{P}_{y,\phi^{\prime}}
and
y\in U
.
By the above discussion, we can assume that

\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
.
Let
\phi\mathchar 61\relax\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z_{x}(\gamma)
.
As before, we assume that
0<\eta<1/2
and
d(\mathcal{P}_{x},\mathcal{P}_{y})<\eta/2
for all
y\in U
.
Then
\phi_{x}^{\pm}(E)\in I^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax(\phi^{\prime}-\eta/2,\phi^{\prime}+\eta/2)
.
We can assume that
E
and
\phi^{\prime}
are chosen in such way that
\phi\in I^{\prime}
.
Then
\phi_{x}^{\pm}(E)\in I\mathchar 61\relax(\phi-\eta,\phi+\eta)
, hence
E\in\mathcal{P}_{x,I}
.
All nonzero objects in
\mathcal{P}_{x,I}
have classes in the semigroup
S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
, where
V\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi I}
is a strict cone.
There are finitely many such classes that are
\leq\gamma
.
By decreasing
\eta
(and shrinking
U
) we can assume that all such classes are contained in
S(\ell,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
, where
\ell\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}
.
This implies that if we can still find
y\in U
and a
\sigma_{y}
-semistable object
E
with
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cl}E
, then
E
is automatically
\sigma_{x}
-semistable.
∎

Theorem 3.10

(Cf.
[
6
,
4
]
)
.

Let
M
be path-connected,

(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability conditions on
M
, and
Q\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form negative semi-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}
for all
x\in M
.
If there exists
x\in M
such that
Q(\operatorname{cl}E)\geq 0
for all
\sigma_{x}
-semistable objects
E
, then the same is true for all points of
M
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7
and Lemma
3.9
,
we can apply Theorem
2.14
.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
M
be locally path-connected and

(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of pre-stability conditions on
M
.
Let
Q\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form and
x\in M
be such that
Q
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}

and
Q(\operatorname{cl}E)\geq 0
for all
\sigma_{x}
-semistable objects
E
.
Then the same is true in some neighborhood of
x
.

3.6.
Action on stability conditions

Let
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{D})
be the group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{D}
,

\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
be the group of elements in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
having positive determinant and let
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
be its universal cover.
There is a left action of the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{D})
and the right action of the group
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
on
\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
defined as follows
[
13
, Lemma 8.2]
.

For any
T\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{D})
and stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
, we define

(39)

T\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}),\qquad Z^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax ZT^{-1},\qquad\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\phi}\mathchar 61\relax T\mathcal{P}_{\phi}.

To describe
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, let us consider the group homomorphism

(40)

\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{Diff}(U(1)),\qquad T\mapsto\bar{T},\qquad\bar{T}(z)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax T(z)/\left\lvert T(z)\right\rvert,

where
\operatorname{Diff}(U(1))
is the group of (orientation-preserving) diffeomorphisms of
U(1)\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
.
Let
\operatorname{Diff}^{*}(\mathbb{R})
be the group of (orientation-preserving) diffeomorphisms
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
that commute with the translation
\phi\mapsto\phi+2
.
Any
f\in\operatorname{Diff}^{*}(\mathbb{R})
induces

(41)

\bar{f}\in\operatorname{Diff}(U(1)),\qquad e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}\mapsto e^{\mathbf{i}\pi f(\phi)}.

Then
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
can be defined using the following diagram with Cartesian squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}^{+}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}^{*}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}^{+}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}(U(1)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\mathbf{i}\pi(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\mathbf{i}\pi(-)}}

Here
\mathbb{C}^{*}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
is given by
a+\mathbf{i}b\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&-b\\
b&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and

(42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{*}\to\operatorname{Diff}(U(1)),\qquad w\mapsto[z\mapsto zw/\left\lvert w\right\rvert]

(43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\to\operatorname{Diff}^{*}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad a+bi\mapsto[\phi\mapsto\phi+a].

For any stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
and an element
g\mathchar 61\relax(T,f)\in\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})

(where
T\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in\operatorname{Diff}^{*}(\mathbb{R})
are mapped to the same element in
\operatorname{Diff}(U(1))
),
we define a new stability condition

(44)

\sigma[g]\mathchar 61\relax(Z[g],\mathcal{P}[g]),\qquad Z[g]\mathchar 61\relax T^{-1}Z,\qquad\mathcal{P}[g]_{\phi}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{f(\phi)}.

In particular, for
a+\mathbf{i}b\in\mathbb{C}
, we define (cf. (
24
))

(45)

Z[a+\mathbf{i}b]\mathchar 61\relax e^{-\mathbf{i}\pi a+\pi b}Z,\qquad\mathcal{P}[a+\mathbf{i}b]_{\phi}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi+a}.

3.7.
Phase behavior in families

Let
(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability conditions.
We denote
\phi^{\pm}_{\sigma_{x}}
by
\phi^{\pm}_{x}
.
For any object
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
the maps

(46)

M\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto\phi^{-}_{x}(E),\qquad M\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto\phi^{+}_{x}(E),

are continuous by (
33
).

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
(\sigma_{t}\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{t},\mathcal{P}_{t}))_{t\in[0,1]}
be a
continuous family of stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
such that
the map
Z\colon[0,1]\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
is differentiable
and

(47)

\operatorname{Im}(Z^{\prime}_{t}(E)\cdot\bar{Z}_{t}(E))\geq 0

for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\sigma_{t}
-stable objects
E\in\mathcal{D}
.
Then, for any object
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
,
the functions

t\mapsto\phi_{t}^{-}(E),\qquad t\mapsto\phi_{t}^{+}(E)

are weakly-increasing .

Proof.

We will prove just that
\phi_{t}^{-}(E)
is weakly-increasing.
It is enough to show that if
E
is
\sigma_{t_{0}}
-stable and
t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+\varepsilon)
for
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
, then
\phi^{-}_{t}(E)\geq\phi_{t_{0}}(E)
.
We can assume that
E
is
\sigma_{t}
-stable for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\varepsilon)
.
The condition (
47
) means that

Z^{\prime}_{t}(E)\in Z_{t}(E)\cdot\bar{}\mathbb{H},\qquad\bar{}\mathbb{H}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.x+\mathbf{i}y\in\mathbb{C}\ \right|y\geq 0\right\},

as
\bar{Z}_{t}(E)
and
Z_{t}(E)^{-1}
are contained in the same ray.
This condition implies that
Z_{t}(E)\in Z_{t_{0}}(E)\cdot\bar{}\mathbb{H}
for
t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+\varepsilon)
and
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
.
Therefore
\phi_{t}(E)\geq\phi_{t_{0}}(E)
.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Given a stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
on a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
,
we define a stability condition
\bar{}\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(\bar{Z},\bar{}\mathcal{P})
on the opposite category
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
as follows.
We define
\bar{}\mathcal{P}_{\phi}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{-\phi}^{\mathrm{op}}
and we define
\bar{Z}(E)
to be the conjugate of
Z(E)
.
Note that if
0\neq E\in\bar{}\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
, then
Z(E)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{-i\pi\phi}
,
hence

\bar{Z}(E)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{i\pi\phi}
.
Assuming that we have a family of stability conditions
(\sigma_{t})_{t}
on
\mathcal{D}
as above, we obtain a family
(\bar{}\sigma_{t})_{t}
of stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Let
\bar{}\phi_{t}^{\pm}
be the phase functions corresponding to these stability conditions.
We have
\operatorname{Im}(\bar{Z}^{\prime}_{t}(E)\cdot Z_{t}(E))\leq 0
, hence we obtain from the first part of the above result that
\bar{}\phi_{t}^{-}(E)
is weakly-decreasing, hence
\phi^{+}_{t}(E)\mathchar 61\relax-\bar{}\phi_{t}^{-}(E)
is weakly-increasing.

Remark 3.14
.

Let
(\sigma_{t}\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{t},\mathcal{P}_{t}))_{t\in[0,1]}
be a continuous family of stability conditions such that the map
Z\colon[0,1]\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
is differentiable.
For any
t\in[0,1]
we define the quadratic form

(48)

Q_{t}\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\gamma\mapsto\operatorname{Im}(Z^{\prime}_{t}(\gamma)\cdot\bar{Z}_{t}(\gamma)),

which is zero on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{t}
.
The requirement of Theorem
3.12
means that
Q_{t}(\operatorname{cl}E)\geq 0
for all
\sigma_{t}
-stable (or
\sigma_{t}
-semistable) objects
E\in\mathcal{D}
.

3.8.
Global dimension of a slicing

We define the
global dimension
of a slicing
\mathcal{P}
to be

(49)

\operatorname{gldim}(\mathcal{P})\mathchar 61\relax\sup\left\{\left.\phi^{\prime}-\phi\ \right|\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi},\mathcal{P}_{\phi^{\prime}})\neq 0\right\}\in[0,+\infty].

Note that if
\phi^{\prime}-\phi<0
, then
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi},\mathcal{P}_{\phi^{\prime}})\mathchar 61\relax 0
by assumption, hence the global dimension is indeed non-negative.
If
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
is a stability condition, then we define its global dimension to be
\operatorname{gldim}(\sigma)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{gldim}(\mathcal{P})
.

Example 3.15
.

Let us assume that
\mathcal{D}
has the Serre functor, meaning an automorphism
\mathcal{S}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
such that there are natural isomorphisms
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(Y,\mathcal{S}X)^{\vee}
for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{D}
.
Let us assume that there exists
d\in\mathbb{R}
, such that

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{\phi})\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{\phi+d}
for all
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
X\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
,
Y\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi^{\prime}}
and
\phi^{\prime}>\phi+d
,
then
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(Y,\mathcal{S}X)^{\vee}\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
Therefore
\operatorname{gldim}(\mathcal{P})\leq d
.
Taking
Y\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{S}X
with
X\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
, we obtain that
\operatorname{gldim}(\mathcal{P})\mathchar 61\relax d
.

Example 3.16
.

Let us assume that the Serre functor has the form
\mathcal{S}\mathchar 61\relax\Phi[d]
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}
and a functor
\Phi
that preserves the heart
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{(0,1]}
.
If
X\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
,
Y\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi^{\prime}}

for
\phi\in(0,1]
and
\phi^{\prime}>\phi+d+1
, then

\mathcal{S}X\in\mathcal{P}_{(d,d+1]}
, hence

\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(Y,\mathcal{S}X)\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
This implies that
\operatorname{gldim}(\mathcal{P})\leq d+1
.
On the other hand, let
X\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi}
for
\phi\in(0,1]
and let
Y\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi^{\prime}}
be the first term of the HN-filtration of
\mathcal{S}X\in\mathcal{P}_{(d,d+1]}
.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)\neq 0
and
\phi^{\prime}>d
, hence
\operatorname{gldim}(\mathcal{P})>d-1
.

4.
Stability data

4.1.
Graded Lie algebras

Let
\Gamma
be a free abelian group of finite rank.
Given a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
,
we define its support to be

(50)

\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}\neq 0\right\}.

We will say that
\mathfrak{g}
has a strict support if
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})

generates a strict semigroup without zero in
\Gamma
.
If
\mathfrak{g}
has a finite strict support, then
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent and we can define the corresponding nilpotent group
G\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\mathfrak{g})
equipped with the bijective exponential map
\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\to G
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
has a strict support, we define its pro-nilpotent completion
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
as follows.
Let
S\subset\Gamma
be the strict semigroup (without zero) generated by
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})
.
We equip
S
with a partial order as in §
2.2
.
Given a finite lower set
I\subset S
,
we define the Lie algebra

(51)

\mathfrak{g}_{I}\mathchar 61\relax\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{m}_{I}\simeq\bigoplus_{\gamma\in I}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\qquad\mathfrak{m}_{I}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S\backslash I}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}.

If
I\subset J
are two lower sets, then there is a canonical epimorphism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{J}\to\mathfrak{g}_{I}
.
We define the completion

(52)

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\varprojlim_{I\subset S}\mathfrak{g}_{I},

where the limit is taken over all finite lower sets
I\subset S
.
Note that we have an isomorphism of vector spaces

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\simeq\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
.
The Lie algebra
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
is pro-nilpotent as the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{I}
, for finite lower sets
I\subset S
, are nilpotent.
We define the corresponding pro-nilpotent group
\hat{G}\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{g})\mathchar 61\relax\varprojlim_{I}\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{I})
and the bijective exponential map
\exp\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\to\hat{G}
(we denote its inverse by
\log
).

For an arbitrary
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we
define the vector space

(53)

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}.

Given
a\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
, we define

(54)

\operatorname{supp}(a)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|a_{\gamma}\neq 0\right\}.

4.2.
Examples of graded Lie algebras

The following two examples of graded Lie algebras usually appear in the study of stability data and wall-crossing structures.

Example 4.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a semisimple Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
, with a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
, the set of roots
\Delta\subset\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}

and a set of positive roots
\Delta_{+}\subset\Delta
.
Let
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
be the root lattice of
\mathfrak{g}
, so that
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})\mathchar 61\relax\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{h}
.
Using the root space decomposition
\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta\cup\left\{0\right\}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
with
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathfrak{h}
,
we can consider
\mathfrak{g}
as a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra.
Let
\Pi\subset\Delta_{+}
be the set of simple roots and
e_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}
,
f_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}
,
for
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi
, be the standard generators.
The algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is equipped with the Chevalley involution
which is a Lie algebra automorphism
\omega\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
given by

(55)

\omega(e_{i})\mathchar 61\relax-f_{i},\qquad\omega(f_{i})\mathchar 61\relax-e_{i},\qquad\omega(h)\mathchar 61\relax-h,\qquad h\in\mathfrak{h}.

One can also consider
\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
with the subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
consisting of diagonal matrices
and the root lattice
