\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
generated by
\varepsilon_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
,
\sum_{j}a_{j}E_{jj}\mapsto a_{i}
.
As before, we have a root space decomposition

\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta\cup\left\{0\right\}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
with
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathfrak{h}
and
\Delta\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\ \right|i\neq j\right\}
.
Therefore
\mathfrak{g}
is
\Gamma
-graded.

Example 4.2
.

Let
\Gamma\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be equipped with a skew-symmetric
\mathbb{Z}
-valued bilinear form
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle

and let
R
be a commutative ring with an invertible element

s\in R
(in our later considerations
R
is the ring of motivic classes and
s\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, where
\mathbb{L}
is the class of the Lefschetz motive).
We define the
quantum torus
to be a
\Gamma
-graded associative
R
-algebra

(56)

\mathbb{T}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}Rx^{\gamma},\qquad x^{\alpha}\circ x^{\beta}\mathchar 61\relax s^{\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle}x^{\alpha+\beta}.

We can also consider it as a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra,
where the Lie bracket is given by the commutator.
The algebra
\mathbb{T}
is equipped with the involution

x^{\alpha}\mapsto x^{-\alpha}
.

Assuming that
s^{2}-1
is invertible,
we define new generators

\bar{x}^{\alpha}\mathchar 61\relax x^{\alpha}/(s-s^{-1})
, so that

(57)

[\bar{x}^{\alpha},\bar{x}^{\beta}]\mathchar 61\relax\frac{s^{\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle}-s^{-\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle}}{s-s^{-1}}\bar{x}^{\alpha+\beta}.

Taking the limit
s\to-1
, we define a new
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra

(58)

\bar{}\mathbb{T}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathbb{Q}\bar{x}^{\gamma},\qquad[\bar{x}^{\alpha},\bar{x}^{\beta}]\mathchar 61\relax(-1)^{\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle}\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle\bar{x}^{\alpha+\beta},

called the torus Lie algebra.
It is equipped with the involution
\bar{x}^{\alpha}\mapsto\bar{x}^{-\alpha}
.

4.3.
Factorizations

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a graded Lie algebra with a finite strict support having a decomposition
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})\mathchar 61\relax S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}

such that
\mathfrak{g}_{i}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S_{i}}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
are subalgebras for
i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2
.
Then the map

\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{1})\times\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{2})\to\exp(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad(g_{1},g_{2})\mapsto g_{1}g_{2},

is a bijection.

Proof.

There exists
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\mathfrak{g}]\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
Let us assume that
\gamma\in S_{1}

and let

G_{\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma})
.
We have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{1})\times\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{1}/\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda})\times\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda})}

where both vertical arrows are
G_{\gamma}
-torsors, the top arrow is
G_{\gamma}
-equivariant,
and the bottom arrow is a bijection by induction on the size of
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})
.
We conclude that the top arrow is also a bijection.
∎

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a graded Lie algebra with a strict support having a decomposition
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})\mathchar 61\relax S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}

such that
\mathfrak{g}_{i}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S_{i}}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
are subalgebras for
i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2
.
Then the map

\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{1})\times\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{2})\to\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{g}),\qquad(g_{1},g_{2})\mapsto g_{1}g_{2},

is a bijection.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra with a strict support
and
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear map.
Let
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\simeq\prod_{\gamma}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
be the pro-nilpotent completion of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\hat{G}
be the corresponding pro-nilpotent group.
Given a strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
,
we define the Lie algebra

(59)

\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{Z(\gamma)\in V}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}\subset\mathfrak{g}

and consider its pro-nilpotent completion
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z}\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}

and the corresponding pro-nilpotent group
\hat{G}_{V,Z}\subset\hat{G}
.
In particular, for any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
, we consider the pro-nilpotent Lie algebra
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z}\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}

and the corresponding pro-nilpotent group
\hat{G}_{\ell,Z}\subset\hat{G}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any blunt strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
, the map

\prod_{\ell\subset V}\hat{G}_{\ell,Z}\to\hat{G}_{V,Z},\qquad(g_{\ell})_{\ell}\mapsto\prod^{\to}_{\ell\subset V}g_{\ell},

is a bijection, where the product is taken in the clockwise order over all rays
\ell\subset V
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the statement for
\mathfrak{g}
having a finite strict support contained in
Z^{-1}(V)
.
For any ray
\ell\subset V
, let
S_{\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})\ \right|Z(\gamma)\in\ell\right\}
.
Then
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{g})\mathchar 61\relax\bigsqcup_{\ell}S_{\ell}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S_{\ell}}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
.
Now the statement follows from Lemma
4.3
.
∎

4.4.
Stability data

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra
and let us equip
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\mathchar 61\relax\Gamma\otimes\mathbb{R}
with a norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
.
We define
stability data

[
41
, §2.1]

to be a pair
(Z,a)
, where
Z\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})

and
a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{\gamma})_{\gamma}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}

is such that there exists
\varepsilon>0
satisfying
(the
support property
)

(60)

\operatorname{supp}(a)\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}.

Sometimes
Z\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
will be clear from the context and we will call
a\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
stability data.

For any blunt strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
,
let us consider the semigroup (
11
)

(61)

S(V,Z,\varepsilon)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{sgr}\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|Z(\gamma)\in V,\,\left\lvert Z(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}\subset\Gamma

which is strict by Lemma
2.2
.
We define the corresponding Lie algebra and its pro-nilpotent completion

(62)

\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S(V,Z,\varepsilon)}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\qquad\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}\simeq\prod_{\gamma\in S(V,Z,\varepsilon)}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}.

We consider the corresponding pro-nilpotent group
\hat{G}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}

and the bijective exponential map

\exp\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}\to\hat{G}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma
4.5
, we also have a bijection

(63)

\exp_{Z}\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}\to\hat{G}_{V,Z,\varepsilon},\qquad a\mapsto\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\exp(a_{\ell}),\qquad a_{\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in\ell}a_{\gamma}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z,\varepsilon}.

where the product is taken in the clockwise order over all rays in
V
.

If
\varepsilon>\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, then
S(V,Z,\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z,\varepsilon^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.
We define the ind-pro-nilpotent Lie algebra

(64)

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z}\mathchar 61\relax\varinjlim_{\varepsilon>0}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}\subset\prod_{Z(\gamma)\in V}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g},

the ind-pro-nilpotent group
\hat{G}_{V,Z}\mathchar 61\relax\varinjlim_{\varepsilon>0}\hat{G}_{V,Z,\varepsilon}

and the bijective exponential map

(65)

\exp\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z}\to\hat{G}_{V,Z}.

Note that
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z}
is independent of a choice of a norm on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Note also that the vector space

\prod_{Z(\gamma)\in V}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
is not a Lie algebra in general.
The maps in (
63
) induce a bijection

(66)

\exp_{Z}\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{V,Z}\to\hat{G}_{V,Z}.

Let
a\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
be stability data.
For any blunt strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
,
we define

(67)

A_{V}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\exp(a_{\ell})\in\hat{G}_{V,Z},\qquad a_{\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in\ell}a_{\gamma}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z}.

The above stability data
a\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
is equivalent
(see
[
41
]
)
to the family
(A_{V}\in\hat{G}_{V,Z})_{V}
, where
V
runs through all blunt strict cones in
\mathbb{C}
, such that for a decomposition
V\mathchar 61\relax V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}
(in the clockwise order), we have the
factorization property
in
\hat{G}_{V,Z}

(68)

A_{V}\mathchar 61\relax A_{V_{1}}A_{V_{2}}.

Note that rays
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
can be identified with elements of the quotient group
\mathbb{C}^{*}/\mathbb{R}_{>0}\simeq U(1)
, hence stability data is equivalent to a collection of elements

(69)

(a_{\ell}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z})_{\ell\in U(1)}.

Let
\eta\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
be an involutive automorphism of Lie algebras that maps
\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
to
\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}
.
Then it induces an automorphism
\eta\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z}\to\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{-\ell,Z}
.
We say that stability data is
\eta
-symmetric if
\eta(a_{\ell})\mathchar 61\relax a_{-\ell}
for all rays
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
.
Equivalently, this means that
\eta(a_{\gamma})\mathchar 61\relax a_{-\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

4.5.
Families of stability data

For more information on continuous families of stability data see
[
41
,
44
]
.
Let
M
be a topological space.
We define a
continuous family of stability data
on
M
to be a pair
(Z,a)
, where

Z\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
is a continuous map
and
a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{x}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g})_{x\in M}
is a collection such that

(1)

For any
x\in M
, there exists an open neighborhood
x\in U\subset M
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}\qquad\forall y\in U.

(2)

For any strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
, we define (
67
)

A_{x,V}\mathchar 61\relax\prod^{\to}_{\ell\subset V}\exp(a_{x,\ell})\subset\hat{G}_{V,Z_{x}},\qquad a_{x,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{Z_{x}(\gamma)\in\ell}a_{x,\gamma}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z_{x}}.

We require that if
Z_{x}(\operatorname{supp}a_{x})\cap\partial V\mathchar 61\relax\varnothing
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then the map

M\ni y\mapsto p_{\gamma}\log(A_{y,V})\in\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}

is constant in a neighborhood of
x
,
where
p_{\gamma}\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
is the projection.

The second axiom is called the
Kontsevich-Soibelman wall-crossing formula

[
41
, Def. 3]
.
Sometimes
Z\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
will be clear from the context and we will call
a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{x}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g})_{x\in M}
a continuous family of stability data.
Locally, we can perform calculations by applying Lemma
2.7
.

Remark 4.6
.

The above definition is slightly different from
[
41
, Def. 3]
.
Instead of the first axiom one requires in loc.cit.
a seemingly stronger condition that if
a_{x}
satisfies the support property with respect to a quadratic form
Q
, meaning that
Q
is negative definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}
(this is an open condition) and
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}(a_{x})
, then stability data also satisfy the support property with respect to
Q

in a neighborhood of
x
.
We will prove in Theorem
4.10
that this condition is automatically satisfied if
M
is locally path connected.
For a similar result for families of stability conditions
see Theorem
3.10
.

Remark 4.7
.

Because of the continuity of
Z
, the first axiom is equivalent to the requirement that for any
x\in M
there exists an open neighborhood
x\in U\subset M
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

(70)

\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}\qquad\forall y\in U.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
Z\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
be continuous and

a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{x}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g})_{x\in M}

be a collection satisfying the first axiom.
Then the following conditions are equivalent

(1)

For any
x\in M
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
and a strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
Z_{x}(\operatorname{supp}a_{x})\cap\partial V\mathchar 61\relax\varnothing
, the map

y\mapsto p_{\gamma}\log(A_{y,V})

is constant in a neighborhood of
x
.

(2)

For any
x\in M
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
and a strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
Z_{x}(\Gamma)\cap\partial V\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0\right\}
, the map

y\mapsto p_{\gamma}\log(A_{y,V})

is constant in a neighborhood of
x
.

(3)

For any
x\in M
,
\gamma\in\Gamma

and a strict cone
V\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in V
,
there exists a strict cone
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in V^{\prime}\subset V

such that
Z_{x}(\Gamma)\cap\partial V^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0\right\}
and the map

y\mapsto p_{\gamma}\log(A_{y,V^{\prime}})

is constant in a neighborhood of
x
.

Proof.

It is clear that (1) implies (2) and (3).
To see that (2) implies (1), let us choose
\varepsilon>0
such that the first axiom is satisfied for
x\in\ M
and
\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\neq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
for all
0\neq\gamma\in\Gamma
.
We embed
V\subset\mathbb{C}
into a strict cone
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
Z_{x}(\Gamma)\cap\partial V^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0\right\}
.
We consider the semigroup
S\mathchar 61\relax S(V^{\prime},Z_{x},\varepsilon)
and the lower set
I\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma^{\prime}\in S\ \right|\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma\right\}
.
By Lemma
2.7
, we can assume that
I\subset S(V^{\prime},Z_{y},\varepsilon)
is a lower set for all
y
.
Therefore we can restrict our considerations from
\mathfrak{g}
to the nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{I}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}/\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S\backslash I}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}\simeq\bigoplus_{\gamma\in I}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
.
By assumption, the map
y\mapsto A_{y,V^{\prime}}
(in the nilpotent group
\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{I})
) is constant in a neighborhood of
x
.
This implies that the map
y\mapsto A_{y,V}
is also constant in a neighborhood of
x
.

To see that (3) implies (2), let us choose
\varepsilon>0
as before.
We consider the semigroup
S\mathchar 61\relax S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon)
and the lower set
I\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma^{\prime}\in S\ \right|\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma\right\}
.
By Lemma
2.7
, we can assume that
I\subset S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)
is a lower set for all
y
.
Therefore we can restrict our considerations from
\mathfrak{g}
to the nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{I}
.
By induction, we can assume that
y\mapsto p_{\gamma^{\prime}}\log(A_{y,V})
is constant in a neighborhood of
x
for all
\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
.
By assumption, we can decompose
V\mathchar 61\relax V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup V_{3}
, where
V_{i}
are strict cones ordered clockwise, such that
Z_{x}(\Gamma)\cap\partial V_{i}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0\right\}
,
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in V_{2}
and the map
y\mapsto p_{\gamma}\log A_{y,V_{2}}
is constant in a neighborhood of
x
.
Shrinking the neighborhood of
x
if needed, we conclude that
y\mapsto A_{y,V_{i}}
(in the nilpotent Lie group
\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{I})
) is constant for
i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2,3
.
Therefore also
y\mapsto A_{y,V}
is constant.
∎

Note that the second axiom allows us to express
a_{x,\gamma}
locally as finite Lie expressions of elements
a_{y,\gamma^{\prime}}
. The following result shows that this can be done globally
(cf.
[
41
, §2.3]
).

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
M
be path-connected and
(Z,a)
be a continuous family of stability data on
M
.
For any
x,y\in M
and
\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, the element
a_{x,\gamma}
is a finite Lie expression of elements
a_{y,\gamma^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We can assume that
M\mathchar 61\relax[0,1]
.
We will show that for any
t\in[0,1]
and
\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, the element
a_{t,\gamma}
is a finite Lie expression of elements
a_{1,\gamma^{\prime}}
.
As
M
is compact, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\operatorname{supp}a_{t}\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}

for all
t\in M
.
Let
0<\theta<\pi/4
.
By dividing
[0,1]
into a finite union of intervals,
we can assume that

\left\lVert Z_{t}-Z_{1}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sin(\theta)

for all
t\in[0,1]
.
For any
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}a_{t}
, we have

\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)-Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)\right\rvert
, hence

\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert.

On the other hand, if
\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}
satisfies

\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
, then

\left\lvert Z_{t}(\gamma)-Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\sin(\theta)\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\leq\sin(\theta)\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert,

hence the angle between
Z_{t}(\gamma)
and
Z_{1}(\gamma)
is
\leq\theta
.
If
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}
with
Z_{t}(\gamma_{i})
contained in the same ray as
Z_{t}(\gamma)
and
\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma_{i})\right\rvert\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma_{i}\right\rVert
,
then the angle between
Z_{t}(\gamma_{i})
and
Z_{1}(\gamma_{i})
is
\leq\theta
,
hence the angle between
Z_{1}(\gamma_{1})
and
Z_{1}(\gamma_{2})
is
\leq 2\theta<\pi/2
.
This implies that

\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma_{i}\right\rVert\leq\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma_{i})\right\rvert<\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert,\qquad i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2.

For a fixed
\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, we can assume by induction that the result is true for all elements of the finite set

D\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}\ \right|\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma^{\prime})\right\rvert<\left\lvert Z_{1}(\gamma)\right\rvert\right\}.

By the second axiom, for any
t\in[0,1]
, there exists an open set
t\in U\subset[0,1]
such that, for any
t^{\prime}\in U
,
the element
a_{t,\gamma}
can be written as a finite Lie expression of
a_{t^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}
with
Z_{t}(\gamma^{\prime})
contained in the same ray as
Z_{t}(\gamma)
.
Therefore, there exists a finite sequence
0\mathchar 61\relax t_{0}<\dots<t_{n}\mathchar 61\relax 1
such that,
for any
t^{\prime}\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
,
the element
a_{t_{i},\gamma}
can be written
as a finite Lie expression of
a_{t^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}

with
Z_{t_{i}}(\gamma^{\prime})
contained in the same ray as
Z_{t_{i}}(\gamma)
.
By the discussion above we conclude that
\gamma^{\prime}\in D\cup\left\{\gamma\right\}
.
Therefore by the inductive assumption we only need to express
a_{t_{i+1},\gamma}
in terms of
a_{1,\gamma^{\prime}}
.
Now we repeat the previous step.
∎

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
M
be a path-connected topological space and
(Z,a)
be a continuous family of stability data on
M
.
Let
Q\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form that is negative semi-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}
for all
x\in M
.
If there exists
x\in M
such that

\operatorname{supp}a_{x}\subset\left\{Q\geq 0\right\}
, then the same is true for all points of
M
.

Proof.

We will use the same notation and assumptions as in the previous lemma and we assume that
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}a_{1}
.
Let
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}a_{0}
and let
0\leq i\leq n
be the maximal number such that
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}a_{t_{i}}
.
If
i\mathchar 61\relax n
, then we are done.
Otherwise, we can express
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{k\mathchar 61\relax 1}^{m}\gamma_{k}
, where
\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname{supp}a_{t_{i+1}}
and
Z_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{k})
are contained in the same ray as
Z_{t_{i}}(\gamma)
.
We conclude that
\gamma_{k}\in D
, hence by induction
Q(\gamma_{k})\geq 0
.
By Lemma
2.9
, we obtain
Q(\gamma)\geq 0
.

Alternatively, we can apply Theorem
2.14
.
For any
x\in M
, let
\mathfrak{s}_{x}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{supp}(a_{x})
and let
\mathfrak{r}_{x}\mathchar 61\relax\bigcup_{\ell\subset\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{r}_{x,\ell}
, where
\mathfrak{r}_{x,\ell}
is the set of minimal elements in the strict semigroup
\operatorname{sgr}(\mathfrak{s}_{x}\cap Z^{-1}(\ell))
.
It is easy to see that
(Z_{x},\mathfrak{r}_{x},\mathfrak{s}_{x})_{x\in M}
is a continuous family of stable supports
(cf. Lemma
3.9
),
hence we can apply Theorem
2.14
.
∎

The following results follows from
[
41
, Theorem 3]
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
M
be a path-connected topological space,
(Z,a)
be a continuous family of stability data on
M
and
Q\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic form that
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{x}
and satisfies
\operatorname{supp}a_{x}\subset\left\{Q\geq 0\right\}
for all
x\in M
.
If
x,y\in M
are such that
Z_{x}\mathchar 61\relax Z_{y}
, then
a_{x}\mathchar 61\relax a_{y}
.

This result implies that it is enough to parametrize stability data by subsets of
\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
as long as stability data are supported on
\left\{Q\geq 0\right\}
for a fixed quadratic from
Q
.

4.6.
Wall-crossing structures

In this section we introduce wall-crossing structures following
[
43
]
and we discuss their relationship to continuous families of stability data.
A simplified definition of a wall-crossing structure, without usage of WCS sheaves, is given in (
91
).

4.6.1.
Group slicing

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra with a finite strict support.
For any
x\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
, we define the subalgebras

(71)

\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{+}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{x(\gamma)>0}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\qquad\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{x(\gamma)\mathchar 61\relax 0}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\qquad\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{-}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{x(\gamma)<0}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent, hence we can define the corresponding nilpotent groups

(72)

G\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad G^{(x)}_{\star}\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{\star}),\qquad\star\in\left\{+,0,-\right\}.

By Lemma
4.3
, there is a bijection

(73)

G_{+}^{(x)}\times G_{0}^{(x)}\times G_{-}^{(x)}\to G,\qquad(g_{+},g_{0},g_{-})\mapsto g\mathchar 61\relax g_{+}g_{0}g_{-}.

Taking the inverse and the projection to the middle term,
we obtain a map (which is not a group homomorphism in general)

(74)

\pi_{x}\colon G\to G^{(x)}_{0},\qquad g\mapsto g_{0}.

Similarly, let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra with a strict support,

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
be its pro-nilpotent completion and
\hat{G}
be the corresponding pro-nilpotent group.
For any
x\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
, we consider Lie algebras (
71
), the corresponding pro-nilpotent Lie algebras
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{\pm},\,\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{0}
and the corresponding pro-nilpotent groups
\hat{G}^{(x)}_{\pm},\,\hat{G}^{(x)}_{0}
.
Then we have a bijection

(75)

\hat{G}_{+}^{(x)}\times\hat{G}_{0}^{(x)}\times\hat{G}_{-}^{(x)}\to\hat{G},\qquad(g_{+},g_{0},g_{-})\mapsto g\mathchar 61\relax g_{+}g_{0}g_{-},

and we consider the corresponding projection

(76)

\pi_{x}\colon\hat{G}\to\hat{G}^{(x)}_{0},\qquad g\mapsto g_{0}.

These projections are natural with respect to morphisms of
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebras.
More precisely, for any homomorphism
f\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
of
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebras with strict support and
\hat{G}\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{g})
,
H\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{h})
,
we have a commutative diagram

(77)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{G}^{(x)}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}^{(x)}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{x}}

4.6.2.
Special sheaf construction

Let
M
be a topological space and
(\pi_{x}\colon S\to S_{x})_{x\in M}
be a collection of maps between sets.
Then we define the sheaf
\mathcal{S}
over
M
to be the
sheafification of the presheaf

(78)

M\supset U\mapsto\left\{\left.(\pi_{x}(s))_{x\in U}\ \right|s\in S\right\}\subset\prod_{x\in U}S_{x}.

In particular, let us assume that the maps
\pi_{x}\colon S\to S_{x}
are surjective and

\forall s,s^{\prime}\in S
the set

(79)

\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\pi_{x}(s)\mathchar 61\relax\pi_{x}(s^{\prime})\right\}

is open in
M
(cf.
[
41
, §2.1.2]
).
Then the set of sections
\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})
over an open set
U\subset M

consists of
a\in\prod_{x\in U}S_{x}

such that, for any
x\in U
,
there exists an open neighborhood
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U

and
s\in S

satisfying

(80)

\pi_{y}(s)\mathchar 61\relax a_{y}\qquad\forall y\in U^{\prime}.

The stalk
\mathcal{S}_{x}
of the sheaf
\mathcal{S}
is equal to
S_{x}
for all
x\in M
.

Let us assume additionally that, for all
x\in M
, we have
S_{x}\subset S
such that
S_{x}\hookrightarrow S\xrightarrow{\pi_{x}}S_{x}

is the identity.
Then
\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})
consists of
a\in\prod_{x\in U}S_{x}
(or
a\in S^{U}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{x\in U}S
) such that, for any
x\in U
,
there exists an open neighborhood
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U

satisfying

(81)

\pi_{y}(a_{x})\mathchar 61\relax a_{y}\qquad\forall y\in U^{\prime}.

4.6.3.
WCS sheaf: finite strict support

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra with a finite strict support.
As in §
4.6.1
, we have the maps
\pi_{x}\colon G\to G_{0}^{(x)}
for
x\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
.
For any
g,g^{\prime}\in G
, the set

(82)

\left\{\left.x\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}\ \right|\pi_{x}(g)\mathchar 61\relax\pi_{x}(g^{\prime})\right\}

is open, see e.g.
[
52
]
.
Applying construction from §
4.6.2
to the topological space
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
and the collection of surjective maps
(\pi_{x})_{x\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}}
,
we define the sheaf
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of
wall-crossing structures
over
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
as follows.
For any open set
U\subset\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
,
the set of sections
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})
consists of elements

a\in\prod_{x\in U}\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{(x)}

(or
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{U}
)
such that, for any
x\in U
,
there exists an open neighborhood
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U

satisfying

(83)

\pi_{y}(e^{a_{x}})\mathchar 61\relax e^{a_{y}}\qquad\forall y\in U^{\prime}.

4.6.4.
WCS sheaf: strict support

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra having a strict support and let
S\subset\Gamma
be the strict semigroup generated by this support.
As in (
52
),
we define the pro-nilpotent Lie algebra

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\varprojlim_{I\subset S}\mathfrak{g}_{I}\simeq\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
,
where the limit runs over finite lower sets
I\subset S
and
\mathfrak{g}_{I}
denotes the corresponding nilpotent Lie algebra (
51
).
We define the sheaf
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of wall-crossing structures over
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
to be

(84)

\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathchar 61\relax\varprojlim_{I\subset S}\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{I}},

where the limit runs over finite lower sets
I\subset S
.
For any open set
U\subset\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
, the set of sections is equal to

(85)

\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})\mathchar 61\relax\varprojlim_{I\subset S}\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{I}}).

Explicitly, this means that
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})
consists of

a\in\prod_{x\in U}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{0}

(or
a\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{U}
)
such that

(86)

(p_{I}a_{x})_{x\in U}\in\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{I}}),

where
p_{I}\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}_{I}
is the projection,
for any finite lower set
I\subset S
.
This means that
a\in\prod_{x\in U}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{0}
is contained in
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})
if and only if
for any
x\in U
and
\gamma\in S
,
there exists an open set
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U
such that

(87)

a_{y,\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax p_{\gamma}\log(\pi_{y}(e^{a_{x}}))\qquad\forall y\in U^{\prime},

where
p_{\gamma}\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
is the projection and
\pi_{y}\colon\hat{G}\to\hat{G}_{0}^{(y)}
is the map defined in §
4.6.1
.

4.6.5.
WCS sheaf: arbitrary support

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be an arbitrary
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of all strict semigroups
S\subset\Gamma
, equipped with the partial order induced by inclusion.
For every semigroup
S\in\mathcal{P}
,
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{S}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}

has a strict support.
If
S\subset S^{\prime}
, then
\mathfrak{g}_{S}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{S^{\prime}}
and we obtain the induced inclusion of sheaves
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{S}}\subset\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{S^{\prime}}}
.
We define the sheaf
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of wall-crossing structures over
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
to be the colimit of sheaves

(88)

\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathchar 61\relax\varinjlim_{S\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{S}}.

Note that for any open set
U\subset\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
,
we have
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{S}})\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{S}^{U}\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{U}
,
hence
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{U}
.
More explicitly,
the set of sections
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})
consists of elements

a\in\prod_{x\in U}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(x)}_{0}\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{U}

such that, for any
x\in U
, there exists an open neighborhood
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U
such that the set

\bigcup_{y\in U^{\prime}}\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})\subset\Gamma

generates a strict semigroup
S\subset\Gamma
and

(89)

(a_{y})_{y\in U^{\prime}}\in\Gamma(U^{\prime},\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{S}}).

4.6.6.
Wall-crossing structures

Given a topological space
M
and a continuous map
\theta\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
, we define the sheaf

(90)

\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g},\theta}\mathchar 61\relax\theta^{*}\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}

of
wall-crossing structures
over
M
.
We define a
wall-crossing structure
over
M
to be a global section of
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g},\theta}
.
We can actually describe the sheaf
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g},\theta}
directly in the same way as the sheaf
\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

(1)

If
\mathfrak{g}
has a finite strict support,
then the set of sections
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g},\theta})

(for
U\subset M
)
consists of
a\in\prod_{x\in U}\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{(\theta_{x})}
such that, for any
x\in U
, there exists an open set
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U
such that
\pi_{\theta_{y}}(e^{a_{x}})\mathchar 61\relax e^{a_{y}}
for all
y\in U^{\prime}
,
where
\pi_{\theta_{y}}\colon G\to G^{(\theta_{y})}_{0}
is the projection.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{g}
has a strict support generating the semigroup
S\subset\Gamma
, then
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g},\theta})
(for
U\subset M
)
consists of

a\in\prod_{x\in U}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(\theta_{x})}_{0}
such that

(p_{I}a_{x})_{x\in U}\in\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}_{I},\theta})
,
where
p_{I}\colon\hat{}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}_{I}
is the projection,
for any lower set
I\subset S
.

(3)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is arbitrary, then
\Gamma(U,\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g},\theta})

(for
U\subset M
)
consists of
a\in\prod_{x\in U}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}^{(\theta_{x})}_{0}

such that, for any
x\in U
, there exists an opens set
x\in U^{\prime}\subset U
such that

\bigcup_{y\in U^{\prime}}\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})
generates a strict semigroup
S\subset\Gamma
and

(a_{y})_{y\in U^{\prime}}\in\Gamma(U^{\prime},\operatorname{WCS}_{\theta,\mathfrak{g}_{S}})
.

We conclude that a wall-crossing structure on
M
(for a fixed continuous map
\theta\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
) is a collection
a\in\prod_{x\in M}\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{(\theta_{x})}
such that for any
x\in M
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, there exists an open set
x\in U\subset M
such that
\bigcup_{y\in U}\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})
generates a strict semigroup
S\subset\Gamma
and

(91)

a_{y,\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax p_{\gamma}(\log\pi_{y}(e^{a_{x}}))\qquad\forall y\in U,

where we consider projections
\pi_{y}\colon\hat{G}_{S}\to\hat{G}_{S,0}^{(\theta_{y})}
with
\hat{G}_{S}\mathchar 61\relax\exp(\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{S})
,
\mathfrak{g}_{S}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma^{\prime}\in S}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.

Remark 4.12
.

It is proved in
[
43
, Theorem 2.1.6]
that if
\mathfrak{g}
has a strict support,
then

(92)

\Gamma(\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee},\operatorname{WCS}_{\mathfrak{g}})\simeq\hat{}\mathfrak{g}.

Wall-crossing structures on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
can be identified with scattering diagrams
[
43
]
.

4.6.7.
WCS induced by families of stability data

Let
M
be a topological space and
(Z_{x},a_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability data on a
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
Then every
Z_{x}\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
has the form
Z_{x}\mathchar 61\relax-\theta_{x}+\mathbf{i}\rho_{x}
for some
\theta_{x},\rho_{x}\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
.
We define the continuous map

(93)

\theta\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee},\qquad\theta_{x}\mathchar 61\relax-\operatorname{Re}(Z_{x})\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Im}(\mathbf{i}^{-1}\cdot Z_{x}).

Note that if
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in\mathbb{H}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then
\theta_{x}(\gamma)\mathchar 61\relax 0
if and only if
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in\ell_{0}\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

The collection

(94)

b\mathchar 61\relax(b_{x})_{x\in M},\qquad b_{x}\mathchar 61\relax(a_{x,\gamma})_{Z_{x}(\gamma)\in\ell_{0}}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{(\theta_{x})},

is a wall-crossing structure on
M
.

Proof.

For any
x\in M
, there exists an open set
x\in U\subset M
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})\subset\left\{\left.\gamma\in\Gamma\ \right|\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\right\}.

Let
0<\eta<\pi/4
and let us assume that
\left\lVert Z_{x}-Z_{y}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sin(\eta)
for all
y\in U
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}(a_{y})
, we have

\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
and

\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)-Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert<\varepsilon\sin(\eta)\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert\leq\sin(\eta)\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert.

This implies that the angle between
Z_{x}(\gamma)
and
Z_{y}(\gamma)
is
<\eta
.
Let
V\subset\mathbb{C}
be the cone around
\ell_{0}
having angle
2\eta<\pi/2
.
If
\gamma\in\operatorname{supp}b_{y}
, then
Z_{y}(\gamma)\in\ell_{0}
, hence
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in V
.
We conclude that

\operatorname{supp}(b_{y})\subset S\mathchar 61\relax S(V,Z_{x},\varepsilon/2)\qquad\forall y\in U,

hence
\bigcup_{y\in U}\operatorname{supp}(b_{y})
generates a strict semigroup.
Let us consider
0\neq\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
.
If
Z_{x}(\gamma)\notin\ell_{0}
, then we can decrease
\eta
and shrink
U
so that
\gamma\notin S
and the condition (
91
) is automatically satisfied.
If
Z_{x}(\gamma)\in\ell_{0}
, then we can decrease
\eta
and shrink
U
so that the finite lower set
I\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\gamma^{\prime}\in S\ \right|\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma\right\}
is contained in
Z_{x}^{-1}(\ell_{0})
.
Note the condition (
91
) follows from the second axiom of a continuous family of stability data.
∎

Note that the above wall-crossing structure does not capture the full information of the family of stability data as it remembers only stability data along a particular ray.
To solve this problem, we either need to assume that rays rotate in the family
M
or we can introduce such rotation explicitly as follows
[
43
]
.
Let us consider a new continuous family of stability data
over
\hat{M}\mathchar 61\relax U(1)\times M

(95)

U(1)\times M\ni(z,x)\mapsto(\mathbf{i}z^{-1}Z_{x},a_{x}).

Then we define the corresponding continuous map

(96)

\hat{}\theta\colon\hat{M}\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee},\qquad(z,x)\mapsto\operatorname{Im}(z^{-1}Z_{x})

By the previous lemma, we have a wall-crossing structure

(97)

b\mathchar 61\relax(b_{z,x})_{(z,x)\in\hat{M}}\qquad b_{z,x}\mathchar 61\relax(a_{x,\gamma})_{Z_{x}(\gamma)\in z\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{(\hat{}\theta_{z,x})}.

This wall-crossing structure can be used to completely recover the original continuous family of stability data on
M
.

5.
Wall-crossing formulas

5.1.
Grothendieck ring of stacks

For more details see e.g.
[
15
,
35
,
66
]
.
In this section we will consider only algebraic (or Artin) stacks locally of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
.
Let
\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}
denote the
2
-category of algebraic stacks of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
having affine stabilizers.
Given an algebraic stack
S
with affine stabilizers, we denote by
K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)
the corresponding Grothendieck group with rational coefficients generated by isomorphism classes
[X\to S]
of objects in
\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S
subject to usual relations
[
15
, §3]
.
We can similarly consider the category
\operatorname{Var}
of finite type algebraic varieties over
\mathbb{C}
and the Grothendieck group
K(\operatorname{Var}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)
with rational coefficients.

In particular, for
S\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}
, the corresponding Grothendieck groups
K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}})\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})

and
K(\operatorname{Var})\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{Var}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})

have a ring structure with the product defined by
[X]\cdot[Y]\mathchar 61\relax[X\times Y]
.
We define
\mathbb{L}\mathchar 61\relax[\mathbb{A}^{1}]
so that
[\operatorname{GL}_{n}]\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{i\mathchar 61\relax 0}^{n-1}(\mathbb{L}^{n}-\mathbb{L}^{i})
.
It is proved in
[
66
]
(see also
[
15
]
) that there is a natural isomorphism

(98)

K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}})\simeq K(\operatorname{Var})[[\operatorname{GL}_{n}]^{-1}\mathchar 58\relax n\geq 1]\\
\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{Var})[(\mathbb{L}-1)^{-1},\mathbb{L}^{-1},[\mathbb{P}^{n}]^{-1}\mathchar 58\relax n\geq 1].

For any algebraic stack
S
with affine stabilizers, the Grothendieck group
K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)
is a module over
K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}})
with the product defined by

(99)

[X]\cdot[Y\to S]\mathchar 61\relax[X\times Y\to Y\to S].

Let us also define

(100)

K^{\circ}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Im}\left(K(\operatorname{Var}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)[\mathbb{L}^{-1},[\mathbb{P}^{n}]^{-1}\mathchar 58\relax n\geq 1]\to K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)\right).

which is a module over
K^{\circ}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}})\mathchar 61\relax K^{\circ}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})
.
Note that the above map is not necessarily injective as
\mathbb{L}-1
can be a zero divisor in
K(\operatorname{Var}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})
, cf.
[
11
,
49
]
.

Given a morphism of stacks
f\colon S\to T
, we define

(101)

f_{*}\colon K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S)\to K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}T),\qquad[X\to S]\mapsto[X\to S\to T].

If
f\colon S\to T
is of finite type, then it induces

(102)

f^{*}\colon K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}T)\to K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}S),\qquad[Y\to T]\mapsto[S\times_{T}Y\to S].

In what follows we will
introduce the square root
\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}
of
\mathbb{L}

and define

(103)

\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})[\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}],\qquad\mathbf{K}^{\circ}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})\mathchar 61\relax K^{\circ}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})[\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}].

By the results of Deligne
[
22
]
, there exists a ring homomorphism

(104)

E\colon K(\operatorname{Var}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{Q}[u,v],\qquad[X]\mapsto\sum_{p,q,n}(-1)^{n}\dim\left(\operatorname{Gr}^{p}_{F}\operatorname{Gr}^{W}_{p+q}H^{n}_{c}(X,\mathbb{C})\right)u^{p}v^{q},

called the
Hodge-Deligne polynomial map
or the
E-polynomial map
.
Note that
H^{*}_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathbb{Q})\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-2]
, hence
E(\mathbb{L})\mathchar 61\relax uv
.
We can extend
E
to

(105)

E\colon\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{Q}(u,\sqrt{uv}),

where
\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto-\sqrt{uv}
.
Similarly, we define the
Poincaré polynomial map

(106)

P\colon K(\operatorname{Var}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{Q}[y],\qquad[X]\mapsto E(X;y,y),

which extends to

P\colon\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{Q}(y)
,
where
\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto-y
.

5.2.
Motivic Hall algebra of an exact category

For more details see e.g.
[
15
,
33
,
24
]
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be an exact category, meaning an additive category equipped with a class of short exact sequences (to be called admissible exact sequences) satisfying Quillen’s axioms
[
59
,
38
]
.
We define admissible monomorphisms and epimorphisms to be respectively monomorphisms and epimorphisms of admissible short exact sequences.
Let us recall the Waldhausen construction of the simplicial groupoid associated with an exact category
\mathcal{E}
.
Let us define
\mathcal{M}_{n}
to be the groupoid of chains

(107)

0\mathchar 61\relax E_{0}\hookrightarrow E_{1}\hookrightarrow\dots\hookrightarrow E_{n},

where
E_{i-1}\hookrightarrow E_{i}
are admissible monomorphisms,
together with choices of cokernels
E_{j/i}
of
E_{i}\hookrightarrow E_{j}
.
Note that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is a point,
\mathcal{M}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{M}_{1}
parametrizes objects of
\mathcal{E}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
parametrizes admissible exact sequences
0\to E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{2/1}\to 0
in
\mathcal{E}
.

Groupoids
\mathcal{M}_{n}
form a simplicial groupoid,
where for any weakly increasing map
\alpha\colon[m]\to[n]\mathchar 61\relax\left\{0,1,\dots,n\right\}
, we define the face map

(108)

\alpha^{*}\colon\mathcal{M}_{n}\to\mathcal{M}_{m},\qquad[E_{0}\hookrightarrow\dots\hookrightarrow E_{n}]\mapsto[E_{\alpha_{0}}\hookrightarrow\dots\hookrightarrow E_{\alpha_{m}}]/E_{\alpha_{0}}.

The fundamental property of this simplicial groupoid is the 2-Segal property
[
24
]
.
We will identify increasing maps
\alpha\colon[m]\to[n]
with subsets
\left\{\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{m}\right\}\subset[n]
and we will denote
\alpha^{*}
by
p_{\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{m}}
.
In particular, we consider

(109)

\displaystyle q\mathchar 61\relax(p_{01},p_{12})\colon\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M},\qquad[E_{1}\hookrightarrow E_{2}]\mapsto(E_{1},E_{2/1}),

(110)

\displaystyle p\mathchar 61\relax p_{02}\colon\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{M},\qquad[E_{1}\hookrightarrow E_{2}]\mapsto E_{2}.

Let us assume that every
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is equipped with a structure of an
algebraic stack locally of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
with affine stabilizers,
so that all face maps are morphisms of algebraic stacks and
the map
q\colon\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
is of finite type.
We define the
motivic Hall algebra

(111)

H(\mathcal{E})\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M})

with the product given by

(112)

K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M})\otimes K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M})\to K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M}^{2})\xrightarrow{q^{*}}K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M}_{2})\xrightarrow{p_{*}}K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M}),

The associativity is a consequence of the 2-Segal property.

Remark 5.1
.

It is actually enough to equip groupoids
\mathcal{M}_{n}
with a slightly weaker structure than an algebraic stack structure.
Given a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, let us consider the set of pairs
(X,f)
, where
X\in\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}
and
f\colon X(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{G}
is an equivalence of categories.
We will say that two such pairs
(X_{1},f_{1})
and
(X_{2},f_{2})
are equivalent if there exist morphisms
g_{i}\colon Z\to X_{i}
in
\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}
such that the functors
Z(\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{g_{i}(\mathbb{C})}X_{i}(\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{f_{i}}\mathcal{G}
for
i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2
are naturally isomorphic.
Note that morphisms
g_{i}\colon Z\to X_{i}
are geometric bijections, meaning that
g_{i}\colon Z(\mathbb{C})\to X_{i}(\mathbb{C})
are equivalences of categories.
Therefore there exist finite stratifications of
Z
and
X_{i}
such that
g_{i}
induce isomorphisms between the strata
[
15
, Lemma 3.2]
.
This implies that
g_{i}
induce isomorphisms
g_{i*}\colon K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}Z)\to K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}X_{i})
, hence we obtain an isomorphism
K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}X_{1})\simeq K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}X_{2})
.
We will call an equivalence class of above pairs an
atlas
of
\mathcal{G}
.
In what follows, we will have a linear map
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{E})\to\Gamma
and a decomposition
\mathcal{M}\mathchar 61\relax\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
, where
\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
is the groupoid parameterizing objects
E\in\mathcal{E}
with
\operatorname{cl}E\mathchar 61\relax\gamma
.
For our considerations it will be enough to equip every groupoid
\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
(as well as the fibers of
\mathcal{M}_{n}\xrightarrow{(p_{i-1,i})_{i}}\mathcal{M}^{n}\to\Gamma^{n}
)
with an atlas.

5.3.
Wall-crossing in the Hall algebra

In this section we will restrict our considerations to the triangulated category
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
, where
X
is a smooth projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
, although most of the results can be formulated for other triangulated categories.
By the results of
[
47
]
, the
stack
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
parameterizing objects
E\in\mathcal{D}
with
\operatorname{Hom}^{<0}(E,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
is algebraic and locally of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
.
More precisely, we consider the
2
-functor

(113)

\bar{}\mathcal{M}\colon\operatorname{Sch}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C}\to\operatorname{Grpd}

that sends a scheme
S
to the groupoid of objects
E\in D^{b}(X\times S)
that are relatively perfect
[
47
]
and satisfy
\operatorname{Hom}^{<0}(E_{s},E_{s})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
s\in S
.
Then
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
is an algebraic stack locally of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 5.2
.

The stack
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
has affine stabilizers.
Indeed, the stabilizer of any object
E
can be identified with the group
\operatorname{Aut}(E)
which is the group of invertible elements of the finite-dimensional algebra
A\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(E,E)
.
Let us consider an injective morphism of algebras
A\hookrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(A)
given by the left multiplication.
Then the group of invertible elements of
A
can be identified with
A\cap\operatorname{GL}(A)
, which is a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}(A)
.

We consider the numerical Grothendieck group
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
and the projection
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{D})\to\Gamma
.
Let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
be a stability condition on
\mathcal{D}
(with respect to
\operatorname{cl}
).
For any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
of length
\leq 1
(we assume that
I
is non-closed if it has length
1
),
let
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}
be the substack of
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
parameterizing objects in
\mathcal{P}_{I}
.
We will assume that

Assumption 1
.

For
I
of length
1
, the substack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}
is open in
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
.

Assumption 2
.

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
Z(\gamma)\neq 0
,
the stack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
is of finite type,
where
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
is the stack of
\sigma
-semistable objects in
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
having class
\gamma
and phase
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z(\gamma)
.

Note that the first assumption implies that for any interval
I
of length
<1
, the substack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}
is open in
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
.
In particular, the stack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,\phi}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,[\phi,\phi]}
,
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
, is open in
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
.
The stack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
is the substack of
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,\phi}
for
\phi\mathchar 61\relax\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z(\gamma)
, hence it is also open in
\bar{}\mathcal{M}
.
The second assumption implies that, for any interval
I
of length
<1
, the stack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}(\gamma)
of objects in
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}
having class
\gamma
is of finite type.
The above assumptions are proved for a large class of stability conditions on surfaces in
[
64
]
and on 3-folds in
[
57
]
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval of length
<1
and

(114)

V\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi I}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.re^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi}\ \right|r>0,\,\phi\in I\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}

be the corresponding strict cone.
The category
\mathcal{E}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{I}
is quasi-abelian
[
13
]
.
In particular, it is automatically exact, with the class of admissible exact sequences consisting of all short exact sequences.
By our assumptions, its objects are parametrized by the algebraic stack
\mathcal{M}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}
.
The stacks
\mathcal{M}_{n}
parameterizing chains (
107
) in
\mathcal{E}
are also algebraic.
For example, the fiber of
q\colon\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
over
(E_{1},E_{2})
can be identified with the stack

(115)

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{2},E_{1})/\operatorname{Hom}(E_{2},E_{1})

and we can stratify
\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
so that
q
is a trivial fibration over the strata (cf.
[
15
, Prop. 6.2]
).
This also implies that
q
is of finite type.

We consider the decomposition
\mathcal{M}\mathchar 61\relax\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
,
where
\mathcal{M}(\gamma)\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}(\gamma)
is the stack parameterizing objects
E\in\mathcal{E}
with
\operatorname{cl}E\mathchar 61\relax\gamma
.
By the support property of
\sigma
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\operatorname{cl}E\right\rVert
for any
\sigma
-semistable object
E
.
Therefore, for any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{E}
we have

(116)

\operatorname{cl}E\in S\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax S(V,Z,\varepsilon),

where
S(V,Z,\varepsilon)
is the strict semigroup defined in (
11
).
Let
S_{0}\mathchar 61\relax S\cup\left\{0\right\}
.
Then the Hall algebra
H(\mathcal{E})\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}/\mathcal{M})
is
S_{0}
-graded

(117)

H(\mathcal{E})\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S_{0}}H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E}),\qquad H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E})\mathchar 61\relax K(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathcal{M}(\gamma)).

We define the completion of the Hall algebra to be

(118)

\hat{H}(\mathcal{E})\mathchar 61\relax\varprojlim_{J\subset S}H(\mathcal{E})_{J},\qquad H(\mathcal{E})_{J}\mathchar 61\relax H(\mathcal{E})/\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S\backslash J}H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\bigoplus_{\gamma\in J\cup\left\{0\right\}}H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E}),

where the limit is taken over all finite lower sets
J\subset S
.
Note that
\hat{H}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\prod_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E})
as a vector space.
Let us define

(119)

\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,V}\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in V}\mathbbm{1}_{\gamma}\in\hat{H}(\mathcal{E}),\qquad\mathbbm{1}_{\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax[\mathcal{M}(\gamma)\to\mathcal{M}(\gamma)]\in H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E}).

On the other hand, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma\cap Z^{-1}(V)
, we can consider
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
as an open substack of
\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
.
We define

(120)

\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in\ell}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\gamma}\in\hat{H}(\mathcal{E}),\qquad\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)\to\mathcal{M}(\gamma)]\in H_{\gamma}(\mathcal{E}),

for any ray
\ell\subset V
.
The following result is well-known
[
33
,
41
]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

We have

(121)

\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,V}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell}.

Proof.

Given
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in S\subset\Gamma
, we have
Z(\gamma_{i})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi_{i}}
for some
\phi_{i}\in I
,
i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2
.
We will write
\gamma_{1}\succ\gamma_{2}
if
\phi_{1}>\phi_{2}
.
By the HN-property of
\sigma
, for any
E\in\mathcal{E}
, there exists a unique filtration

0\mathchar 61\relax E_{0}\subset\dots\subset E_{n}\mathchar 61\relax E

such that
E_{i}/E_{i-1}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi_{i}}
with
\phi_{i}\in I
satisfying
\phi_{1}>\dots>\phi_{n}
.
We define the
\sigma
-HN type of
E
to be the tuple
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
, where
\gamma_{i}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cl}(E_{i}/E_{i-1})\in S

and
\gamma_{1}\succ\gamma_{2}\succ\dots\succ\gamma_{n}
.
By Lemma
2.5
, for any fixed
\gamma\in S
, there exist finitely many tuples
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
in
S
such that
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}
.
This implies that
\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
has a finite stratification with strata
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
parameterizing objects in
\mathcal{M}(\gamma)
having
\sigma
-HN type
(\gamma_{1},\dots,,\gamma_{n})
.
By the uniqueness of the HN-filtration we have

[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\to\mathcal{M}]\mathchar 61\relax[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma_{1})\to\mathcal{M}]*\dots*[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma_{n})\to\mathcal{M}].

This implies that

[\mathcal{M}(\gamma)\to\mathcal{M}]\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{n}\mathchar 61\relax\gamma\\
\gamma_{1}\succ\dots\succ\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}}[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma_{1})\to\mathcal{M}]*\dots*[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma_{n})\to\mathcal{M}]

which is a reformulation of the required formula.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Note that it is important in the above theorem that the interval
I
has length
<1
.
If
I
has length
1
, for example
I\mathchar 61\relax(0,1]
, then the corresponding semigroup
S(V,Z,\varepsilon)
is not necessarily strict and the ordered product in the theorem is not necessarily well-defined.

5.4.
Wall-crossing in the quantum torus

We use the same notation and assumptions as in the previous section as well as

Assumption 3
.

The stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
has global dimension
\leq 2
.

Let
\chi
be the Euler form on
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
and let
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
be its anti-symmetrization

(122)

\left\langle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\right\rangle\mathchar 61\relax\chi(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})-\chi(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}).

We define the algebra over
R\mathchar 61\relax\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})
(
103
)

(123)

\mathbb{T}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}Rx^{\gamma},\qquad x^{\gamma_{1}}*x^{\gamma_{2}}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\right\rangle}\cdot x^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}},

called the
quantum torus
.
For a strict semigroup
S\mathchar 61\relax S(V,Z,\varepsilon)\subset\Gamma
, we consider the subalgebra

(124)

\mathbb{T}_{S}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S\cup\left\{0\right\}}Rx^{\gamma}

and define its completion
\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}
similarly to
(
118
).
We define the
integration map

(125)

\mathcal{I}\colon H(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{T}_{S},\qquad[X\to\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}(\gamma)]\mapsto\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}[X]\cdot x^{\gamma}

which extends to

\mathcal{I}\colon\hat{H}(\mathcal{E})\to\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}
.
Note that

(126)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,V})\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in V}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,I}(\gamma)]x^{\gamma},

(127)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell})\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in\ell}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)]x^{\gamma}.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2}\in\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}
and let
X_{i}\to\mathcal{M}
,
i\mathchar 61\relax 1,2
, be morphisms such that for arbitrary
x_{i}\in X_{i}
and the corresponding objects
E_{i}\in\mathcal{E}
, we have
\phi^{-}_{\sigma}(E_{1})>\phi^{+}_{\sigma}(E_{2})
.
Then

(128)

\mathcal{I}([X_{1}\to\mathcal{M}]*[X_{2}\to\mathcal{M}])\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{I}([X_{1}\to\mathcal{M}])*\mathcal{I}([X_{2}\to\mathcal{M}]).

Proof.

We can stratify
X_{i}
and assume that they are of the from
Y_{i}/\operatorname{GL}_{n_{i}}
, where
Y_{i}
are algebraic varieties.
Furthermore, we can substitute
X_{i}\to\mathcal{M}
by
Y_{i}\to\mathcal{M}
and divide both sides of the equation by

[\operatorname{GL}_{n_{1}}]\cdot[\operatorname{GL}_{n_{2}}]
.
Therefore we can assume that
X_{i}
are algebraic varieties.
We can also assume that
X_{i}
is mapped to
\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{i})
for some
\gamma_{i}\in S
. Let
\gamma\mathchar 61\relax\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}
.
Expression on the left is given by the motivic class of the Cartesian product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}

multiplied by
\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}x^{\gamma}
.
The fiber of
\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
over
(E_{1},E_{2})
is given by the stack

\operatorname{Hom}^{1}(E_{2},E_{1})/\operatorname{Hom}(E_{2},E_{1}).

For any
k\geq 2
, we have
\phi_{\sigma}^{-}(E_{1}[k])-\phi^{+}_{\sigma}(E_{2})>k\geq 2
,
hence
\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{2},E_{1})\mathchar 61\relax 0
by the assumption that
\sigma
has global dimension
\leq 2
.
We conclude that the motivic class of the fiber is equal to
\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1})}
.
We note that

{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)-\chi(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1})\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1})+{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{2})+{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\right\rangle

which is exactly the power of
\mathbb{L}
on the right hand side of the required equation.
∎

As in Theorem
5.3

we will write
\gamma_{1}\succ\gamma_{2}
if

Z(\gamma_{i})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi_{i}}
for
\phi_{i}\in I
satisfying
\phi_{1}>\phi_{2}
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
\gamma_{1}\succ\dots\succ\gamma_{n}
be elements in
S(V,Z,\varepsilon)
.
Then

(129)

\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\gamma_{1}}*\dots*\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\gamma_{n}})\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\gamma_{1}})*\dots*\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\gamma_{n}}).

Corollary 5.7
.

We have

(130)

\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,V})\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell}).

Proof.

By Theorem
5.3
we have

\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,V})\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{I}\left(\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell}\right)

and by the previous result we have

\mathcal{I}\left(\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell}\right)\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\ell\subset V}^{\to}\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell})
.
∎

5.5.
Stability data associated to a stability condition

Let us consider the
\Gamma
-graded Lie algebra

(131)

\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}Rx^{\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{T},\qquad R\mathchar 61\relax\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C}),

with the Lie bracket given by the commutator of
\mathbb{T}
(
123
).
For any strict semigroup
S\subset\Gamma
without zero,
we consider the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{S}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in S}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
and the corresponding pro-nilpotent Lie algebra
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{S}
and the pro-nilpotent group
\hat{G}_{S}
.
Note that we can identify
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{S}

with the ideal
\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}^{+}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\gamma\in S}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
of
\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\gamma\in S\cup\left\{0\right\}}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
and identify
\hat{G}_{S}
with the group
1+\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}^{+}\subset\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}

so that we have a commutative diagram

(132)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{G}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

Let
\varepsilon>0
be such that
\left\lvert Z(E)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\operatorname{cl}E\right\rVert
for any
\sigma
-semistable object
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
.
For any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
, we consider the strict semigroup
S(\ell,Z,\varepsilon)
and define

(133)

\displaystyle A_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma,\ell})\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in\ell}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)]x^{\gamma}\in 1+\hat{}\mathbb{T}_{S(\ell,Z,\varepsilon)}^{+}\simeq\hat{G}_{S(\ell,Z,\varepsilon)}\subset\hat{G}_{\ell,Z},

(134)

\displaystyle a_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{Z(\gamma)\in\ell}a_{\sigma,\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax\log(A_{\sigma,\ell})\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{S(\ell,Z,\varepsilon)}\subset\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z},

where
\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z}
is the ind-pro-nilpotent Lie algebra defined in (
64
) and

\hat{G}_{\ell,Z}
is the corresponding ind-pro-nilpotent group.
We define
stability data associated to the stability condition
\sigma
to be the family

(135)

a_{\sigma}\mathchar 61\relax(a_{\sigma,\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}.

Remark 5.8
.

In the above discussion we considered the quantum torus (
123
) over the algebra
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})
and constructed stability data
a_{\sigma}
with values in
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\mathbb{C})
.
Alternatively, we can consider the quantum torus over the algebra
\mathbb{Q}(u,\sqrt{uv})
and apply the Hodge-Deligne (polynomial) map (
104
) to construct stability data
E(a_{\sigma})
with values in
\mathbb{Q}(u,\sqrt{uv})
.
Similarly, we can consider the quantum torus over the algebra
\mathbb{Q}(y)
and apply the Poincaré (polynomial) map (
106
) to construct stability data
P(a_{\sigma})
with values in
\mathbb{Q}(y)
.

5.6.
Families of stability data and stability conditions

Let
M
be a topological space and
(\sigma_{x}\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{x},\mathcal{P}_{x}))_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
satisfying assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
.
For every
x\in M
, we define stability data

(136)

a_{x}\mathchar 61\relax(a_{\sigma_{x},\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}

as in the previous section.

Theorem 5.9
.

The family of stability data
(Z_{x},a_{x})_{x\in M}
is continuous.

Proof.

Axiom (
1
) follows from Lemma
3.7
.
Let us verify axiom (
2
).
Given a strict cone
V\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi I}

(where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval of length
<1
),
we have by Corollary
5.7

\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_{y},V})\mathchar 61\relax\prod^{\to}_{\ell\subset V}\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_{y},\ell})\mathchar 61\relax\prod^{\to}_{\ell\subset V}A_{y,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax A_{y,V}.

Therefore we need to show that components of

\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_{y},V})
are constant in a neighborhood of a fixed
x\in M
.
The component of
\mathcal{I}(\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_{y},V})
at
\gamma\in\Gamma
is
equal to
[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma_{y},I}(\gamma)]
(up to some factor depending on
\gamma
).
We will compare it to
[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma_{x}}(\gamma)]
.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
and an open set
x\subset U\subset M
such that

\left\lvert Z_{y}(E)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\operatorname{cl}E\right\rVert
for all
\sigma_{y}
-semistable objects
E
and
y\in U
.
We can assume that
\left\lVert Z_{x}-Z_{y}\right\rVert\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}
for all
y\in U
.
This implies that if
\left\lvert Z_{y}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
,
then
\left\lvert Z_{x}(\gamma)\right\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert
.
Therefore
S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z_{x},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
.
If
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma_{y},I}(\gamma)
is non-empty, then

\gamma\in S(V,Z_{y},\varepsilon)\subset S(V,Z_{x},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
.

Let us fix
\gamma\in S(V,Z_{x},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
and let

Z_{x}(\gamma)\in\ell_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\phi_{0}}
for some
\phi_{0}\in I
.
By Lemma
4.8
it is enough to consider open cones
V
around
\ell_{0}
of arbitrarily small angle.
For any
0<\eta<{\frac{1}{2}}
, let
I_{\eta}\mathchar 61\relax(\phi_{0}-\eta,\phi_{0}+\eta)

and
V_{\eta}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}_{>0}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi I_{\eta}}
.
We can assume that for any
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma
in
S(V_{\eta},Z_{x},\varepsilon/2)
, we have
Z_{x}(\gamma^{\prime})\in\ell_{0}
.
We can also assume that
d(\mathcal{P}_{x},\mathcal{P}_{y})<\eta/2
for all
y\in U
.

Let
I\mathchar 61\relax I_{\eta/2}
and
V\mathchar 61\relax V_{\eta/2}
.
If
E\in\mathcal{P}_{y,I}
, then
\phi_{y}^{\pm}(E)\in I
, hence

\phi_{x}^{\pm}(E)\in I_{\eta}
.
If
\operatorname{cl}E\mathchar 61\relax\gamma
, then by our assumption, for any
\sigma_{x}
-HN-factor
F
of
E
, we have
Z_{x}(\operatorname{cl}F)\in\ell_{0}
,
hence
E
is
\sigma_{x}
-semistable and
E\in\mathcal{P}_{x,\phi_{0}}
.
Conversely,
if
E\in\mathcal{P}_{x,\phi_{0}}
, then
\phi_{y}^{\pm}(E)\in I
,
hence
E\in\mathcal{P}_{y,I}
.
This proves that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma_{y},I}(\gamma)\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{M}_{\sigma_{x}}(\gamma)
.
The same can be done for all
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma
in
S(V_{\eta},Z_{x},\varepsilon/2)
.
Taking the logarithm
\log(A_{y,V})
, we obtain that its
\gamma
-component is constant for
y\in U
.
∎

6.
Stability conditions on surfaces

6.1.
Some conventions

6.1.1.
Numerical intersection ring

Let
X
be a smooth projective (connected) variety over
\mathbb{C}
of dimension
d
.
Let
A^{*}(X)
be its intersection ring (also called the Chow ring)
[
26
, §8.3]
.
It is a graded commutative ring with the involution

(137)

A^{*}(X)\to A^{*}(X),\qquad a\mapsto a^{*}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}a_{k},

where
a_{k}\in A^{k}(X)
denotes the
k
-th component of
a\in A^{*}(X)
.
We consider the
degree map

(138)

\int\colon A^{d}(X)\mathchar 61\relax A_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{Z},\qquad\sum_{x}n_{x}[x]\mapsto\sum_{x}n_{x},

and extend it to
\int\colon A^{*}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}
.
We define the
intersection form
on
A^{*}(X)

(139)

(a,b)\mathchar 61\relax-\int a^{*}b.

It satisfies

(140)

(a,b)\mathchar 61\relax(-1)^{d}(b,a).

Let
A_{\mathrm{num}}^{*}(X)\subset A^{*}(X)
be the kernel of this form,
having components

(141)

A_{\mathrm{num}}^{k}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\Big.a\in A^{k}(X)\Big.\ \right|\int ab\mathchar 61\relax 0\ \forall b\in A^{d-k}(X)\right\}.

It is an ideal of
A^{*}(X)
.
We define the
numerical intersection ring

(142)

N^{*}(X)\mathchar 61\relax A^{*}(X)/A_{\mathrm{num}}^{*}(X).

The above intersection form induces a non-degenerate form on
N^{*}(X)
.
Every component
N^{k}(X)
is a free abelian group of finite rank, as it can be embedded into the torsion free part of
H^{2k}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.
We have
N^{0}(X)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and
N^{d}(X)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
.
The group
N^{1}(X)
is called the (torsion free)
Néron-Severi group
.
The elements of
N^{1}(X)
will be called divisors and the elements of
N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\mathchar 61\relax N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
will be called
\mathbb{R}
-divisors.
For
a\in N^{k}(X)
and
b\in N^{d-k}(X)
, we will interpret
ab\in N^{d}(X)
as an integer, using the isomorphism
N^{d}(X)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
.
We also define
N_{k}(X)\mathchar 61\relax N^{d-k}(X)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The involution (
137
) produces adjoint operators, namely

(143)

(ab,c)\mathchar 61\relax(b,a^{*}c),\qquad a,b,c\in N^{*}(X).

Remark 6.1
.

The pairing
\sigma(a,b)\mathchar 61\relax\int ab
on
N^{*}(X)
makes
N^{*}(X)
a (graded) symmetric Frobenius algebra, meaning that
\sigma
is symmetric, non-degenerate and
\sigma(ab,c)\mathchar 61\relax\sigma(a,bc)
for all
a,b,c\in N^{*}(X)
.
However, we will not use this pairing.

If
X
is a surface, then we can write the intersection form on
N^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
as

(144)

(a,b)\mathchar 61\relax a_{1}b_{1}-a_{0}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{0},\qquad a,b\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(X).

This intersection form has signature
(2,\rho)
,
where
\rho\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{rk}N^{1}(X)
is the Picard number of
X
,
because of the following result.

Theorem 6.2

(Hodge index theorem)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface.
Then the intersection form on
N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
has signature
(1,\rho-1)
, where
\rho\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{rk}N^{1}(X)
.
In particular, for any ample divisor
H
, the intersection form restricted to
H^{\perp}\subset N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
is negative-definite.

6.1.2.
Numerical Grothendieck group

Let
K(X)\mathchar 61\relax K_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
be the Grothendieck group of coherent sheaves on
X
.
It is a commutative ring with an involution, where

(145)

[E]\cdot[F]\mathchar 61\relax[E\otimes F],\qquad[E]^{*}\mathchar 61\relax[E^{\vee}],

for
E,F\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
with
E
locally-free.
The Chern character map
\operatorname{ch}\colon K(X)\to A^{*}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a ring homomorphism preserving involutions.
It induces an isomorphism
[
26
, §18.3]

(146)

\operatorname{ch}\colon K(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}\to A^{*}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Let
\chi
be the Euler form on
K(X)
, defined by

(147)

\chi([E],[F])\mathchar 61\relax\chi(E,F)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{k}(E,F).

By Serre duality, we have
\chi(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax(-1)^{d}\chi(F,E\otimes\omega_{X})
,
where
\omega_{X}
is the canonical bundle of
X
.
This implies that the left and the right kernels of the Euler form coincide.
The kernel
K_{\mathrm{num}}(X)\subset K(X)

is an ideal of the ring
K(X)
and we define the

numerical Grothendieck group
(which is a ring)

(148)

\mathcal{N}(X)\mathchar 61\relax K(X)/K_{\mathrm{num}}(X).

By the Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch theorem, we have

(149)

\chi([E],[F])\mathchar 61\relax-(\operatorname{ch}E,\operatorname{ch}F\cdot\operatorname{Td}(X)),

where the Todd class

\operatorname{Td}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{td}(T_{X})\in A^{*}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is an invertible element.
The Chern character induces the ring homomorphism

(150)

\operatorname{ch}\colon\mathcal{N}(X)\to N^{*}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}

which becomes an isomorphism after tensoring with
\mathbb{Q}
.

6.1.3.
Ample cone

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety.
We define the
ample cone

\operatorname{Amp}(X)\subset N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
to be the convex (blunt) cone generated by ample classes in
N^{1}(X)
.
We define the
cone of curves

\operatorname{NE}(X)\subset N_{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}

(151)

\operatorname{NE}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.\sum\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[C_{i}]\ \right|C_{i}\subset X\text{ is an integral curve}\right\}

and let
\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X)
be its closure.
Then

(152)

\operatorname{Amp}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.D\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\ \right|DC>0\ \forall C\in\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X)\backslash\left\{0\right\}\right\}.

We define the
nef cone
to be the dual of
\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X)

(153)

\operatorname{Nef}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.D\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\ \right|DC\geq 0\ \forall C\in\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X)\right\}.

It is equal to the closure of
\operatorname{Amp}(X)
, while
\operatorname{Amp}(X)
is equal to the interior of
\operatorname{Nef}(X)
(see e.g.
[
45
]
).
By the Nakai–Moishezon criterion,
if
X
is a surface, then
D\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
is ample if and only if
D^{2}>0
and
DC>0
for all curves
C\subset X
.

6.2.
Construction of stability conditions

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
be its bounded derived category.
We consider the numerical Grothendieck group
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
and the projection
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{D})\to\Gamma
.
For any ample divisor
\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
and a torsion-free sheaf
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
, we define the
slope

(154)

\mu_{\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{c_{1}(E)\cdot\omega}{\operatorname{rk}(E)}.

We say that a torsion-free sheaf
E
is
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable
if, for any subsheaf
0\neq E^{\prime}\subset E
,
we have
\mu_{\omega}(E^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\omega}(E)
.
Then every
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
has a unique filtration (called the
Harder-Narasimhan filtration
)

E_{0}\subset E_{1}\subset\dots\subset E_{n}\mathchar 61\relax E,

such that
E_{0}
is the torsion part of
E

and
E_{i}/E_{i-1}
are
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable and
have decreasing slopes.
We define

(155)

\mu^{+}_{\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\begin{cases}+\infty&E_{0}\neq 0,\\
\mu_{\omega}(E_{1})&E_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 0,\end{cases}\qquad\mu^{-}_{\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\mu_{\omega}(E_{n}/E_{n-1}).

Remark 6.3
.

The above discussion can be interpreted in the framework of §
3.4
by considering the linear map

(156)

Z\colon\mathcal{N}(X)\to\mathbb{C},\qquad E\mapsto-c_{1}(E)\cdot\omega+\mathbf{i}\operatorname{rk}(E).

Note, however, that
Z(\mathcal{O}_{x})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
x\in X
, so that
Z
is not a stability function on
\operatorname{Coh}(X)
.

Let us define the
discriminant

\Delta
to be the quadratic form on
\mathcal{N}(X)
corresponding to the intersection form, meaning that

(157)

\Delta(E)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax(\operatorname{ch}(E),\operatorname{ch}(E))\mathchar 61\relax a_{1}^{2}-2ra_{2},\qquad\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2}).

Recall that it has signature
(2,\rho)
,
where
\rho\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{rk}N^{1}(X)
.

Theorem 6.4

(Bogomolov inequality)
.

Let
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
be a
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable sheaf.
Then

\Delta(E)\geq 0.

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
let
\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,>s}
be the subcategory of
\operatorname{Coh}(X)

consisting of
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
with
\mu^{-}_{\omega}(E)>s

and let
\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\leq s}
be the subcategory of
\operatorname{Coh}(X)
consisting of
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
with
\mu_{\omega}^{+}(E)\leq s
.
Then
(\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,>s},\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\leq s})
is a torsion pair on
\operatorname{Coh}(X)
and we define the category

(158)

\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\#s}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\leq s}[1],\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,>s}\right\rangle\subset\mathcal{D}

which is the heart of a bounded t-structure on
\mathcal{D}
.
In particular, for any
\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
, we define

(159)

\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\#\beta\omega}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\leq\beta\omega}[1],\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,>\beta\omega}\right\rangle.

The following result was formulated in
[
2
, §2]
, where it was proved
that the linear map
Z_{\beta,\omega}
maps
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
to the upper half-plane.
The support property and the HN property were proved in

[
65
]
(see also
[
6
]
).
This result is a generalization of

[
14
, Lemma 6.2]
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
and let
\omega
be ample.
Then the linear map

(160)

Z_{\beta,\omega}\colon\mathcal{N}(X)\to\mathbb{C},\qquad Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega},\operatorname{ch}(E))\mathchar 61\relax-\int e^{-\beta-\mathbf{i}\omega}\operatorname{ch}(E)

is a stability function on the abelian category
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
.

We denote by
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
the stability condition corresponding to
(Z_{\beta,\omega},\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega})
.
If
\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
, then

(161)

Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\left(\frac{r}{2}(\omega^{2}-\beta^{2})+a_{1}\beta-a_{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}\left(a_{1}-r\beta\right)\omega.

We can also write this expression in the form

(162)

Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2r}\left(r^{2}\omega^{2}+\Delta(E)-(a_{1}-r\beta)^{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}\left(a_{1}-r\beta\right)\omega&r\neq 0,\\
(a_{1}\beta-a_{2})+\mathbf{i}a_{1}\omega&r\mathchar 61\relax 0.\end{cases}

It is convenient to define

(163)

\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax e^{-\beta}\operatorname{ch}(E)\in N^{*}(X).

If
\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,b_{1},b_{2})
, then

(164)

Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(\tfrac{1}{2}r\omega^{2}-b_{2})+\mathbf{i}b_{1}\omega.

Lemma 6.6
.

The discriminant
\Delta\colon\mathcal{N}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z_{\beta,\omega}
.

Proof.

Let
E
be such that
Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
and let
\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,b_{1},b_{2})
.
Then
b_{1}\omega\mathchar 61\relax 0
and
b_{2}\mathchar 61\relax\tfrac{1}{2}r\omega^{2}
, hence
b_{1}^{2}\leq 0
.
If
(r,b_{1},b_{2})\neq 0
, then either
b_{1}^{2}<0
or
r\neq 0
.
Therefore

\Delta(E)\mathchar 61\relax b_{1}^{2}-2rb_{2}\mathchar 61\relax b_{1}^{2}-r^{2}\omega^{2}<0.

∎

6.3.
Cone of positive planes

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface as before.
We will say that a subspace
V\subset N^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
is a positive plane if
\dim V\mathchar 61\relax 2
and the intersection form restricted to
V
is positive-definite.
We define

(165)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(X)\mathchar 61\relax

\displaystyle\left\{\left.x+\mathbf{i}y\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)\ \right|\left\langle x,y\right\rangle\text{ is a positive plane}\right\},

(166)

\displaystyle\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)\mathchar 61\relax

\displaystyle\left\{\left.e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)\ \right|\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X),\,\omega^{2}>0\right\},

(167)

\displaystyle\bar{}\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\mathchar 61\relax
