\displaystyle\left\{\left.e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)\ \right|\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X),\,\omega\in\operatorname{Amp}(X)\right\}.

Lemma 6.7

(Cf.
[
14
]
)
.

We have
\bar{}\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\subset\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
and

(168)

\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\left\{\left.u\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)\ \right|u_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 1,\,(u,u)\mathchar 61\relax 0,\,(u,\bar{u})>0\right\}.

The action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathcal{P}(X)
is free and
\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(X)/\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
is a bijection.

Proof.

For
u\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
to be of the form
u\mathchar 61\relax e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}
,
we require that
u_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 1
and
u_{2}\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}u_{1}^{2}
.
If
u_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 1
, then
(u,u)\mathchar 61\relax u_{1}^{2}-2u_{2}
.
Therefore we require
u_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 1
and
(u,u)\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
If
u\mathchar 61\relax e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}
, then

(u,u)\mathchar 61\relax-\int u^{*}u\mathchar 61\relax 0,\qquad(u,\bar{u})\mathchar 61\relax-\int e^{-2\mathbf{i}\omega}\mathchar 61\relax 2\omega^{2}.

This implies that
\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)
is given by (
168
).
If
u\mathchar 61\relax x+\mathbf{i}y\mathchar 61\relax e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}
and
\omega^{2}>0
,
then

(169)

(x,x)\mathchar 61\relax(y,y)\mathchar 61\relax\omega^{2}>0,\qquad(x,y)\mathchar 61\relax 0,

hence the vectors
x,y
span a positive plane and

\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
.

If
u\mathchar 61\relax x+\mathbf{i}y\in\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)
and
g\mathchar 61\relax\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
are such that
gu\in\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)
, then
g\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathchar 61\relax\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, hence
a\mathchar 61\relax 1
,
c\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
Therefore
gu\mathchar 61\relax(x+by)+idy
.
We conclude from (
169
) that
1+b^{2}\mathchar 61\relax d^{2}
and
bd\mathchar 61\relax 0
, hence
b\mathchar 61\relax 0
and
d\mathchar 61\relax\pm 1
.
As
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, we conclude that
g
is the identity.
This proves that the map
\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(X)/\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
is injective.

If
u\mathchar 61\relax x+\mathbf{i}y\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, then by applying an element of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
we can assume that

(x,y)\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
If
x_{0}\mathchar 61\relax y_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 0
, then
(x,x)\mathchar 61\relax x_{1}^{2}>0
,
(y,y)\mathchar 61\relax y_{1}^{2}>0
and
(x,y)\mathchar 61\relax x_{1}y_{1}\mathchar 61\relax 0
,
which would imply that
N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
contains a positive plane.
This is impossible, hence
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{0}\\
y_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq 0
and there exists a matrix in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cdot\operatorname{SO}(2)
that sends this vector to
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Therefore we can assume that
(x,y)\mathchar 61\relax 0
,
x_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 1
,
y_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
By applying a positive scalar to
y
, we can make
(x,x)\mathchar 61\relax(y,y)
.
Then
x+\mathbf{i}y\in\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)
.
∎

Let us define

(170)

\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\cdot\bar{}\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}(X)\subset N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X).

Note that we have the quotient maps
\mathcal{P}(X)\to\bar{}\mathcal{P}(X)
and
\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\to\bar{}\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)
and the subset
\bar{}\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)
is open, hence the subset
\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
is open.

6.4.
Geometric stability conditions

In this section we will give a characterization of stability conditions that are equal to
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
up to the action of
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
X
be a smooth projective surface,
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
We consider
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
and the projection
\operatorname{cl}\colon K(\mathcal{D})\to\Gamma
.
We will say that a stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
on
\mathcal{D}
is
geometric
if the skyscraper sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{x}
are stable and have the same phase for all
x\in X
.
By rotating the stability condition (
45
), we can assume that all
\mathcal{O}_{x}
have phase
1
.
We will say that a stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
on
\mathcal{D}
is
special
if the discriminant
\Delta
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
(\sigma_{x})_{x\in M}
be a continuous family of stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
.
Then the subsets

\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\sigma_{x}\text{ is geometric}\right\},\qquad\left\{\left.x\in M\ \right|\sigma_{x}\text{ is special}\right\}

are open in
M
.

Proof.

The statement about special stability conditions is clear.
The proof of the statement about geometric stability repeats the arguments of Lemma
3.9
.
∎

Lemma 6.9

(See
[
14
, Lemma 10.1]
)
.

Let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
be a stability condition on
X
such that all sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{x}
are stable of phase
1
, and let
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{(0,1]}
be its heart.
Then

(1)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}
, then
H^{i}(E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
i\neq 0,-1
and
H^{-1}(E)
is torsion free.

(2)

If
E\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
is stable, then either
E\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}_{x}
for some
x\in X
or
E[-1]
is a locally-free sheaf.

(3)

If
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
, then
E\in\mathcal{P}_{(-1,1]}
and if
E
is a torsion sheaf, then
E\in\mathcal{A}
.

(4)

The pair of categories

\mathcal{T}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}(X)\cap\mathcal{A},\qquad\mathcal{F}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}(X)\cap(\mathcal{A}[-1])

is a torsion pair in
\operatorname{Coh}(X)
and
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\mathcal{F}[1],\mathcal{T}\right\rangle
.

Theorem 6.10

(Cf.
[
14
, Prop. 10.3]
)
.

Let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
be a special geometric stability condition.
Then there exist unique
g\in\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
,
\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
and
\omega\in\operatorname{Amp}(X)
such that
\sigma[g]\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
.

Proof.

There exists a unique
u\mathchar 61\relax x+\mathbf{i}y\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
such that

Z(E)\mathchar 61\relax(u,\operatorname{ch}E)
for all
E\in\mathcal{D}
.
The condition that
Z
is special means that
\left\langle x,y\right\rangle
is a positive plane §
6.3
.
By Lemma
6.7
there exist unique
T\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
and
\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
with
\omega^{2}>0
such that

T^{-1}u\mathchar 61\relax e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}
.
We have
T^{-1}Z(\mathcal{O}_{p})\mathchar 61\relax(e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega},\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{p}))\mathchar 61\relax-1
for all
p\in X
.
The kernel of
\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
(see §
3.6
) acts on stability conditions by even shifts, hence there exists a unique
g\in\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
that is mapped to
T\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
and such that
\mathcal{O}_{p}
are
\sigma[g]
-stable objects of phase
1
.

Therefore we can assume that
u\mathchar 61\relax x+\mathbf{i}y\mathchar 61\relax e^{\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega}
and
\mathcal{O}_{p}
are
\sigma
-stable objects of phase
1
.
Note that
y\mathchar 61\relax(0,\omega,\beta\omega)
.
For any
E
with
\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
, we have

\operatorname{Im}Z(E)\mathchar 61\relax a_{1}\omega-r\beta\omega.

If
C\subset X
is a curve, then
\mathcal{O}_{C}
is torsion, hence
\mathcal{O}_{C}\in\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{P}_{(0,1]}
by Lemma
6.9
(3).
If
Z(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is real, then
\mathcal{O}_{C}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
which is impossible by Lemma
6.9
(2).
This implies that
\operatorname{Im}Z(\mathcal{O}_{C})\mathchar 61\relax C\cdot\omega>0
for any curve
C\subset X
.
As
\omega^{2}>0
, we conclude that
\omega
is ample.

Now we only need to show that
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
.
The proof of this statement goes through the lines of
[
14
, Prop. 10.3 (Step 2)]
.
∎

Let

(171)

\operatorname{Stab}^{+}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})\subset\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})

be the open subset of special geometric stability conditions.
Let us identify
N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
with
\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
(where
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(X)
) by sending
u\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
to

(172)

Z_{u}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C},\qquad Z_{u}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(u,\operatorname{ch}E).

Then the open subsets
\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}(X)\subset N^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
can be interpreted as open subsets of
\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
.
By the previous theorem,
the local homeomorphism
\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
,
(Z,\mathcal{P})\mapsto Z
, restricts to a local homeomorphism

(173)

\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}^{+}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)

and we have bijections

(174)

\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}^{+}(\mathcal{D})\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\delimiter 84079374\mathopen{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\bar{}\mathcal{P}^{+}(X).

6.5.
Gieseker and twisted stability

Given an ample divisor
\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
, we define the

Hilbert polynomial
of
E\in\operatorname{Coh}(X)
to be

(175)

P_{\omega}(E,n)\mathchar 61\relax\int e^{n\omega}\operatorname{ch}(E)\operatorname{Td}(X).

If
E
is torsion-free, then we define its
reduced Hilbert polynomial
to be

(176)

p_{\omega}(E,n)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{P_{\omega}(E,n)}{\operatorname{rk}E}.

We say that a torsion-free sheaf
E
is

\omega
-Gieseker semistable
if for any
0\neq E^{\prime}\subset E
we have

(177)

p_{\omega}(E^{\prime},n)\leq p_{\omega}(E,n),\qquad n\gg 0.

Let us give a more explicit characterization
of Gieseker stability.
We have

(178)

\operatorname{Td}(X)\mathchar 61\relax(1,-\tfrac{1}{2}K_{X},\chi),\qquad\chi\mathchar 61\relax\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

If
\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
, then

(179)

P_{\omega}(E,n)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{r}{2}n^{2}\omega^{2}+n(a_{1}-r\tfrac{K_{X}}{2})\omega+(a_{2}-a_{1}\tfrac{K_{X}}{2}+r\chi).

Therefore we can substitute the reduced Hilbert polynomial by

(180)

\bar{p}_{\omega}(E,n)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{na_{1}\omega+(a_{2}-a_{1}\tfrac{K_{X}}{2})}{r},\qquad\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2}),

and use it instead of
p_{\omega}(E,n)
to test Gieseker stability.
More generally, given another divisor
\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
, we define
for any torsion-free sheaf
E
with
\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})

(181)

\nu_{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}\beta}{r},\qquad p_{\beta,\omega}(E,n)\mathchar 61\relax n\mu_{\omega}(E)+\nu_{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{na_{1}\omega+(a_{2}-a_{1}\beta)}{r}.

We say that a torsion-free sheaf
E
is

(\beta,\omega)
-
twisted semistable
(cf.
[
14
]
) if,
for any subsheaf
0\neq E^{\prime}\subset E
, we have

(182)

p_{\beta,\omega}(E^{\prime},n)\leq p_{\beta,\omega}(E,n),\qquad n\gg 0.

Equivalently, this means that either
\mu_{\omega}(E^{\prime})<\mu_{\omega}(E)
or

\mu_{\omega}(E^{\prime})\mathchar 61\relax\mu_{\omega}(E)

and

\nu_{\beta}(E^{\prime})\leq\nu_{\beta}(E)
.
In particular,
E
is
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable.

6.6.
Large volume limit

Let us consider
\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
with ample
\omega
and let us define

(183)

\sigma_{t}\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{t},\mathcal{P}_{t})\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta,t\omega},\qquad t>0.

Note that the heart of
\sigma_{t}
is equal to
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
for all
t>0
.
We will say that an object
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
is
\sigma_{\infty}
-semistable (or semistable in the
large volume limit
) if
E
is
\sigma_{t}
-semistable for
t\gg 0
.

Lemma 6.11

(See
[
14
, Prop. 14.2]
)
.

Let
0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}
with
\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
be such that

(184)

r>0,\qquad\operatorname{Im}Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(a_{1}-r\beta)\omega>0.

Then
E
is
\sigma_{\infty}
-semistable if and only if
E
is a shift of a
(\beta,\omega)
-twisted semistable sheaf.

Lemma 6.12

(Cf.
[
6
, Lemma 2.7]
)
.

Let
E\in\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
be
\sigma_{\infty}
-semistable.
Then

(1)

If
\operatorname{rk}(E)<0
, then
H^{-1}(E)
is a
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable sheaf and
H^{0}(E)
has dimension
0
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{rk}(E)\geq 0
, then
E\mathchar 61\relax H^{0}(E)
is either a
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable or a torsion sheaf.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{t}
be the phase function on
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
corresponding to
\sigma_{t}
.
For any object
F\in\mathcal{D}
with
\operatorname{ch}(F)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
,
we obtain from (
162
)

(185)

\phi_{\infty}(F)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z_{t}(F)\mathchar 61\relax\begin{cases}0&r>0\\
\tfrac{1}{2}&r\mathchar 61\relax 0,\,a_{1}\omega>0\\
1&r\mathchar 61\relax a_{1}\mathchar 61\relax 0,\,a_{2}>0\end{cases}

Note that if
0\neq F\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\phi_{\infty}(F)\mathchar 61\relax\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi_{t}(F)
.
Let

\mathcal{T}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,>\beta\omega},\qquad\mathcal{F}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\leq\beta\omega}

so that
\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\left\langle\mathcal{F}[1],\mathcal{T}\right\rangle
.
Then there is an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}

0\to E_{1}[1]\to E\to E_{0}\to 0

where
E_{1}\in\mathcal{F}
and
E_{0}\in\mathcal{T}
.

Let us assume that
E_{1}\neq 0
.
Then
E_{1}\in\mathcal{F}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\leq\beta\omega}
is torsion-free,
hence
\phi_{\infty}(E_{1})\mathchar 61\relax 0
by (
185
).
This implies that
\phi_{\infty}(E_{1}[1])\mathchar 61\relax 1
.
If
E_{0}
has dimension
2
or
1
, then

\phi_{\infty}(E_{0})\mathchar 61\relax 0
or
\tfrac{1}{2}
by (
185
).
But this implies that
\phi_{t}(E_{1}[1])>\phi_{t}(E_{0})
for
t\gg 0
, which is a contradiction.
We conclude that
E_{0}
has dimension
0
,
hence
Z_{t}(E)\mathchar 61\relax-Z_{t}(E_{1})-\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E_{0})
.
Let
\operatorname{ch}(E_{1})\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
.
If
(a_{1}-r\beta)\omega\mathchar 61\relax 0
, then
\mu_{\omega}(E_{1})\mathchar 61\relax\beta\omega
, hence
E_{1}\in\operatorname{Coh}_{w,\leq\beta\omega}
is automatically
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable.
Let us assume that
(a_{1}-r\beta)\omega<0
.
If
E_{1}
is not
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable, then there exists
0\neq F\subset E_{1}
with
\mu_{\omega}(F)>\mu_{\omega}(E)
and
F,E_{1}/F\in\mathcal{F}
.
This implies that we have an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}

0\to F[1]\to E_{1}[1]\to(E_{1}/F)[1]\to 0

Let
\operatorname{ch}(F)\mathchar 61\relax(s,b_{1},b_{2})
.
If
(b_{1}-s\beta)\omega\mathchar 61\relax 0
, then
\phi_{t}(F[1])\mathchar 61\relax 1>\phi_{t}(E_{1}[1])
for any
t
, which is a contradiction.
Therefore
(b_{1}-s\beta)\omega<0
.
We obtain from (
161
) and
\sigma_{\infty}
-semistability of
E
that

\frac{-r}{(a_{1}-r\beta)\omega}\geq\frac{-s}{(b_{1}-s\beta)\omega}

which implies that
\mu_{\omega}(F)\leq\mu_{\omega}(E_{1})
.

Let us assume that
E_{1}\mathchar 61\relax 0
, hence
E\mathchar 61\relax E_{0}\in\mathcal{T}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{w,>\beta\omega}
.
Let
\operatorname{ch}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(r,a_{1},a_{2})
.
If
r>0
, then

(a_{1}-r\beta)\omega>0
and the statement follows from Lemma
6.11
.
If

, then
E
is a torsion sheaf.
∎

6.7.
Bogomolov-type inequalities

In this section we will prove a new Bogomolov-type inequality for
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects (Theorem
6.16
).
This inequality will be important in our analysis of the behavior of mini/max phases of objects under stability deformations.
Let us first describe the known results.

Let
\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
with ample
\omega
.
We define

(186)

\bar{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}\omega)^{2}-2b_{0}b_{2}\omega^{2},\qquad b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax e^{-\beta}\operatorname{ch}(E).

Recall that for every
D\in\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X)\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, we have
D\omega>0
.
Let us introduce a norm on
N_{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\supset\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X)
and define

(187)

C\mathchar 61\relax\sup\left\{\left.0,\,\frac{-D^{2}}{(D\omega)^{2}}\ \right|D\in\overline{\operatorname{NE}}(X),\,\left\lVert D\right\rVert\mathchar 61\relax 1\right\}

Then
C\geq 0
and for every effective divisor
D
we have

(188)

C(D\omega)^{2}+D^{2}\geq 0.

We define

(189)

\bar{}\Delta^{C}_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\Delta(E)+C(b_{1}\omega)^{2},\qquad b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E).

The following result is proved in

[
65
,
7
,
6
]
.

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
E\in D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
be a
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable object. Then

(190)

\bar{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}(E)\geq 0,\qquad\bar{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{C}(E)\geq 0.

Before we will formulate a new Bogomolov-type inequality, let us derive a consequence of the classical Bogomolov inequality (Theorem
6.4
).

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\omega,\beta,H
be divisors such that
\omega
is ample and
H
is nef.
For any
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable sheaf
E
, we have

\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)-b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)\geq 0,\qquad b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E).

Proof.

We can assume that
\omega^{2}\mathchar 61\relax 1
.
Let
t\mathchar 61\relax H\omega\geq 0
and
s\geq 0
be such that
s^{2}\mathchar 61\relax H^{2}
.
Then
H\mathchar 61\relax t\omega+D_{1}
for some
D_{1}\in\omega^{\perp}
,
hence
s^{2}\mathchar 61\relax H^{2}\mathchar 61\relax t^{2}+D_{1}^{2}\leq t^{2}
and
s\leq t
.
By the Bogomolov inequality, we have

\Delta(E)\mathchar 61\relax(\operatorname{ch}(E),\operatorname{ch}(E))\mathchar 61\relax(\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E),\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E))\mathchar 61\relax b_{1}^{2}-2b_{0}b_{2}\geq 0.

Therefore
2b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)\leq b_{1}^{2}(H\omega)
and we need to prove that

b_{1}^{2}(H\omega)\leq 2(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega).

We can represent
b_{1}\mathchar 61\relax xH+y\omega+D
, where
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
D\in\left\langle H,\omega\right\rangle^{\perp}
, hence
D^{2}\leq 0
.
It is enough to show that

t(x^{2}s^{2}+y^{2}+2xyt)\leq 2(xs^{2}+yt)(xt+y).

This simplifies to
ts^{2}x^{2}+ty^{2}+2s^{2}xy\geq 0
.
We have
ts^{2}x^{2}+ty^{2}+2s^{2}xy\geq s^{3}x^{2}+sy^{2}+2s^{2}xy\mathchar 61\relax s(sx+y)^{2}\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 6.15
.

The quadratic form
\Delta^{H}_{\beta,\omega}
is negative semi-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}(Z_{\beta,\omega}\colon\mathcal{N}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{C})
.
Therefore, for any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
, the cone

Z_{\beta,\omega}^{-1}(\ell)\cap\left\{\Delta^{H}_{\beta,\omega}\geq 0\right\}

is convex.

Proof.

Let
b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)
and
Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}\omega^{2}-b_{2}+ib_{1}\omega\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
Then
b_{1}\omega\mathchar 61\relax 0
and
b_{2}\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}\omega^{2}
.
Therefore
\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax-{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{2}\omega^{2}(H\omega)\leq 0
.
The last assertion follows from
Lemma
2.9
.
∎

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
\omega,\beta,H\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
be divisors such that
\omega
is ample and
H
is nef.
For any
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable object
E\in D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
,
we have

\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)-b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)\geq 0,\qquad b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E).

Proof.

Our proof is similar to the proof of

[
6
, Theorem 3.5]
.
Let us assume first that
\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
.
Recall that if
a\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}(E)
, then

Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\left(\frac{a_{0}}{2}(\omega^{2}-\beta^{2})+a_{1}\beta-a_{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}\left(a_{1}-a_{0}\beta\right)\omega.

Let
\rho(E)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Im}Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)
.
Then the image of the map
\rho\colon\mathcal{N}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
is discrete.

Let us consider stability conditions
\sigma_{t}\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta,t\omega}
for
t\geq 1
.
If
E\in\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
is
\sigma_{t}
-semistable for
t\gg 1
, then we can apply Lemma
6.12
.
If
E_{1}\mathchar 61\relax H^{-1}(E)
is
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable and
E_{0}\mathchar 61\relax H^{0}(E)
has dimension zero,
let
\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E_{1})\mathchar 61\relax b
and
\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E_{0})\mathchar 61\relax(0,0,n)
.
Then
b_{0}>0
,
n\geq 0
and

\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E_{1})+b_{0}n(H\omega)\geq 0

by Lemma
6.14
.
If
E\mathchar 61\relax H^{0}(E)
is
\mu_{\omega}
-semistable, then

\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\geq 0
by Lemma
6.14
.
If
E\mathchar 61\relax H^{0}(E)
is torsion, let
\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(0,b_{1},b_{2})
.
Then
b_{1}\mathchar 61\relax c_{1}(E)
and

\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)\geq 0.

By Lemma
6.15
, it is enough to prove the statement of the theorem for any stable object
E\in\mathcal{A}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
.
If
\rho(E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
, then
E
is automatically
\sigma_{t}
-semistable of phase
1
for all
t
, hence
\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\geq 0
by the previous discussion.
Let
E\in\mathcal{A}
be a
\sigma_{t_{0}}
-stable object for some
t_{0}\geq 1
and let us assume by induction on
\rho(E)\geq 0
(recall that
\rho
has discrete values) that if
0\neq E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
is
\sigma_{t}
-stable for some
t\geq 1
and
\rho(E^{\prime})<\rho(E)
,
then
\Delta^{H}_{\beta,\omega}(E^{\prime})\geq 0
.
If
E
is
\sigma_{t}
-stable for all
t\geq t_{0}
, then
\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\geq 0
by the previous discussion.
Otherwise, there exists
t_{1}>t_{0}
such that
E
is
\sigma_{t}
-stable for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1})
and is strictly
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-semistable (i.e. semistable, but not stable).
Then all the
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-stable factors
F_{i}
of
E
will have the same
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-phase as
E
, hence
\rho(F_{i})>0
.
Therefore
\rho(F_{i})<\rho(E)
and we conclude by induction that
\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(F_{i})\geq 0
.
As the
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-phases of
E
and
F_{i}
are the same, we conclude from Lemma
6.15
that
\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\geq 0
.

Let us assume now that
(\beta,\omega)\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\times\operatorname{Amp}(X)
is arbitrary and let
E\in D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
be
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-stable.
Then there exists an open neighborhood
U\ni(\beta,\omega)
such that
E
is stable in this neighborhood.
We can find a sequence of rational points
(\beta_{n},\omega_{n})_{n}
in
U
that converges to
(\beta,\omega)
.
Then
\Delta^{H}_{\beta_{n},\omega_{n}}(E)\geq 0
by the previous discussion.
Therefore
\Delta^{H}_{\beta,\omega}(E)\geq 0
.
∎

We will also need later a minor modification of the above result.

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
\omega,\beta,H\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
be divisors such that
\omega
and
H
are ample.
For any
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable object
E\in D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
having non-integer phase,
we have

\widetilde{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)-b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)+\tfrac{1}{2}b_{0}^{2}(H\omega)\omega^{2}>0,\qquad b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E).

Proof.

Let us use the same notation as in the previous theorem.
We can assume that
E\in\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
is
\sigma_{t_{0}}
-semistable of phase
\neq 1
.
If
E
is
\sigma_{t}
-semistable for
t\gg 0
and
b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)
, then either
b_{0}\neq 0
or
b_{0}\mathchar 61\relax 0

and
E\mathchar 61\relax H^{0}(E)
has a
1
-dimensional support.
In both cases we obtain
\widetilde{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)>0
.
Now we repeat the argument of the previous theorem.
More precisely, if we meet an object of non-zero rank in our inductive process, then
\widetilde{}\Delta^{H}_{\beta,\omega}(E)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}(H\omega)\omega^{2}
.
And if
E
is
\sigma_{t}
-stable for
t\in[t_{0},t_{1})
and is strictly
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-semistable with all
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-stable factors having zero rank, then all these
\sigma_{t_{1}}
-stable factors have the same
\sigma_{t_{0}}
-phase as
E
, which is a contradiction.
We conclude that
E
(having zero rank) is
\sigma_{t}
-stable for all
t\gg 0
.
Therefore
E\mathchar 61\relax H^{0}(E)
has a
1
-dimensional support and

\widetilde{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)>0
.
∎

6.8.
Alternative parametrization of stability conditions

The following parametrization of stability conditions

\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
also appears in the literature.
For any pair of divisors
(\beta,\omega)\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\times\operatorname{Amp}(X)
,
there exists a unique tuple

(191)

(D,H,s,t)\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\times\operatorname{Amp}(X)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}

such that

(192)

\displaystyle H^{2}\mathchar 61\relax 1,\qquad HD\mathchar 61\relax 0,

(193)

\displaystyle\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega\mathchar 61\relax(sH+D)+\mathbf{i}tH\mathchar 61\relax(s+\mathbf{i}t)H+D.

Indeed, we require
\omega\mathchar 61\relax tH
, hence
t\mathchar 61\relax\sqrt{\omega^{2}}
and
H\mathchar 61\relax\frac{1}{t}\omega
.
On the other hand, equation
\beta\mathchar 61\relax sH+D
determines
s
and
D
uniquely as we have a decomposition
N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{R}H\oplus H^{\perp}
.

Note that
\beta\omega\mathchar 61\relax st
and we have
\mu_{\omega}(E)\leq\beta\omega
if and only if
\mu_{H}(E)\leq s
.
Therefore

(194)

\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\#\beta\omega}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{H,\#s}.

From now on, we fix divisors
D
and
H
as above and we consider
s
and
t
as parameters.
Let us define linear maps

(195)

\displaystyle v_{H}\colon N^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)\to\mathbb{R}^{3},\qquad a\mapsto(a_{0},a_{1}H,a_{2}),

(196)

\displaystyle v_{D,H}\colon K(X)\to\mathbb{R}^{3},\qquad E\mapsto v_{H}(\operatorname{ch}^{D}(E)).

We define a linear map
Z^{\prime}_{s,t}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax Z^{\prime}_{s,t}(v_{D,H}(E))\qquad\forall E\in K(X).

Explicitly, if
v\mathchar 61\relax v_{D,H}(E)\mathchar 61\relax(a_{0},a_{1}H,a_{2})
with
a\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{D}(E)
, then

(197)

Z^{\prime}_{s,t}(v)\mathchar 61\relax-\int e^{-(s+\mathbf{i}t)H}\cdot a\mathchar 61\relax\left(\frac{a_{0}}{2}(t^{2}-s^{2})+sHa_{1}-a_{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}\left(tHa_{1}-sta_{0}\right)\\
\mathchar 61\relax\left(\frac{v_{0}}{2}(t^{2}-s^{2})+sv_{1}-v_{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}t\left(v_{1}-sv_{0}\right)\mathchar 61\relax\mathchar 58\relax-\theta(v)+\mathbf{i}t\rho(v).

We can substitute
Z^{\prime}_{s,t}
by the equivalent stability function (semistability is preserved)

(198)

\displaystyle Z^{\prime\prime}_{s,q}(v)\mathchar 61\relax-\theta(v)-s\rho(v)+\mathbf{i}\rho(v)\mathchar 61\relax\left(q{v_{0}}-v_{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}\left(v_{1}-sv_{0}\right),

(199)

\displaystyle q\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}(s^{2}+t^{2})>{\frac{1}{2}}s^{2}.

7.
Stability data associated to geometric stability conditions

7.1.
Vanishing results

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and
\beta,\omega\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
with ample
\omega
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For any line bundle
L\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
, the automorphism

D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X),\qquad E\mapsto E(D)\mathchar 61\relax E\otimes L,

maps
(Z_{\beta,\omega},\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega})
to

(Z_{\beta+D,\omega},\mathcal{A}_{\beta+D,\omega})
.

Proof.

We have
\mu_{\omega}(E)\leq\beta\omega
if and only if

\mu_{\omega}(E(D))\mathchar 61\relax\mu_{\omega}(E)+D\omega\leq(\beta+D)\omega
.
This implies that
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
is mapped to
\mathcal{A}_{\beta+D,\omega}
.
If
a\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}(E)
, then

Z_{\beta+D,\omega}(E(D))\mathchar 61\relax(e^{\beta+D+i\omega},e^{D}\operatorname{ch}(E))\mathchar 61\relax(e^{\beta+i\omega},\operatorname{ch}(E))\mathchar 61\relax Z_{\beta,\omega}(E).

Therefore semistability in
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
with respect to
Z_{\beta,\omega}
corresponds to
semistability in
\mathcal{A}_{\beta+D,\omega}
with respect to
Z_{\beta+D,\omega}
.
∎

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
E,F
be
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects with
\phi_{\beta,\omega}(E)<\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)
.
Then, for any nef line bundle
L
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0.

Proof.

Let
L\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}_{X}(H)
.
We will prove that
\phi_{\beta-H,\omega}^{-}(F)\geq\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)
.
Then

\phi_{\beta-H,\omega}^{-}(F)\geq\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)>\phi_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{\beta-H,\omega}(E(-H)).

By Lemma
7.1
, the object
E(-H)
is semistable with respect to
\sigma_{\beta-H,\omega}
.
Therefore

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(F,E(-H))\mathchar 61\relax 0.

To prove the inequality
\phi_{\beta-H,\omega}^{-}(F)\geq\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)
,
let us consider the family of stability conditions
\sigma_{t}\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{t},\mathcal{P}_{t})\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta-tH,\omega}
for
t\in[0,1]
and let
\phi_{t}
be the corresponding phase function.
Then the required inequality
\phi^{-}_{1}(F)\geq\phi_{0}(F)
will follow from Theorem
3.12
, after we prove that

\operatorname{Im}(Z^{\prime}_{t}(E)\cdot\bar{Z}_{t}(E))\geq 0

for any
\sigma_{t}
-semistable object
E
.
It is enough to consider just
t\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
If
b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)
, then (
164
)

Z_{0}(E)\mathchar 61\relax Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\tfrac{1}{2}b_{0}\omega^{2}-b_{2}+\mathbf{i}b_{1}\omega.

Z^{\prime}_{0}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\frac{d}{dt}Z_{\beta-tH,\omega}(E)|_{t\mathchar 61\relax 0}\mathchar 61\relax\frac{d}{dt}(e^{\mathbf{i}\omega-tH},b)|_{t\mathchar 61\relax 0}\mathchar 61\relax(e^{\mathbf{i}\omega},Hb)\mathchar 61\relax-b_{1}H+\mathbf{i}b_{0}(H\omega).

Therefore

(200)

\operatorname{Im}(Z^{\prime}_{0}(E)\cdot\bar{Z}_{0}(E))\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)-b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)+\tfrac{1}{2}b_{0}^{2}(H\omega)\omega^{2}.

By Theorem
6.16
,
for any
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable object
E
with
\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)\mathchar 61\relax b
, we have

(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)-b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)\geq 0.

The summand
\tfrac{1}{2}b_{0}^{2}(H\omega)\omega^{2}
is non-negative.
Therefore
\operatorname{Im}(Z^{\prime}_{0}(E)\cdot\bar{Z}_{0}(E))\geq 0
as required.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
\sigma
be a special geometric stability condition
and
E,F
be
\sigma
-semistable objects with
\phi_{\sigma}(E)<\phi_{\sigma}(F)
.
Then, for any nef line bundle
L
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.10
there exists
g\mathchar 61\relax(T,f)\in\widetilde{}\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\sigma[g]\mathchar 61\relax\sigma^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
for some
\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
and
\omega\in\operatorname{Amp}(X)
.
Then
E,F
are
\sigma^{\prime}
-semistable and
\phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(E)<\phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(F)
.
We conclude from Theorem
7.2
that

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
as required.
∎

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
E,F
be
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects with
\phi_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Then, for any ample line bundle
L
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0.

Proof.

Let us use the same notation as in the previous theorem.
We can assume that
E,F\in\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
have phase
\phi\in(0,1)
.
In our analysis of the behavior of phases under deformations from the previous theorem, we note that
by Theorem
6.17
(where
L\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}_{X}(H)
and
b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}^{\beta}(E)
)

(201)

\operatorname{Im}(Z^{\prime}_{0}(E)\cdot\bar{Z}_{0}(E))\mathchar 61\relax(b_{1}H)(b_{1}\omega)-b_{0}b_{2}(H\omega)+\tfrac{1}{2}b_{0}^{2}(H\omega)\omega^{2}\mathchar 61\relax\widetilde{}\Delta_{\beta,\omega}^{H}(E)>0

whenever
E
is a
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable object having phase in
(0,1)
.
By the same argument as in Theorem
3.12
, we conclude that
\phi_{1}^{-}(F)>\phi_{0}(F)
.
Therefore

\phi_{\beta-H,\omega}^{-}(F)>\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{\beta-H,\omega}(E(-H)).

This implies that
\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,H)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(F,E(-H))\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

Note that the sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{x}
are
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects of phase
1
while
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{x}\otimes L,\mathcal{O}_{x})\neq 0
for any line bundle
L
.
On the other hand, the above theorem can be extended to semistable objects of phase
1
having non-zero rank (see Lemma
6.9
).

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
\sigma
be a special geometric stability condition
and
E,F
be
\sigma
-semistable objects with
\phi_{\sigma}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{\sigma}(F)\notin\phi_{\sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{x})+\mathbb{Z}
for
x\in X
.
Then, for any ample line bundle
L
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes L,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
X
be a surface with a nef anticanonical bundle.
Then any special geometric stability condition
\sigma
on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
satisfies

\operatorname{gldim}(\sigma)\mathchar 61\relax 2.

Proof.

To prove that
\operatorname{gldim}(\sigma)\leq 2
, we need to show that given two
\sigma
-semistable objects
E,F
with
\phi_{\sigma}(F)>\phi_{\sigma}(E)+2
, we have
\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
By Serre duality,
this means that
\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1},E[2])\mathchar 61\relax 0
, where
\omega_{X}^{-1}
is nef and
\phi_{\sigma}(E[2])<\phi_{\sigma}(F)
.
The last equation follows from Corollary
7.3
.
To see that
\operatorname{gldim}(\sigma)\mathchar 61\relax 2
, we note that for any
x\in X
the object
\mathcal{O}_{x}
is
\sigma
-stable and
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{x},\mathcal{O}_{x})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{x},\mathcal{O}_{x})^{\vee}\neq 0
.
∎

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface (meaning that the anticanonical bundle is ample).
Let
E,F
be
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable objects with
\phi_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax\phi_{\beta,\omega}(F)\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Then

\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax 0\qquad\forall k\geq 2.

Proof.

We apply Serre duality and Theorem
7.4
.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

It was proved in
[
46
, Lemma 4]
that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
for a
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable object
E
of nonzero rank under an implicit assumption that
-K_{X}
is proportional to
\omega
.
A similar method was used in
[
25
]
to show that the statement of Theorem
7.7
is true for
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
This method doesn’t seem to generalize to other surfaces with nef or ample anticanonical bundle.

7.2.
Geometric stability data

In this section we will combine all previous results and obtain a continuous family of stability data associated to a surface.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface,
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)

and
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{N}(X)
.
Let
\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
,
\omega\in\operatorname{Amp}(X)
and
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
be the corresponding stability condition on
\mathcal{D}
.
This stability condition satisfies assumptions (
1
) and (
2
) by the results of
[
64
]

(see also
[
57
]
).
We conclude from Theorem
6.10
that the same is true for any special geometric stability condition
\sigma\in\operatorname{Stab}^{+}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
(
171
).

Let us assume now that
X
has a nef anticanonical bundle.
Then we conclude from Theorem
7.7
that
assumption (
3
) is satisfied for any
special geometric stability condition

\sigma\in\operatorname{Stab}^{+}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})
.
Following §
5.4
and §
5.5
, we consider the quantum torus
\mathfrak{g}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}Rx^{\gamma}
,
R\mathchar 61\relax\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{St^{\mathrm{a}}}/\mathbb{C})
,
and define,
for any special geometric stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z_{\sigma},\mathcal{P}_{\sigma})\in\operatorname{Stab}^{+}_{\operatorname{cl}}(X)
,
the corresponding stability data

a_{\sigma}\mathchar 61\relax(a_{\sigma,\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}
by

(202)

\displaystyle a_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{Z_{\sigma}(\gamma)\in\ell}a_{\sigma,\gamma}\mathchar 61\relax\log(A_{\sigma,\ell})\in\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z_{\sigma}},

(203)

\displaystyle A_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z_{\sigma}(\gamma)\in\ell}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}[\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)]x^{\gamma}\in\hat{G}_{\ell,Z_{\sigma}},

for any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
,
where
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
is the moduli stack of
\sigma
-semistable objects having class
\gamma
,

\hat{}\mathfrak{g}_{\ell,Z_{\sigma}}
is the ind-pro-nilpotent Lie algebra defined in (

) and

\hat{G}_{\ell,Z_{\sigma}}
is the corresponding ind-pro-nilpotent group.
We obtain from Theorem
5.9

Theorem 7.10
.

For any smooth projective surface
X
with the nef anticanonical bundle, the family of stability data
(Z_{\sigma},a_{\sigma})_{\sigma\in\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}^{+}(\mathcal{D})}
is continuous.

7.3.
Relation to intersection cohomology

We briefly explain the relationship of the above invariants to the intersection cohomology of the moduli spaces of semistable objects.
Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface, meaning that the anticanonical bundle is ample.
By the results of
[
64
,
57
]
,
for any
\sigma\in\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{cl}}^{+}(\mathcal{D})
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we can construct the moduli space
M_{\sigma}(\gamma)
of
\sigma
-semistable objects having class
\gamma
(and phase
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z_{\sigma}(\gamma)
)
and the moduli space
M_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)
of
\sigma
-stable objects having class
\gamma
.
The moduli space
M_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)
is smooth if
\operatorname{Hom}^{2}(E,E)\mathchar 61\relax 0
for all
E\in M_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)
(or more generally, if
\operatorname{Hom}^{2}(E,E)
has constant dimension).

Given an algebraic variety
Y
of dimension
d_{Y}
, let
\operatorname{\underline{IC}}_{Y}\mathbb{Q}^{H}\in\operatorname{MHM}(Y)
be the corresponding intersection complex (see e.g.
[
62
]
).
It is a simple pure object of weight
d_{Y}
.
If
Y
is smooth, then
\operatorname{\underline{IC}}_{Y}\mathbb{Q}^{H}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{H}[d_{Y}]
.
We define (cf.
[
48
]
)

(204)

\operatorname{IH}^{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\mathchar 61\relax H^{*}_{c}(Y,\operatorname{\underline{IC}}_{Y}\mathbb{Q}^{H})[-d_{Y}]\in D^{b}(\operatorname{MHS}_{\mathbb{Q}}).

Let us consider the Hodge-Deligne polynomial (cf. (
104
))

(205)

E\colon K(D^{b}(\operatorname{MHS}_{\mathbb{Q}}))\simeq K(\operatorname{MHS}_{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbb{Z}[u^{\pm 1},v^{\pm 1}],\qquad V\mapsto\sum_{p,q}\dim\left(\operatorname{Gr}^{p}_{F}\operatorname{Gr}^{W}_{p+q}V_{\mathbb{C}}\right)u^{p}v^{q}.

and define

(206)

\operatorname{IE}(Y;u,v)\mathchar 61\relax E(\operatorname{IH}^{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q}))\in\mathbb{Z}[u^{\pm 1},v^{\pm 1}].

Let us consider the closure
\bar{M}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)

of
M_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)\subset M_{\sigma}(\gamma)
and define
(recall that we defined earlier
E(\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathchar 61\relax-\sqrt{uv}
)

(207)

\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)\mathchar 61\relax E(\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\chi(\gamma,\gamma)-1}\cdot\operatorname{IE}(\bar{M}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma);u,v).

On the other hand, given a ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
, we consider (cf. (
203
))

(208)

E(A_{\sigma,\ell})\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z_{\sigma}(\gamma)\in\ell}E(\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\chi(\gamma,\gamma)}\cdot E(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma))x^{\gamma}.

Let us assume that a ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}
is such that
Z_{\sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{x})\notin\pm\ell
for
x\in X
and

(209)

\chi(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\mathchar 61\relax\chi(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)\qquad\forall\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma\cap Z_{\sigma}^{-1}(\ell).

By Theorem
7.8
, the first assumption implies that for any
\sigma
-semistable objects
E,F
having the same phase and classes in
Z_{\sigma}^{-1}(\ell)
, we have
\operatorname{Hom}^{2}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
The second assumption implies that the quantum torus restricted to degrees in
\Gamma\cap Z_{\sigma}^{-1}(\ell)
is commutative.
By the results of
[
51
,
50
,
56
,
48
]
,
we have

(210)

E(A_{\sigma,\ell})\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Exp}\left(\frac{\sum_{Z_{\sigma}(\gamma)\in\ell}\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)x^{\gamma}}{E(\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}})-E(\mathbb{L}^{-{\frac{1}{2}}})}\right),

where
\operatorname{Exp}
is the plethystic exponential (see e.g.
[
48
]
).

Remark 7.11
.

In
[
12
]
one used the last formula as the definition of the invariants
E(A_{\sigma,\ell})
in the case of
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Note that this formula can not be used if

.

8.
Relation to quivers with potentials

8.1.
Exceptional collections and tilting objects

For more information on tilting theory see e.g.
[
1
]
.
Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
and let

(211)

E_{1},\,\dots,\,E_{n}

be an
exceptional collection
in
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
, meaning that

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{i},E_{i})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
k\neq 0
and
\operatorname{Hom}(E_{i},E_{i})\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{C}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{i},E_{j})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
i>j
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We will say that an exceptional collection is
full
if the objects
E_{i}
generate
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
We will say that an exceptional collection is
strong
if
\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{i},E_{j})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for all
i,j
and
k\neq 0
.
Given a full and strong exceptional collection,
let us define the algebra

(212)

A\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad T^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{i}E_{i},

and let
\operatorname{mod}A
denote the category of finite-dimensional left
A
-modules.
Then there is an equivalence of categories

[
10
,
61
,
1
]

(213)

\Phi\colon D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A),\qquad E\mapsto\operatorname{RHom}(T^{\prime},E).

The objects
E_{i}
are mapped to the projective modules
P_{i}\mathchar 61\relax Ae_{i}
, where
e_{i}\in A
is the idempotent corresponding to
\operatorname{id}\in\operatorname{End}(E_{i})
.
Let us introduce a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
A\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}A_{k}
,
A_{k}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{j-i\mathchar 61\relax k}\operatorname{Hom}(E_{i},E_{j})
, and let

\bar{A}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Ker}(A\to A_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{i\mathchar 61\relax 1}^{n}\mathbb{C})
be the augmentation ideal.
By
[
20
, Lemma A.1]
, one can represent
A
as a quotient
\mathbb{C}Q^{\prime}/I
for a unique quiver
Q^{\prime}
with the set of vertices
Q^{\prime}_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
and the number of arrows from
i
to
j
equal to
\dim e_{j}(\bar{A}/\bar{A}^{2})e_{i}
.

On the other hand, let us consider the canonical bundle

Y\mathchar 61\relax\omega_{X}
as an algebraic variety and let
\pi\colon Y\to X
be the projection.
Note that
Y
is a non-compact Calabi-Yau variety.
We have
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\omega_{X}^{-i}
and
Y\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Spec}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y})
.
Let
\operatorname{Coh}_{c}Y\subset\operatorname{Coh}Y
be the subcategory of coherent sheaves with compact support and let
\operatorname{Coh}_{0}Y\subset\operatorname{Coh}_{c}Y
be the subcategory of coherent sheaves supported on
X\subset Y
embedded as the zero section.
A coherent sheaf
\bar{E}\in\operatorname{Coh}_{c}Y
can be identified with a pair
(E,\phi)
, where
E\mathchar 61\relax\pi_{*}\bar{E}\in\operatorname{Coh}X
and
\phi\colon E\to E\otimes\omega_{X}
is a morphism.
We have
\bar{E}\in\operatorname{Coh}_{0}Y
if and only if
\phi\colon E\to E\otimes\omega_{X}
is nilpotent, meaning that

E\to E\otimes\omega_{X}\to E\otimes\omega_{X}^{2}\to\dots
eventually vanishes.

The category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}_{c}X)
can be identified with the subcategory
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\subset D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
consisting of complexes with cohomology having compact support.
Similarly, the category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}_{0}X)
can be identified with the subcategory
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\subset D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
consisting of complexes with cohomology supported on
X
.
Note that the full embedding
\operatorname{Coh}X\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Coh}_{0}Y
,
E\mapsto(E,0)
, induces a (non-full) functor
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
.
The category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
generates
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
by extensions.

Lemma 8.1

(See e.g.
[
54
]
)
.

Let
\bar{E}\mathchar 61\relax(E,\phi)
,
\bar{F}\mathchar 61\relax(F,\psi)
be objects in
\operatorname{Coh}_{c}Y
.
Then there is an exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\bar{E},\bar{F})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(E,F)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(E,F\otimes\omega_{X})\\
\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{Y}(\bar{E},\bar{F})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(E,F)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(E,F\otimes\omega_{X})\to\dots{}

Corollary 8.2
.

Let
\bar{E}\mathchar 61\relax(E,\phi)
,
\bar{F}\mathchar 61\relax(F,\psi)
be objects in
\operatorname{Coh}_{c}Y
.
Then

\chi_{Y}(\bar{E},\bar{F})\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{X}(E,F)-(-1)^{d}\chi_{X}(F,E)\mathchar 61\relax\mathchar 58\relax\left\langle E,F\right\rangle.

Let us consider the object
T\mathchar 61\relax\pi^{*}T^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{i}\pi^{*}E_{i}
in
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
.
We have

(214)

\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(T,T)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(T^{\prime},T^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y})\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
m\geq 0\end{subarray}}}\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{i},E_{j}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-m}).

Let us assume that

(215)

\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(T,T)\mathchar 61\relax 0\qquad\forall k\neq 0.

The object
T
generates
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
in the sense that

\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}^{\bullet}(T,F)\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
F\in D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
implies
F\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
Indeed,
\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}^{\bullet}(T,F)\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}_{X}^{\bullet}(T^{\prime},\pi_{*}F)\mathchar 61\relax 0
implies
\pi_{*}F
, hence
F\mathchar 61\relax 0
.
This means that
T
is a
classical tilting object
,
hence there is an equivalence of categories
[
30
, §7]
(see also
[
20
, Theorem 3.6]
)

(216)

\Phi\colon D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}B),\qquad E\mapsto\operatorname{RHom}(T,E),\qquad B\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T)^{\mathrm{op}}.

Let us consider the ideal
\mathcal{I}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{m\geq 1}\operatorname{Hom}(T^{\prime},T^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-m})
of
B
and let
\operatorname{mod}_{0}B\subset\operatorname{mod}B
consist of modules on which
\mathcal{I}
acts nilpotently.
Then the above functor induces an equivalence

(217)

\Phi\colon D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\to D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{mod}B)\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}_{0}B),\qquad E\mapsto\operatorname{RHom}(T,E).

Let us extend the above exceptional collection to a sequence
(E_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that

(218)

E_{i+n}\mathchar 61\relax E_{i}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1},\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

This is an example of a helix
[
20
]
.
By our assumptions
\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{i},E_{j})\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
j>i-n

and
k\neq 0
.
On the other hand,
\operatorname{Hom}(E_{i},E_{j})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}^{d}(E_{j+n},E_{i})^{\vee}\mathchar 61\relax 0
for
i>j
.
Therefore

(219)

B^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq n,j\geq i}\operatorname{Hom}(E_{i},E_{j}).

8.2.
Local surfaces

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface,
Y\mathchar 61\relax\omega_{X}
be its canonical bundle and
\pi\colon Y\to X
be the projection as before.
Then
Y
is a non-compact 3-Calabi-Yau variety which is sometimes called a
local surface
.
Recall that

(220)

\displaystyle\chi_{X}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax\int a^{*}b\cdot\operatorname{Td}(X),\qquad a\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}(E),\,b\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}(F),

(221)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Td}(X)\mathchar 61\relax(1,-\tfrac{1}{2}K_{X},\chi),\qquad\chi\mathchar 61\relax\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

We have
a^{*}a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{0}^{2},0,2a_{0}a_{2}-a_{1}^{2})
, hence

(222)

\chi_{X}(E,E)\mathchar 61\relax\chi\cdot a_{0}^{2}-\Delta(E).

We have
a^{*}b-b^{*}a\mathchar 61\relax 2(0,a_{0}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{0},0)
, hence

(223)

\left\langle E,F\right\rangle\mathchar 61\relax(a_{1}b_{0}-a_{0}b_{1})K_{X}\mathchar 61\relax(a,bK_{X}).

For many surfaces
X
(see e.g.
[
8
]
)
one can find an exceptional collection (
211
)
such that
T^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{i}E_{i}
and
T\mathchar 61\relax\pi^{*}T^{\prime}
satisfy the assumptions of §
8.1
,
the algebra
B\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T)^{\mathrm{op}}
can be identified with the Jacobian algebra
J_{W}
of some quiver with potential
(Q,W)
(see §
8.4
)
and the algebra
A\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}
can be identified with the partial Jacobian algebra
J_{W,I}
corresponding to a cut
I\subset Q_{1}
(see §
8.4
).
This implies that we can construct three, a priori unrelated, families of stability data:

(1)

For the category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
.

(2)

For the category
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\simeq D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
.

(3)

For the category
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
.

Because of Corollary
8.2
, all these families are defined in the same quantum torus.
We will show later that for
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
stability data on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
and on
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
can be glued together.
This implies that knowing stability data on
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
, we can reconstruct stability data on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
A complete (but still conjectural except for low dimension vectors) description of stability data on
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})

was given in
[
8
,
55
]
.
A partial description of stability data on

D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
was given in
[
12
]
(the scattering diagram used there captures a significant, but incomplete information about the stability data).
It should be not difficult to generalize our results to other del Pezzo surfaces.
A complete (conjectural) description of stability data for
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
is also available in this case

[
8
,
55
]
.

Recall that for every
\beta\in N^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
and
\omega\in\operatorname{Amp}(X)
we have a stability condition
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
This stability condition can be extended to
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
(see e.g.
[
5
]
).
Alternatively, one can use the approach of
[
31
]
to extend a stability condition from

D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
to
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
.
Motivic DT invariants can be defined for the 3CY categories
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
and
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
using the framework of
[
41
]
, as long as one proves that various necessary assumptions are satisfied (and this is not a simple task).
On the other hand, as was observed in
[
12
]
(see also
[
21
, Prop. 3.1]
), in many situations (assuming that
X
is a del Pezzo surface) semistable objects in
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
are automatically semistable objects in
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
.
Intuitively, the reason is that for any semistable object
(E,\phi)
, there is a morphism
\phi\colon(E,\phi)\to(E\otimes\omega_{X},\phi\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\omega_{X}})
which has to vanish if the object on the right is semistable of phase less than the phase of
(E,\phi)
.
This means that for del Pezzo surfaces one can perform computations in
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
instead of the 3CY categories
D^{b}_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
and
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
(at least for some stability conditions and some Chern characters).

In the next sections we will consider the case of
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
and we will see how computation of stability data for
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
reduces to computations of stability data for a certain quiver with potential.

8.3.
Relation to quiver representations

In this section we will identify the derived category
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
for
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
with the derived category of some explicit finite-dimensional algebra
A
of homological dimension two.
We will see later that this algebra arises from a quiver with a potential and a cut.

8.3.1.
Derived equivalence

Let us consider the Beilinson exceptional collection on
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}

(224)

\mathcal{O},\,\mathcal{O}(1),\,\mathcal{O}(2).

As in §
8.1
, we define the algebra

(225)

A\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad T^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(2),

and consider an equivalence of categories

(226)

\Phi\colon D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A),\qquad E\mapsto\operatorname{RHom}(T^{\prime},E).

The algebra
A
can be described as the quotient of the path algebra
\mathbb{C}Q^{\prime}
for the quiver
Q^{\prime}

(227)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{i}}

by the relations

(228)

a_{i}b_{j}\mathchar 61\relax a_{j}b_{i},\qquad i\neq j.

Let
e_{i}\in A
be the idempotent corresponding to the trivial path at the vertex
i\in Q^{\prime}_{0}
.
Let
S_{i}\in\operatorname{mod}A
denote the corresponding simple module,
P_{i}\mathchar 61\relax Ae_{i}\in\operatorname{mod}A
denote the projective indecomposable module, and
I_{i}\mathchar 61\relax D(e_{i}A)\in\operatorname{mod}A
denote the injective indecomposable module, where
DV\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V,\mathbb{C})
for a vector space
V
.
Note that
S_{0}\mathchar 61\relax P_{0}
and
S_{2}\mathchar 61\relax I_{2}
.

The objects of the exceptional sequence (
224
) are mapped to the projective modules
P_{0}
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
respectively.
Note that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(P_{0},P_{1})\mathchar 61\relax e_{0}Ae_{1},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(2))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(P_{1},P_{2})\mathchar 61\relax e_{1}Ae_{2},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(2))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{6}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(P_{0},P_{2})\mathchar 61\relax e_{0}Ae_{2}.

8.3.2.
Simple objects

From now on we will identify
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
with
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A)
using the equivalence
\Phi
(
226
).
We claim that the simple objects
S_{0}
,
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
correspond to

(229)

\mathcal{O},\,\Omega_{X}(1)[1],\,\mathcal{O}(-1)[2],

where
\Omega_{X}
is the cotangent bundle.
It is clear that
S_{0}\mathchar 61\relax P_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}
.
Note that

(230)

\dim\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(P_{i},S_{j})\mathchar 61\relax\delta_{ij}\delta_{k0}.

It is proved in
[
20
, Lemma 2.5]
that for a full exceptional collection
(E_{1},\dots,E_{n})
there exists a unique collection
(F_{n},\dots,F_{1})

such that
\dim\operatorname{Hom}^{k}(E_{i},F_{j})\mathchar 61\relax\delta_{ij}\delta_{k0}

(the new collection is also a full exceptional collection).
Therefore it is enough to show that (
229
) has the required properties.
Below we will give a direct proof instead.

The category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
has the Serre functor (note that
\omega_{X}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}(-3)
)

(231)

\mathcal{S}\colon D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X),\qquad E\mapsto E\otimes\omega_{X}[2].

On the other hand, the category
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A)
also has the Serre functor (called the Nakayama functor in this context; the same construction works for any finite-dimensional algebra
A
of finite homological dimension)

(232)

\nu\colon D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A),\qquad M\mapsto DA\otimes^{L}_{A}M,

where we consider
DA\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(A,\mathbb{C})
as an
A
-bimodule.
Note that
\nu P_{i}\mathchar 61\relax I_{i}
.
We can identify the Serre functors
\mathcal{S}
and
\nu
, hence

(233)

I_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}(-3)[2],\qquad I_{1}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}(-2)[2],\qquad S_{2}\mathchar 61\relax I_{2}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}(-1)[2].

Let us consider the universal exact sequence on
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}

[
26
, B.5.7]

(234)

0\to\mathcal{O}(-1)\to\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}\to\mathcal{Q}\to 0.

Then the tangent bundle is given by
T_{X}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{Q}(1)
.
Dualizing the above exact sequence, we obtain a distinguished triangle

(235)

\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}\to\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathcal{Q}^{\vee}[1]\to

which corresponds to the canonical short exact sequence in
\operatorname{mod}A

(236)

0\to S_{0}^{\oplus 3}\to P_{1}\to S_{1}\to 0.

We conclude that
S_{1}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{Q}^{\vee}[1]\mathchar 61\relax\Omega_{X}(1)[1]
.

8.3.3.
Chern classes and dimension vectors

The above equivalence

\Phi\colon\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}A)

induces an isomorphism between the (numerical) Grothendieck groups.
We have linear maps
\operatorname{ch}\colon\mathcal{N}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\to N^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
and

(237)

\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}\colon\mathcal{N}(\operatorname{mod}A)\to\mathbb{Z}Q^{\prime}_{0},\qquad M\mapsto(\dim e_{i}M)_{i\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}.

Given
E\in\mathcal{D}
, let us write

\operatorname{ch}(E)\in N^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
and
\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}E\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}\Phi(E)\in\mathbb{Z}Q^{\prime}_{0}

as column vectors and let
M
be the transition matrix such that
\operatorname{ch}E\mathchar 61\relax M\cdot\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}E
.
Let us also define matrices

(238)

A\mathchar 61\relax(\chi(P_{i},P_{j}))_{ij},\qquad B\mathchar 61\relax(\chi(S_{i},S_{j}))_{ij},\qquad C\mathchar 61\relax(\operatorname{ch}P_{i})_{i}.

Then

(239)

A\cdot B^{t}\mathchar 61\relax I,\qquad M\mathchar 61\relax C\cdot A^{-1}.

We can calculate
A
and
C
using (
224
)
and we obtain

(240)

A\mathchar 61\relax\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&3&6\\
0&1&3\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\qquad B\mathchar 61\relax\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
-3&1&0\\
3&-3&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\qquad C\mathchar 61\relax\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1\\
0&1&2\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\qquad M\mathchar 61\relax\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-2&1\\
0&1&-1\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

In particular, if
a\mathchar 61\relax(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}(E)
and

d\mathchar 61\relax(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}E
, then

(241)

a_{0}\mathchar 61\relax d_{0}-2d_{1}+d_{2},\qquad a_{1}\mathchar 61\relax d_{1}-d_{2},\qquad 2a_{2}\mathchar 61\relax d_{1}+d_{2}.

Remark 8.3
.

The above formula also follows from the fact that the Chern classes of simple objects (
229
) are

(242)

\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{O}\mathchar 61\relax(1,0,0),\qquad\operatorname{ch}\Omega_{X}(1)[1]\mathchar 61\relax(-2,1,\tfrac{1}{2}),\qquad\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]\mathchar 61\relax(1,-1,\tfrac{1}{2}).

Let us consider the quiver
Q
(cf. (
227
))

(243)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{i}}

and the corresponding Euler form

(244)

\chi_{Q}(d,d^{\prime})\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i}d_{i}(d^{\prime}_{i}-3d^{\prime}_{i-1}).

Then the above explicit formula for the matrix
B
implies

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
E,F\in\mathcal{D}
and
d\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}E
,
d^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}F
. Then

(245)

\chi_{X}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{Q}(d,d^{\prime})+3d_{2}d^{\prime}_{0}+3d_{0}d^{\prime}_{2}.

In particular, we have

(246)

\displaystyle\chi_{X}(E,E)\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{Q}(d,d)+6d_{0}d_{2},

(247)

\displaystyle\left\langle E,F\right\rangle\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{Q}(d,d^{\prime})-\chi_{Q}(d^{\prime},d)\mathchar 61\relax\mathchar 58\relax\left\langle d,d^{\prime}\right\rangle.

8.4.
Quivers with potential and their motivic invariants

The quiver
Q
defined in (
243
) is the McKay quiver of the representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
given by
1\mapsto\operatorname{diag}(\xi,\xi,\xi)
,
\xi\mathchar 61\relax e^{2\mathbf{i}\pi/3}
.
This quiver is automatically equipped with the potential
[
27
, §4.4]

(248)

W\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{3}}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot a_{\sigma 1}b_{\sigma 2}c_{\sigma 3},

where
\Sigma_{3}
is the permutation group.

Generally, let us assume that we have a quiver
Q
equipped with a
potential

W\in\mathbb{C}Q/[\mathbb{C}Q,\mathbb{C}Q]
, that is, a finite linear combination
W\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{u}c_{u}u
of cyclic paths considered up to cyclic shift.
We define the
Jacobian algebra
of
(Q,W)
to be

(249)

J_{W}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{C}Q/(\partial W)\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{C}Q/(\partial W/\partial a\mathchar 58\relax a\in Q_{1}),

where
\partial u/\partial a\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{i\colon a_{i}\mathchar 61\relax a}a_{i-1}\dots a_{1}a_{n}\dots a_{i+1}
for any cycle
u\mathchar 61\relax a_{n}\dots a_{1}
.
Let
I\subset Q_{1}
be a cut, meaning a subset such that every non-zero term of
W
contains exactly one arrow from
I
.
We consider the quiver
Q^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax(Q_{0},Q_{1}\backslash I)
and define
the
partial Jacobian algebra

(250)

J_{W,I}\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{C}Q^{\prime}/(\partial W/\partial a\mathchar 58\relax a\in I).

For any dimension vector
d\in\mathbb{N}Q_{0}
, we define

(251)

R(Q,d)\mathchar 61\relax\bigoplus_{a\colon i\to j}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{d_{j}})

and we define
R(J_{W},d)\subset R(Q,d)
and
R(J_{W,I},d)\subset R(Q^{\prime},d)
to be the closed subsets corresponding to the relations of the algebras
J_{W}
and
J_{W,I}
respectively.
We define

(252)

\operatorname{tr}W\colon R(Q,d)\to\mathbb{C},\qquad M\mapsto\operatorname{tr}W|M\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{u}c_{u}\cdot\operatorname{tr}u|M,

where
\operatorname{tr}u|M
for a cycle
u\mathchar 61\relax a_{n}\dots a_{1}
is defined to be
\operatorname{tr}(M_{a_{n}}\dots M_{a_{1}})
.
Then
R(J_{W},d)
can be identified with the critical locus of
\operatorname{tr}W
(see e.g.
[
63
]
).
Let us consider the skew-symmetric form on
\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}

(253)

\left\langle d,d^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{Q}(d,d^{\prime})-\chi_{Q}(d^{\prime},d)

and the corresponding quantum torus
\mathbb{T}
(
123
) (with coefficients in the Grothendieck ring of varieties with exponentials,
see e.g.
[
53
, §5]
).
We define the series

(254)

A_{0}^{Q,W}\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{d\in\mathbb{N}Q_{0}}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{Q}(d,d)}\frac{[R(Q,d),\operatorname{tr}W]}{[\operatorname{GL}_{d}]}x^{d}\in\hat{}\mathbb{T},

where
[R(Q,d),\operatorname{tr}W]
is the exponential motivic class (see e.g.
[
53
, §5]
) and
\operatorname{GL}_{d}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{i}\operatorname{GL}_{d_{i}}
.
This expression can be simplified in the presence of a cut

I\subset Q_{1}
,
namely
[
53
, §5]

(255)

\displaystyle[R(Q,d),\operatorname{tr}W]\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{L}^{\gamma_{I}(d,d)}\cdot[R(J_{W,I},d)],

(256)

\displaystyle\gamma_{I}(d,d^{\prime})\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{(a\colon i\to j)\in I}d_{i}d^{\prime}_{j}.

Let us assume now that we have a stability function
Z\colon\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
, meaning that
Z(e_{i})\in\mathbb{H}
for
i\in Q_{0}
.
It defines the notion of stability on the abelian category
\operatorname{Rep}Q
§
3.4
.
Let
R_{Z}(Q,d)\subset R(Q,d)
denote the open subset of
Z
-semistable representations.
For any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{H}
, we define

(257)

A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(d)\in\ell}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{Q}(d,d)}\frac{[R_{Z}(Q,d),\operatorname{tr}W]}{[\operatorname{GL}_{d}]}x^{d}\in\hat{}\mathbb{T}

and
A_{Z,-\ell}^{Q,W}\mathchar 61\relax A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}
.
We define
stability data of the quiver with potential

(Q,W)
to be the collection
A_{Z}^{Q,W}\mathchar 61\relax(A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W})_{\ell}
.
It is parametrized by
Z\in\mathbb{H}^{Q_{0}}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}Q_{0},\mathbb{C})
, although one can extend the set of parameters to

\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\cdot\mathbb{H}^{Q_{0}}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}Q_{0},\mathbb{C})
.

The following result is well-known.
It is important to stress that while we compute invariants
[R(Q,d),\operatorname{tr}W]
which morally correspond to counting representations of the Jacobian algebra (and these representations form the heart of a bounded t-structure on the 3CY derived category of DG modules over the Ginzburg DG algebra of
(Q,W)

[
39
]
), in reality all computations are performed on the level of
Q
-representations which form an abelian category of homological dimension one.
Therefore the proof of the below wall-crossing formula is the same as in
[
60
,
33
]
and we don’t need to invoke the framework of
[
41
]
.

Theorem 8.5
.

For any stability function
Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
, we have

(258)

A_{0}^{Q,W}\mathchar 61\relax\prod_{\ell\subset\mathbb{H}}^{\to}A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.07672pt}{3.35277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\qquad W\mathchar 61\relax\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{3}}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot a_{\sigma 1}b_{\sigma 2}c_{\sigma 3}.

Let us consider the cut
I\mathchar 61\relax\left\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\right\}\subset Q_{1}
.
Then the quiver
Q^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax(Q_{0},Q_{1}\backslash I)
has the form

(260)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{i}}

which coincides with the quiver (
227
)
and the relations of the algebra
J_{W,I}
coincide with (
228
).
We conclude that

(261)

A\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}\mathchar 61\relax J_{W,I},\qquad T^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(2),

and
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
, where
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Similarly, let
\pi\colon Y\mathchar 61\relax\omega_{X}\to X
be the projection and
T\mathchar 61\relax\pi^{*}T^{\prime}
.
Then

(262)

B\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{End}(T)^{\mathrm{op}}\mathchar 61\relax J_{W}

and
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
.

Remark 8.6
.

The canonical bundle
Y\mathchar 61\relax\omega_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
can be interpreted as a crepant resolution of
\mathbb{C}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}
.
This implies that
[
19
]

(263)

D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)\simeq D^{b}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(\operatorname{Coh}_{c}\mathbb{C}^{3})\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W}).

Note that the left hand side of (
247
) is the Euler form of
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}Y)
and the right hand side of (
247
) is the Euler form of
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
.

We conclude from Lemma
8.4
that the Euler form
\chi_{X}
of the derived category

D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})

is given by

(264)

\displaystyle\chi_{X}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax\bar{}\chi_{X}(d,d^{\prime}),\qquad d\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}E,\,d^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}F,

(265)

\displaystyle\bar{}\chi_{X}(d,d^{\prime})\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{Q}(d,d^{\prime})+\gamma_{I}(d,d^{\prime})+\gamma_{I}(d^{\prime},d),

where
\gamma_{I}(d,d^{\prime})
was defined in (
256
).

Remark 8.7
.

This seems to be a rather general phenomenon.
In
[
8
]
one considered a large family of examples of surfaces
X
with a nef anticanonical bundle (called weak Fano surfaces there) and quivers with potential
(Q,W)
such that
D^{b}_{c}(\operatorname{Coh}\omega_{X})\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W})
.
We verified in all of these examples that there exists a special cut
I\subset Q_{1}
such that
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
and such that

(266)

\chi_{X}(E,F)\mathchar 61\relax\chi_{Q}(d,d^{\prime})+\gamma_{I}(d,d^{\prime})+\gamma_{I}(d^{\prime},d),\qquad d\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}E,\,d^{\prime}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}F.

It would be interesting to give a conceptual explanation of this phenomenon.

Let
Z\colon\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
be a stability function on the abelian category
\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
and let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})
be the corresponding stability condition on
\mathcal{D}\mathchar 61\relax D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(J_{W,I})
.
For any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{H}
, we define stability data (
133
)

(267)

A_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z(d)\in\ell}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{}\chi_{X}(d,d)}\frac{[R_{Z}(J_{W,I},d)]}{[\operatorname{GL}_{d}]}x^{d}

and
A_{\sigma,-\ell}\mathchar 61\relax A_{\sigma,\ell}
.
Note that the algebra
J_{W,I}
has homological dimension
2
, hence we don’t have a wall-crossing formula for the above stability data.
Nevertheless, we can formulate such a result if a stability condition on
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
has global dimension
\leq 2
, similarly to Corollary
5.7
.
We will see later that there exist geometric stability conditions
\sigma
on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
with the heart
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
.
These stability conditions have global dimension
2

by Theorem
7.7
.

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
Z\colon\Gamma\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
be a stability function such that the corresponding stability condition
\sigma
on
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
has global dimension
\leq 2
.
Then, for any ray
\ell\subset\mathbb{H}
, we have

A_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}.

Proof.

Let
\ell_{0}\subset V\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{cone}\left\{Z(e_{i})\mathchar 58\relax i\in Q_{0}\right\}\subset\mathbb{H}

be a ray and let
Z_{0}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
be such that
Z_{0}(e_{i})\in\ell_{0}
for all
i\in Q_{0}
.
Let
\sigma_{0}
be the corresponding stability condition on

D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})

(we will call it trivial as all objects in
\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
are automatically semistable).
Then we have (
267
)

A_{\sigma_{0},\ell_{0}}\mathchar 61\relax 1+\sum_{Z_{0}(d)\in\ell_{0}}\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{}\chi_{X}(d,d)}\frac{[R(J_{W,I},d)]}{[\operatorname{GL}_{d}]}x^{d}.

This series coincides with
A_{0}^{Q,W}
(
254
) as

\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{}\chi_{X}(d,d)}[R(J_{W,I},d)]\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{Q}(d,d)+\gamma_{I}(d,d))}[R(J_{W,I},d)]\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{L}^{{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{Q}(d,d)}\cdot[R(Q,d),\operatorname{tr}W].

By the assumption that
\sigma
has global dimension
\leq 2
, we obtain (see Corollary
5.7
)

A_{\sigma_{0},\ell_{0}}\mathchar 61\relax A_{\sigma,V}\mathchar 61\relax\prod^{\to}_{\ell\subset\mathbb{H}}A_{\sigma,\ell}.

By Theorem
8.5
, we have
A_{0}^{Q,W}\mathchar 61\relax\prod^{\to}_{\ell\subset\mathbb{H}}A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}
.
We conclude that
A_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}
.
∎

8.6.
Gluing families of stability data

Let
X\mathchar 61\relax\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
H
be the ample divisor such that
H^{2}\mathchar 61\relax 1
.
We will identify
N^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by sending
a\mapsto(a_{0},a_{1}H,a_{2})
.
Let (cf. §
6.8
)

\beta+\mathbf{i}\omega\mathchar 61\relax(s+\mathbf{i}t)H,\qquad s\in\mathbb{R},\,t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

We consider the abelian category
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{\omega,\#\beta\omega}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{Coh}_{H,\#s}

and the stability function
(see (
197
))

(268)

Z_{\beta,\omega}(E)\mathchar 61\relax Z^{\prime}_{s,t}(a)\mathchar 61\relax\left(ya_{0}+sa_{1}-a_{2}\right)+\mathbf{i}t\left(a_{1}-sa_{0}\right),\qquad a\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{ch}(E),

where
y\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}-s^{2})>-{\frac{1}{2}}s^{2}
.
Let
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
be the corresponding
stability condition on
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)\simeq D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
.
The following result is similar to
[
12
, §4]
, although one works with a different exceptional collection there.

Lemma 8.9
.

Assume that

(269)

s>-\tfrac{1}{2},\qquad t>0,\qquad s+t<0.

Then the heart of
\sigma_{\beta,\omega}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
is equal to
\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
and let
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}[{\frac{1}{2}}]
denote the heart of
\sigma[{\frac{1}{2}}]
.
Recall from §
8.3.2
that the category

\mathcal{A}_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
has simple objects

\mathcal{O},\,\Omega_{X}(1)[1],\,\mathcal{O}(-1)[2].

We will show that the objects

(270)

\mathcal{O},\,\Omega_{X}(1)[1],\,\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]

are contained in
\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
and

(271)

\operatorname{Re}Z(\mathcal{O})<0,\qquad\operatorname{Re}Z(\Omega_{X}(1)[1])<0,\qquad\operatorname{Re}Z(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])>0.

Then
\operatorname{Re}Z
is negative on all simple objects of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
,
hence all these simple objects are contained in
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}[{\frac{1}{2}}]
.
This implies that
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}[{\frac{1}{2}}]
.
As both categories are hearts of bounded t-structures, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}_{0}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}[{\frac{1}{2}}]
as required.

Z(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])
Z(\mathcal{O}(-1)[2])
Z(\Omega_{X}(1)[1])
Z(\mathcal{O})

Let us show first that
\Omega_{X}(1)
is
\mu_{H}
-semistable.
As
\Omega_{X}(1)\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{Q}^{\vee}
, where
\mathcal{Q}
was defined in (
234
),
it is enough to show that
\mathcal{Q}
is
\mu_{H}
-semistable.
We have
\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{Q}\mathchar 61\relax(2,1,-1/2)
, hence
\mu_{H}(\mathcal{Q})\mathchar 61\relax 1/2
.
There are no non-trivial morphisms
\mathcal{O}(k)\to\mathcal{Q}
for
k\geq 1
because of the exact sequence (
234
).
Therefore
\mathcal{Q}
is indeed
\mu_{H}
-semistable.

We have

\mu_{H}(\mathcal{O})\mathchar 61\relax 0,\qquad\mu_{H}(\Omega_{X}(1))\mathchar 61\relax-1/2,\qquad\mu_{H}(\mathcal{O}(-1))\mathchar 61\relax-1.

As
-{\frac{1}{2}}<s<0
, we conclude that
\mathcal{O}\in\operatorname{Coh}_{H,>s}
and
\Omega_{X}(1),\mathcal{O}(-1)\in\operatorname{Coh}_{H,\leq s}
.
Therefore all objects in (
270
) are contained in
\operatorname{Coh}_{H,\#s}\mathchar 61\relax\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Let us check that (
271
) is satisfied.
Recall that (
242
)

\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{O}\mathchar 61\relax(1,0,0),\qquad\operatorname{ch}\Omega_{X}(1)[1]\mathchar 61\relax(-2,1,\tfrac{1}{2}),\qquad\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{O}(-1)\mathchar 61\relax(1,-1,\tfrac{1}{2}).

Therefore we require (with
y\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}-s^{2})
)

y<0,\qquad-2y+s-\tfrac{1}{2}<0,\qquad y-s-\tfrac{1}{2}<0.

Under the assumptions
-{\frac{1}{2}}<s<0
and
-{\frac{1}{2}}s^{2}<y<0
, the other conditions follow.
As
y\mathchar 61\relax{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}-s^{2})
, these assumptions are equivalent to
(
269
).
∎

Choosing parameters
(s,t)
as in the previous lemma,
we obtain a special geometric stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax\sigma_{\beta,\omega}[{\frac{1}{2}}]
and central charge
Z\mathchar 61\relax Z_{\beta,\omega}[{\frac{1}{2}}]\mathchar 61\relax\mathbf{i}^{-1}Z_{\beta,\omega}

such that the heart of
\sigma
is equal to
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I}
.
The corresponding linear map
Z\colon\mathcal{N}(X)\simeq\mathbb{Z}Q_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}

satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
8.8
, hence we obtain
A_{\sigma,\ell}\mathchar 61\relax A_{Z,\ell}^{Q,W}
.
On the other hand, the series
A_{\sigma,\ell}
defined in (
267
) (for the category
D^{b}(\operatorname{mod}J_{W,I})
) and

A_{\sigma,\ell}
defined in (
133
) (for the category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
) are the same.

Note that stability data
A_{\sigma}\mathchar 61\relax(A_{\sigma,\ell})_{\ell}
is defined for every special geometric stability condition
\sigma\mathchar 61\relax(Z,\mathcal{P})\in\operatorname{Stab}^{+}_{\operatorname{cl}}(\mathcal{D})

(
171
).
The discriminant
\Delta
is negative-definite on
\operatorname{Ker}Z
, hence by Theorem
4.11
stability data
A_{\sigma}
depends just on
Z\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
and we will denote it by
A_{Z}
.
On the other hand, stability data
A_{Z}^{Q,W}
is defined for every
Z\in\mathcal{P}(Q)\mathchar 58\relax\mathchar 61\relax\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\cdot\mathbb{H}^{Q_{0}}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}Q_{0},\mathbb{C})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
.
We conclude from Theorem
8.8
and Lemma
8.9

that we can glue together stability data
for the category
D^{b}(\operatorname{Coh}X)
and stability data for the quiver with potential
(Q,W)
and define a family of stability data parametrized by
\mathcal{P}^{+}(X)\cup\mathcal{P}(Q)
.
